TH KI YS

Where They Are and What. They Do

Everyone ought to know how to tell whether the kidneys are acting properly, for their work is of great importance—" you cautiot be well if your kidneys are ill."

What The Kidneys Are and What They DO.----The kidneys are two bean-shaped organs lying beneath the small of the back (see ilrustration). They are composed of thousands or little filtering tubes through which the blood flows hundred of times a day to be filtered.

Enough poisonous waste gets into the blood every leic clays to cause death. Your kidneys alone can sare you. Erery .?4• hours they take out about 500 grains of poisonous waste and pints of water from the blood.

What Happens when the Kidneys are Weak.—The kidneys are s'.) delicate that the least cold, chill, ,train, or excess may affect them. Then they are likely to leave some of the poisonous kidney waste in the blood. This poisonous waste gets carried to all parts of the body ; makes you feel cull, heavy and irritable makes your back ache, your limbs ache and swell you may become rheumatic or dropsical, or suffer with the sharp pains of lumbago.

Kidney poison shows itself in the water, which may be cloudy or sandy. There may be pain in seeking relief, or you may be disturbed several times HI the night. The urinary channels may become clogged up with gravel, or stones may form in the kidneys and bladder.

Weiner), and even children, as well as men, are subject to kidney and bladder disorders.

Treatment—Patient should drink plenty of water to keep the urine dilute, and should take nothing that may excite the kidneys. This remedy is compounded from ingredients of the highest quality and absolute purity. It acts on the kidneys and bladder only, an,?:l is not intended for constipation or disorders of the digestive system.

COMING OF AGE.

JUVENILE RECHABITES' CELEBRATIONS.

Interesting Presentations.

One of the most interesting events in the annals of the John Dimond tent 911, of the Juvenile Order of Rechabites, Warrington District, took place on Monday evening when the 21st anniversary of the tent was celebrated by a large number of members in the Friars Green schoolroom. The proceedings took the form of a tea and entertainment, at which, prizes were pre- sented to a number of children by Mrs Dodds of Penketh. Presentations were also made to Bros. Wright and Massey of barometers inscribed to the effect that they were given in appreciation of the 21 years service they had rendered as officers of the tent. For having passed through the various offices of the order Bro. Arthur Hamblett received a Chief Ruler's sash. The tea, which was well served, was partaken of by about IEO members and amongst others who assisted at the tables were the wives of the superintendents. At the entertainment Bro. W Wright presided and he was sunported by Mrs Dodds and members of the Order. In his opening remarks the CHAIRMAN said he was acting in the absence of Bro Lowe who was their superintendent. As one of the workers, he (the speaker), thought that for discipline, instruction and general conduct of the members the Fist year had been one of the best they had experieccel for a long time. Twenty- one iyears last November the John Dimond Juvenile Tent was instituted in connection with Charity Tent. At that time they found that. the early work of the Rechabites had practically died out in Warrington. It was therefore decided by the Charity Tent members to form and institute a juvenile tent. That institution took place about the 10th November, in the Brick-street Sunday school and about 20 members joined. The John Dimond Juvenile Tent had been interesting and progressive and they continued to grow until that tent held the premier position in the 3nvenile District, and to-day they had a member- ship of :100. Applause.) They had had more than that, but a number had been transferred to the Adult Tent, and that had somewhat lessened their ranks. From a humble beginning, they continued to advance and at the present time they had 12 or 13 juvenile tents in Warrington with over 1,000 members. (Applause.) Looking bark on such a record one saw that good work had been accomplished and it was a source of pride that that there were at least three present. that night who bad seen the whole 21 years with the tent. Two of them were superintendents at the commencement and were still in that position to-day. (Applause.) He was sure there was nothing which gave a man or woman greater pleasure than to look back on 21 years' service rendered in the noble cause of Temperance. (Hear,bear) The influence of that Rechabite movement had spread until, he helieved,it was the main temperance influence in the town to-day, (Hear, hear.) He hoped it would continue and increase in power. They were not as yet quite on the mountain top ; the drinking customs of our land were not entirely broken down. There was a great deal of sin and poverty and they would have to do much before they could siy that the temperance victory was won. They were out to send their temper- ance principles into hundreds of homes in Warrington in order that the people would be lifted up to a higher plane which would all he for the good of the whole town. 'Applause.) They had with them that night their friend and sister Mrs Dodds. They knew that she was not merely an ornament to their order but she was a helpful and willing worker in numerous respects Mrs Dodds, by her personal influence and character was a great pillar in their temperance movement. Applause).

LONDON CITY AND MIDLAND BANK.

" DEAR " GOLD AND THE PRICE OF COMMODITIES.

The annual general meeting of the London City and Midland Bank Ltd., was held last kriday at the Cannon- street Hotel, London, E.C. Sir Edward Holden, Bart , chairman, presided, and in moving the adoption of the report he reviewed the salient conditious which bad prevai!ed and were now current in the leading money markets of the world. As regarded our own country, there was evidence on all sides that

===== 0002601_19130201_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
TRADE DAD BEEN _GOOD,

Having given an exhaustive review of the monetary conditions prevailing in the leading money markets of the world the Chairman pointed out that dear money increased the prices of commodities to the consumers, and drove down the prices of securities. The liabili- ties of the countries he had reviewed had increased in a greater ratio than their gold. In this country the rate nad been higher for the purpose of protecting our gold, and although we had had resources to a rate of 5 per cent., yet the proportion of our resource of gold to liabilities this year was about 3 per cent, lower than it was at the same time last year. Why had we and the above mentioned countries not been able to obtain and retain more gold? The answer was, he said, because it had been taken by India and if those other countries continued in future to increase their trade without being able to increase their gold, then a crisis similar to or even greater than, that in America in 1907 would assuredly take place. Which would cause loans to be called in, loans to be contracted, and great losses to ensue. Being face to face with these conditions, the Indi an question was one which ought to be thoroughly discussed, for during the last two and threequarter years, India had taken no less than 67 millions sterling of gold in bars and sovereigns and little of it had returned. He hoped the Secretary for State for India might be iuduced seriously to consider the question of appoint- ing without delay a fresh commission. not .only in the interests of this country, but also in the interests of all those countries which were now unable to obtain gold to nits:it their increase in trade,increasing liabilities and ia?reasing credit. There were serious dangers ahead and it had become a pressing necessity to provide protection against them. Finally the Chairman made a running commentary on the leading figures of the balance sheet. Their deposits at the end of 1911 were about £4,300,000 higher than at the end of 1910, and at the end of 1912 they were nearly six millions higher than at the end of 1911. Iu two years therefore, they had increased about 10 millions and now stood at £83,664,000. The progress of a Bank depended to a large extent on the increase of its deposits- Their profits for the year 1912, after making additions to their internal reserves amounted to £1,010,000. (Applause.) The interim dividend which they paid and the dividend they now proposed gave them 18 per cent. on the shares. They further paid the income tax amounting to £41,887, which was slightly over 1 per cent of their capital. They there- fore paid in respeci of dividend and income tax an amount equal to 19 per cent on their capital. They provided £160,000 for their investments, £50,000 for Bank Premises Redemption Fund, £20,000 for 011icers' Pension Fund, £10,01,0 for Stall Widows' Fund, carry- ing forward £133,000. He thought these figures spoke for themselves and he ventured to think their results for 1912 would meet with approval. (Applause.) 11e moved the adoption of the report. The resolution was seconded by the Deputy-Chair - man (Mr W G Bradshaw), and passed. The next resolution, declaring a dividend of £1 2s 6,1 per share, making with the interim dividend paiu for the half-year ended ;;Oth Juno last, 18 per cent. for the year free of income tax, was also passed. The retiring directors, Mr William Graham Bradshaw and Mr William Fitz Thomas Wyley, were re-elected. as elso were the auditors. Messrs Whinney Smith and Whinney. The usual complimentary resolutions to the Chair- man, Board of Directors, managers and stall were unanimously agreed.

THE MELBOURNE SAILS.

The Melbourne, the first protected cruiser built at the expense of. the Commonwealth Government of Australia, left Plymouth Sound on Thursday on her voyage to the Antipodes. She takes out with her a large number of men of the Imperial Navy, who have transferred to the Colonial service.

OLD BOYS.

ANNUAL DINNER AT THE GRAMMAR

SCHOOL.

ALDERMAN BENNETT AND THE COUNCIL

"An Ugly Mirror."

Happy memories of school days were conjured up on Saturday evening by "old boss" of the Boteler Grammar School who gathered to show their affection for the honoured school in which their boyhood was spent and to cement the frierdshipi formed in those early years. The re-union was the eleventh of its kind and the excepticnally large attendance was a proof of the increasing popularity of the function. It was also a complete justification of the departura made by the committee in arranging the dinner for a Saturday evening, for the ( hoice f date SE emed to meet the general convenience much mole crmpletely than on any previous occasion. It is also an exceedingly happy idea to meat within the walls of the old s(hrol its-elf, for the sentimental associations which play so large a part in the pro- motion of such a gathering are thereby consider-0)1y emphasised. The President for this year is Mr E A White and among other prominent guests were : Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., Bev E C E Carleton, Rev. Horace Gray (headmaster), Alderman A Bennett, Mr Roger C Parr, Mr J Moore Nlurray (director of educa- tier)); Mes-rs. Cl:as S Burgess, Vele B Davies and C D Parkinson (past presidents), J H Smethurst (chairman of committee), Leonaid Pierp.. jut (hon. treasurer), and Stanley Walton (hon. secretary). Dinner was served by the Winrnarleigh Cafe Co., the catering giving every satisfaction. The toast cl "The King " as honoured on the proposition of the PRESIDENT. THE WILLCOCKS I\IEIIIOMM, FUND.

Mr 3 II S'ivlETHl.7iisT submitted the toast of the frunder—" The pious memory of Sir Thomas Boteltr," and took the opportunity of referring to the WilicEcks Memorial Fond. He puinted out that they bad the sum of .4.,“"25 invested in the Warrinpton Corporation at 3?, per cent., and they had also £l6 10s in Parr's lien, making a total of £241 los ltd. For the first time in the history of the fund, they had bad an application this year to make a grant to abey who was leaving the sChool for one of the Universities. On the recom- mendation of the Headmaster they decided to grant a boy named Woodcock the sum Of £25, pa, able in three instalments. He believed the Rev E J Wincocks would have desired no rnors fitting perpetuation of his memory than that way of helping deserving boys--and he (Mr. Smethurst) believed Woodcock was a most dee erving boy who would do credit to the old school. (Applause ) Re'terring te the provision of an annual prize by the "old boys," Mr. Smethurst said the committee felt that, the prize should be guaranteed for at leaq, two or thief) yews, and accordiugly those " Old Buys" who could not attend the dinner were-invited to contribute 2s each The sum of £3 15s had been raised •in that nay. The committee had also been considering the possibility of altering the constitution of their organisation. At the present time they had an Old Boys' Union without a membership end the suegestion was that they should have a definite Union with a definite subscription of a small amount and a definite membership. The com- mittee agreed that the sooner they could come to an arrangement of that kind, the better. The Headmaster would, no doubt, use his influence with boys leaving school to induce them to join the Old Boys' Union and they would then have no difficulty in blinding up a real Union which would he of value to all who chose to enrol themselves. (Applause). The toast WbS honoured in solemn silence. "PROSPERITY TO THE SCHOOL."

THE EDUCATIONAL VALUE OF NOT WORKING. The PRESIDENT, in proposing " Prosperity to the school " gave a witty speech full of interesting reminiscences of his own school days which went back to 1876. On the general question of education, he said they used to hear a great deal of argument as to the value of various subjects taught in the schools. There had been repeated discussions as to whether the educa- tional value of the classics or of the higher mathematics was sufficient to justify their continuance. There was, however, one phase of the abject which he never heard discussed, and that was : What is the educational value of not working at all ? (Laughter.) It might sound a hit strange, hut if they followed out the idea care- fully, they would find that in schools there was a con- siderable educational value in not working. When a boy, he was told that education did not mean what they put into a boy, but what they brought out of him, and that the classics and various branches of the higher mathematics were useful in the same way the gymnastic exercises were useful—the one. to educate and train the brain and the other to develop the muscles. Mr White then in a humourous way alluded to the " excellent mental training " which the unwilling boy received in evolving ingenious schemes for neglecting hiw home lessons and creating the impression that be was as well-informed as the more diligent pupil. In school life generally the school master and the boy were natural reemies—(laughter)— and in the waging of that very properly enmity, the boy got a good training for the battle of life. He became accustomed to facing failure and taking "lickings"—a training which he would find exceedingly useful when he undertook the serious business of life. He would also learn how to extricate him- self from tight corners. (Laughter.) On the whole, although the scholastic attainments of the boy who would not work might not be very high, his experiences in some cases accounted for h " by does a brilliant school toy often turn le: n oriudtdafailure in after-life, while the boy who is not brilliant at school is found to achieve success?" In school life, unfortunately, there was a strong tendency to remember the things one ought to forget, and to forget the things one ought to remember. %%hen the late Lord Winrnarleigh hart attained the age of 70, be (the speaker), had the privilege of conversing with him on several Occasions and his lordehip favoured him with a few anecdotes of his early days. The only story he related of his school life, however, was bow he played truant in order to watch the Princess Charlotte's funeral procession and how be got flogged for doing it. He (Mr White) gathered that in after-life his lordship thought it was well worth it. (Laughter.) "We used to wonder in our school days" continued Mr White "why those wretched people wrote those classics. (Laughter.) We had a special grudge against Cwsar. We thought he was such an awfully conceited chap,always telling us what he did. (Laughter.) It was quite enough to have done those things without writing about them. (Laughter.) We were also told that a certain number of the books of Euclid had been lost, and we thought it a very unreasonable thing that a similar fate bad not befallen the whole of them. (Laughter.) As T understand they have abolished Euclid from most of the schools, we were only indulging in intelligent anticipation. (Laugh ter.)The most humorous thing that over happened in school in my day was during a hot summer afternoon, when the whole school was in this room, busily engaged in an examination. The door opened straight on to the playground and in the middle of the afternoon the headmaster's pony gravely walked in, looked in en intelligent manner round the schooleossed up his head in disgust end walked out by the other door. I never heard such a hearty burst of laughter in school during the whole of the time f was there, and I think the boys agreed with the pony that we were very foolish in spending a lovely summer afternoon in that way, although we thought he did not understand that we were only doing it because we were compelled. " (Laughter.) Having referred to the glamour which surrounded school life in after years, Mr White said it was natural for " old boys " to cherish the illusion that things were better in their day than they are now. Viewing the matter seriously,however, they must admit that progress had been made and they all sincerely hoped that the school—removed at no very distant date to a better situation—would continue to prosper. (Applause.) The REV HORACE GRAY acknowledged the toast. He said that as part of a general knowledge paper recently set at the school he asked the question : " What would you say in response to the toast, 'Prosperity to the school '" ? He thought such a question might excite the minds of young Botelerians but, to his astonishment, he got only two answers out of the whole school. Air Gray read one of the answers which harked back to the days when the school had only three masters—including a writing master who received .£lO a year—and 30 boys. To-day there were eight masters—all University men—and 130 boys. (Applause). Having commended the effort to encourage the spirit of comradeship between old Botelerians, Mr Gray touched upon the many differ- ing theories advanced on the question of education, and said that only the other day he read an article in which it was laid down as a fact that no great chess player ever made a successful business man There were two great ideals of education—and they were as far apart as the poles. One was repression and the other was guidance. They had probably rill had experience of the master who taught with a long switch on the top of his desk and when he asked a question' the boys had to respond as quickly as it took to say Jack Robinson, or the switch descended. (Laughter.) With the rank and file repression had been largely ttied, but the teacher who adopted the methods of guidance was the one who in time to come would succeed. It was not generally realized that no man could make another learn anything, if he did not wish to learn it. The lot of the idler might be made so difficult and disagreeable that he would appear to learn, hut the knowledge he acquired was very superficial and when the repressive power NVIS removed, that which he had stored by also disappeared. There were many pupils in some schools who were in such a mood that they would learn nothing, except under compulsion. He did not think there were many boys of that type in the Boteler Grammar School. It was not easy for boys to escape, but where that type of pupil did exist, it was largely due to bad training. In the majority of cases, that bad

training was to be attributed to the parents, and it was sometimes followed up by the school master. The school had to put up with those scholars, but it could never make a success of them unless some happy tnuch came to the pupil's will and he wanted to learn. What they wanted to do was to use the boys' natural instincts. In this connection Air Gray commended the direct method of teaching languages and history—the text book being used as a suggestion, and the boy becoming an active unit in the lessons. Finally be quoted the following line., written by a BGtelerian., which be thought fittingly represented the feelings of "old boys" tows:cis the school --

Sail on, 0 splendid in your vast resolve ! The goal draws near—lint say, cen ye forget With ne'er a sigh the lov'd things left?—Those eycs, Betray they not some stirring of regt et ? (Applause.) A c,dlection was then made in aid of the "old boys'" prize fund nod realized £-1 7s. making, with the amount sent by those who were unable to attoud the dinner, a total of 1:8 2.

"-PROSPERITY TO THE COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON."

Mr A.E BowEN proposed "Prosperity to the County Borough of Warrington." His speech consisted chiefly of a recapitulation of the salient features of the history of the town, special reference being made to the work of the Roteler family and to the erection of successive Warringtcn bridges. Mr Bowen also alluded to the tar-off days when Warrington not only supplied neight oaring towns with fruit and vegetables, tut exported potatoes to the Meditcrreuesn. Wairington ranked as one of the earliest manufacturing towns in Lancashire, the produc- tion of linen and sail cloth being two of its earliest industries. Air. Bowen remarked on the s,all wages received by the workers in three early times. Women were paid 2d a day for spinning, and men got an average of lba a week. Women received 23 6d a week for wind- ing and men 7s a week for warping. Men were paid 9s a week for weaving, and women got 55 and boys 3s 6d. At shoc-inaking an average wage of 9s per week could I e earned. With retard to the cost of living, cheese was bought at :;b.d. a pound, beef at 3d., bacon at milk at 21. a quart, while ',louse rent ranged lion, 205., to 303. a year. Referring to the Warrington of to-day the speaker expressed satisfaction at the efforts of the Education Committee to promote the health of the school children and especially commended the appoint- ment of a school dental officer. Trade at the present time was booming and the reason why such prosperity was to be valued was that it brought happiness to the people. (Applause.) ALDERMAN BEI\TNETT'S CHARACTERISTIC SpEEe El. THE COUNCIL AS AN "UGLY MIRROR."

Alderman BENNETT in responding, said he was very much struck with two phases in the history of the lioteler family—the devotion with which they dis- charged !heir duty to the State arid to their native town. There was hardly a battle during the three or four hundred years in which the Botelers were at their prime, in which they did not take part. Warrirgton owed a great debt to the family for their local activities. They were identified with the Church, the Friary and the earlier Warrington bd They established our weekly markets which went back ack four or five hundred years and which were still held on the original dsy (Wednesday). They also founcled.our fairs which had practically fallen into disuse, after a course of some centuries. In the year 1300 they won for Nlarringter. its Magua Charta which secured to the inhaLitents privileges which, in their „y, were elmcst as important as those secured by the Magna Cherta wrung by the Barons front King John. Last, but by no means least, they founded tuat ancient institution. the Grammar School. Impressing upon his hearers the cksirability of following out the old Boteler traditions, the Alderman said there was a magni- ficent, field for public activity in the locality in which they lived. He sometimes thought many of their modern citizens—even old Butelerians—were somewhat blind to that phase of public duty. National duty perhaps appealed to them more prominently and, of course, there was more limelight about it. But it was not one whit more important than local duty. If they really desired to promote the prosperity of the town, and it all their perorations were not to he mere empty words, they ought to try to identify themselves with local lite and to do something for it. He said it with great regret, but be was sure there was a great lack of public spirit in Warrington—except at football matches and that kind of thing. (Laughter.) They were ready enough to show their pride in Warrington when paying a certain number of men to fight their battles in the field of sport, and they thought Warring- ton was winning great glory by achievements of that character. Put he did not think there was much glory in it ; the whole business was too mercenary. flow many were really prepared to wake sacrifices for the town ? Take the subject of public generosity—it was absolutely lamentable. They had had instances to the contrary from friends who had been with them at that festive board but, speaking generally, how little the RICH MEN OF WARRINGTON did for us ! Those great benefactions that we read of in similar towns—how few and far between they were in \Narrington. Len again, the Lumber of townspeople who took part in municipal service was pitifully limited The disgraceful scenes occurring in the Town Council were common talk on every trait:near and in every haunt of local life. Whose fault was it ? It was the fault of every old Boteleriaa and of every citizen of Warrington. "The Town Council " continued the alderman" is what you make it. If you don't like the picture, it is your own reflection. You see the image of yourselves in that ugly mirror. I ask you to improve the imago and then yon will see a better reflection. The people elect the Council and if the Council is not worthy of the town—and I sometimes feel it is not—it is the fault of the citizens themselves, and particularly the fault of those who have the ability, knowledge' energy and enterprise and who are too selfish to devote their time to the public service." Continuing he implored all who felt any vocation for public service to enter it. He could not help harking back to the old days very longingly and sometimes with a feeling of great regret, because while there were many things in Warrington to-clay of which they were legitimately proud there were other featnres of which they hid no occasion to be proud. We had "black rain from black skies," the roofs of the houses seemed to run with black sweat which, in turn, ran into a black river. In some quarters there were black and dirty streets, with grimy people. While we boasted about our wealth and menutacturing prosperity, we ought to be ashamed that such things sere possible—that side by side with wealth, there were the slums which existed in Warrington, that we had whole wards in which there was not a single open space, that we had lost the sense of civic beauty and artistic taste, that we had no statues no monuments and few fine public buildings. Under such circumstances wEAf.TH WAS A MOCKERY, a disgrace and a discredit to us. The more wealthy and prosperous we were, the more degraded we were, unless we used that wealth to the best advantarge. Until we had wiped slums away and made the town a decent, livable place, in which the citizens might find oppor- tunities for a healthy, natural life, we had no reason to plume ourselves. We had yet to learn a new method of valuing things. The mere piling up of tremendous fortunes, the mere spread of a town, the growth of its population and the multiplication of industries, were not sufficient, unless those things were accompanied by the higher things for which the school and educational institutions generally stood. Many of our modern towns, in spite of all the false pride which they had in their prosperity and progressosould disgrace an uncivilized community; they were absolute blots on the face of England. It was not necessary that manufactures should carry with them the slum, squalor, and filth that we saw around us in Warrington. Those present could do something to remedy the conditions by sending the right people to the Council, by personal service on those authorities, and by personal sacrifice. He would infinitely rather see evidences of that real activity and public spirit than hear any amount of beautiful words about the town and its past. (Applause.) They must strive to make the future of the town worthy of its splendid past. (Applause.)

COUNCIL OR CHURCH SCHOOLS?

NONCONFORMIST OBJECTION TO PROPOSED TRANSFER. The Question of Religious Teaching.

It is very seldom indeed that the religious difficulty is raised in the Warrington Echication Committee, but it cropped up on Monday evening on the proposal of the School Management Committee to close Cairo- street (Infants) Council School and to transfer the teachers and scholars to the Holy Trinity (Church) Schools. The chief points of debate were as to whether the teachers to be transferred would be expected to give Church teaching and whether the wishes of the parents had bi en consulted. Councillor H Roberts presided and there were also present :—Mr A J jolley (deputy chairman), Aldermen Bennett. James Evans and Smethurst, Councillors Archer, Birtles, Dickenson, Jenkins Lindley, Osborne, Pemberton and Wilkinson, Revs U 'C Briggs and N Wilson, Messrs R T Fairclough, J Jarman, H Kingdon, t.; E Parker, F Roberts, J Roberts, J M Murray (director) and F It Roberts (assistant director). At a meeting of the School Management Committee communications were read from the managers of the Trinity School making a proposal, in view Of the con- siderable reduction in the number of scholars now attending the school, as to the possibility of opening a portion as an infants' division. The committee recommended that, subject to the concurrence of the manaeers and the appioval of the Board of Education (a) the Trinity School be continued as a division for senior mixed scholars as at present, with the existing staff ; (b) the Cairo-street temperary (infants') Council School be closed ; and (c) an infants' division be formed at the Trinie y School, the existiug staff of the Cairo-street temporary (infants') Council School being offered transference thereto on theft existing terms of service. Mr Uesors, in submitting the minutes, said that to disarm any criticism of the fact that they were closing a Council school and transferring the staff, and pro- bably most of the children, to a non-provided school, he would point out that the Cairo-street school hart been condemned for four years in succession by the Medical Officer of Health, because of its insanitary condition. The Medical Officer had reported again and again that the children's health was endangered by their being taught in the school and the structural arrangements were such as to make it impossible for the sanitary conditions to he improved. That having been pointed out on four different occasions, the com- mittee were not prepared to take the risk of keeping 120 to 130 infants in a building so strongly condemned. Owing to the opening of the Evelyn-street Council Schools, the number of scholars attending the Trinity School had been very seriously reduced. Ot course it was impossible to build a new Couticil school which would cover only the immediate requirements at the moment for any given district. Provision was made at Evelyn- street for 1,260 and probably 500 or COO required accommodation in the locality, which adversely affected the attendance in some other neighbouring schools. The numbers in the Trinity School were reduced to SO on the books. Therefore, there was a large waste of space. If they were to find Cannon school accommodation for the infants et Cairo-street they must build. It was impossible to find land upon which to build in that district and the committee thought it was expedient tc try to make some arrange- ment with the Trinity managers, so that they could take over the Cairo-street staff and form a new infants' department there, particularly when it was remembered that the Trinity managers had surrendered an infants' department in Liverpool-road, as a consequence of the Evelyn-street schools beiug opened. He moved that the proposal be accepted. The Rev Father Winsoer seconded. Mr 1, It ROBERTS moved that the minute be referred back. The suggestion was more serious than appeared on the surface. It was a distinctly retrograde step to transfer children from a Council school to a voluntary non-provided school. Had the transfer been in the reverse direction he would have heartily supported it 'lt had to be borne in mind that the infants NS ho would be sent to the Trinity School had been originally sent to Cairo-street as a Council school. The parents doubt- less had exercised some choice in the matter, and had selected Cairo-street because it wai a Council school,free from religious tests, and it would have a disturbing influence on the educational system of the town that such a transfer should now be made. The feelings of the parents of the children and the feelings and opinions of the teachers whom it was proposed to transfer had to be considered. Had the committee taken into con- sideration the objections that parents and children might possibly haw) to transfer ? It was all very well to say that -Trinity School was the only place where accommodation could be found. He should like to be satisfied on that point. Surely there were buildings in the neighbourhood of Cairo-street which might be utilized and he wondered whether any inquiries on that point had been made. It was a much too serious busi- ness to be dismissed in a light, off-hand way. It was desirable that the fullest consideration should be given to the points he had raised and he therefore moved that the whole question be referred back to the committee.

Alderman BENN'ETT, in seconding the amendment,said be thought the point was deserving of further con- sideration. Whether the proposal was a retrograde step or not he was not prepared to say. To many, possibly, it might have that appearance but he did not think tbe committee ought to adopt the minute with- out the fullest information and every consideration. Mr JARMAN inquired whether in case of the transfer being carried out the infants' department would be absolutely under Church control? The CHAIRMAN : 1 take it the teachers and scholars would be in precisely the same relation to the managers as their present teachers and scholars.

Alderman SMETH URST : Exactly. - Mr JARMAN : That is of course, that they will be under the managers of the Church school and they will be compelled to teach Church subjects as required,such as the Catechism.

The CHAIRMAN : Unless special arrangements are made to the contrary, undoubtedly Mr R T FAIRCLOUGH disagreed with the attitude and views of Mr Roberts, who he considered was sub- mitting his amendment too much on religious grounds. A Church school was to be transferred to a Council school at Latchford, and the managers of tie Christ Church School were not contemplating raising any such points as that; they were approaching the position simply from the point of view of the benefit of the community and of the children. (Mr JOLLEY : Hear, hear.) It seemed to him that the retrograde step would be to act upon the views of Mr F Ft Roberts who ought to show a more open mind on the vitally impor- tant point of the children's health. Churchpeople might have something to say in regard to Christ Church School if that step were taken in regard to the school in question. Alderman BOLTON said there were vacancies in Evelyn-street school and those parents who desired could send their children there. If Holy Trinity School was the only place available for a transfer, it would be much more desirable to make use of it than to leave things in their present unsatisfactory state. It was practically a case of Hobson's choice, and he thought thanks were duo to the committee for trying to make satisfactory arrangements in difficult circum- stances. _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _ _

Councillor ARCHER said the children's health should be the primary consideration. Father WILSON asked wh3ther in the event of the transferred teachers objecting they would be required to give religiou3 instruction ? 'Vie DIRECTOR: The managers said that the matter would be arranged to suit the convenience of the teachers. _ _ _

Father Wir,sox : The wish of the teachers? The DIRECTOR : Yes. Councillor JENKINS : And the parents ? Mr liINGDON said if that were so—if there was an authoritative statement which could be embodied in the arrangements, it would dispose of the objection. He should support the proposal on health grounds. Mr C E PARKER asaed if the Director could tell them whether there was any objection on the part of the parents. If there was any objection on the part of the teachers or the parents it raised a very important issue. The committee recognised that it was pretty well a case of Hobson's choice. It was very undesir- able to keep the children in the condemned school if there was accommodation available near at hand. At the same time the points he had mentioned ought to be cleared up. The DIRECTOR replied that at present there were no objections from either, but lie did not know how fair it was to say that for the teachers, because they had not had any opportunity of objecting. With regard to the parents, the committee could not compulsorily transfer children from one school to another ; it rested with the parents to say which school the children should attend. Councillor PEMBERTON understood from Mr Murray that the teachers would not be called upon to give religious instruction. The CHAIRMAN: No. The value of Mr Murray': statement only amounts to this :--A manager has o some managers have assured him that that point would

be taken into consideration in regard to the service. Councillor PEMBERTON : Well, make it a condition The CHAIRMAN: Exactly, I agree. Councillor PEMBERTON : Then if that condition 15 made everything is safeguarded ? The CHAIRMAN said the matter was not quite !SO simple as it appeared on the face of the minute, and in order to safeguard the interests concerned he suggested that if the proposal was for the present with- drawn and the conditions were carefully considered a satisfactory agreermnt mi At be arrivea at. Cooncillor PEMBERTON : There is no immediate hurry, is there ? '1 he CHAIRMAN: No. Councilior DICKENSON argued that the parents had not the freedom of choice that had been repre- sented. A child might be forced into a school because there was not accemmodation in the locality in which it lived, or where the parent selected it should attend. Toe DIRECTOR : Children are not forced into any particular school. Tne choice of school rests with the parents, E o long as there is accommodation. Couneillor DieKENsoN : But whea there is no accommodation, you are going to force the child into a school. The DIRECTOR: Yes, into some other school, but we do not choose the scnool. The CHAIRMAN: The choice of the parent is limited by the accommodation. Mr F R Ronsirrs said he was informed that the reason children attended the Cairo-street school from outside areas was because it had always been regarded as a feeder to the Arpley-street school. Father WILSON said it, seemed to him that if the Cairo-street school were closed the parents would have the same liberty of selection as any other parents. In the present state of affairs many chddren had to travel considerable distances to school, but that would not be so regrettable as to keep them in an insanitary building He was glad several speakers had emphasised a principle very dear to him, that the parents should be consulted as to the school which the children should attend. He hoped that would be always borne in mind. It seemed very desirable that it should be ascertained with certaintywhat would be required of the teachers. He could see serious objection to forcing teachers to leave a Council school and to go to a non-provided school where they would be asked to give religious teaching to which they might have reasonable objection. From that point of view alone, he supported the amendment. Mr UtesoN said the Cairo-street School must be closed It then became the duty of the committee to suggest an alternative. The School Management Committee refused to have the responsibility of carrying on the school any longer in face of the serious reports as to its insanitary condition. However, after hearing the views of several speakers, he felt there was a certain amount of difficulty and objection in carrying out the transfer without first ascertaining whether the teachers were prepared to give religious instruction which they woula no doubt be asked to give. He had the fullest confidence in the managers of the Trinity School that they would not require as a condition that those teachers should give definite denominational instruction He did not know the teachers, except the headmistress, and whether they might or might not be prepared to give such teaching. It would be an easy matter for the managers to arrange for the instruction to be given by teachers holding their particular views. In view of the discussion,he believed he would be fulfilling the wishes of the School Management Committee by withdrawing the minute in order that provisions might be made for safeguarding the interests of all concerned. The CirmILMAN thanked Mr Upson for having with- drawn the minute as he (Councillor Roberts) had found himself in considerable difficulty on the point. The teachers' Perms of service were with the Corporation and there was no restriction or obligation upon them, to give religious instruction of the kind referred to, so that re ally they would have been asked to enter a service, the conditions of which were entirely different from those under which they were onga,,ed. Whether the teachers objected or not it WWI the committee's duty at any rate to see that the conditions were not materially altered without their consent or approval. Treece was also the question of the children who were attending a school where n a sectarian religion was taught. It might be the wish of the parents that they attended such a school. All parents did not like to be put to the unpleasantness of claiming exemption from religious instruction under the conscience clause. It was an invidious position for any person to 0,, pvb if it could be avoided, and he quite understood that many parents would send their children to nueenomina- tional schools to escape the necessity of having to declare themselves in that way. The minute was accordingly withdrawn. TEACHERS' SALARIES INCREASED. The minutes of the School Nianaeement C ammittee recommended increases of salary 'to 191 elementary school teachers in accordance with scale, as from April Ist. Mr UesoN explained that the increases in the aggre- gate amounted to £990. That did not mean for a moment that they were-going to pay .000 more than last year. The increase was more apparent than real, because the total amount which the committee would be paying would be practically the same as twelve months ago. assuming that they had the same number of teachers in their employ. They had rescued that point in the aGlininistration of the scale where the increases which would take effect on April Ist were balanced by the fact that a considerable and growing number of teachers were reaching the maximum, a considerable number of those at the top were leaving to marry, or to take up new appointments, and their places were taken by young teacuers who began at the minimum. As a matter of fact in 1913 they would be paying .£BBO more in salaries than was paid in 1912, but that would be because they had eight additional certified teachers (including a headmaster) in tLeir service through the opening of the Evelyn-street School. They were not paying any more per head of the staff employed than in 1912.

CHILDREN AND ANIUSENIENTS.

It was reported to the School Attendance Committee that the Watch Committee had made arrangements with the proprietors of all places of amusement, in the borough to exclude all children under 14 from seconl evening performances. SCEIOOLS OVERCROWDED. At the meeting of the School Attendance Committee, communications were submitted from the Board of Education, calling attention to the excess of the average attendance during the past school year over the recognised accommodation in the Christ Conrail, St.. Barnabas' (mixed department) and St. Mary's (boys' and girls' department) Schools. Tne replies which had been sent were approved. The Board of Education also wrote that the grant for the Silver-street Wesleyan School for the past school year would be paid in full, but that a deduction would to made from the grant conditionally due on the 30th September, 1913, if the average attendance then still exceeded the accom- modation unless substantial progress had oy that time been made with the provision of accommodatibm to relieve the school by the erection of the proposed. Oliver-street School.

Mr PfatisTos: drew attention to the repeited threats. which the Board of Education had made to reduce grants because of overcrowding in several schools. He said that the threat with regard to St. Mary's had been carried out, and they had been penalized to the extent of $.:30. There were further complaints with reference to Silver-street and St. Barnabas' Sch iota, while at Hamilton-street School they suffered a reduction of £4O last year, and they were fearing a still greater reduction during the present year. He therefore urged upon the Sites and Buildings Committee to proceed with the utmost possible expedition with the erection of the contemplated new Council schools At the same time he appealed to members to refrain from silly and foolish blocking motions which impeded pro- gress and hampered the work of the Sites and Build- ings Committee. Resolutions were sometimes repeat- edly brought before the committee which at the finish resulted in nothing but a waste of time. Ha asked in future that no obstructive tactics should he employed, but that all would join in assisting forward the work of the Sites and BuAdings Committee. Councillor BIRTLKS seconded.

Alderman BOLTON assured Mr Plinston that the Sites and Buildin2s Cimmittee were idling all they possibly could to push forward the erection of the new schools to relieve the overcrowding, but. they ,13. d architects, the land question, and "a few other obstreperous things of that sort to deal with." (Laughter). Councillor PEMBER.TON did not chink it fair to put the blame on the Sites and Buildings Committee and other people, because the Board of Education was largely rosponsibie for the slow progress. The Board continually imposed new restrictions, and kept altering the conditions, and when questions were addressed to the Board the officials at London took weeks to reply. With regard to the hint at alleged obstructive tactics, he should continue to oppose anything that was wrong and improper, and the only way to obviate that opposi- tion was to carry on the business in a proper way.

GIRL HEROINE DROWNED.

May Estate, aged 10, a girl boarded out by the Dundalk Union, was drowned on Thursday afternoon when attempting to rescue Patrick Mullin, aged 7, from a pond at Kilcurry. When Mullin fell into the water the girl jumped in to -rescue him. Both were drowned.

NOTES BY " OVALIST."

It is rarely that a team gets beaten by 27 points to 7, and yet gives such satisfaction to its supporters as did the Wirepullers last Saturday at Wigan. They played a rare good game, and it was only when Dickinson damaged his knee just before the interval that the home team could claim any substantial advantage. lip to this the Warrington forwards had dominated the play by their all round brilliance, and Wigan spent most of their time defending. But Dickinson's injury necessitated Skelhorne leaving the pack, which just gave the Wigan men the opportunities to push home their attack they had so far lacked.

At half-time Wigan only held the mode3t lead of 11 points to 7, and had had by far the worst of the game' But the ultimate end was sure. The five Warrington forwards could not hope to hold the opposition to the end,and in the closing stages points came rapidly from the speedy Wigan backa. But it was not till three- quarter time that Warrington really cracked-up under the unequal pressure. They might easily have taken the lead early in the second half when Tranter was robbed over the line after a brilliant solo effort. Once they got going, the Wigan men played orilliantly, but I should like to have se.rul Warrington finish the game with all their men fit. It would be a novelty, if nothing else, at Central Park-,which has always been an unlucky ground for Warrington players.

Once again chief praie must go to the six doughty forwards of Warrington. Their pace on the heavy ground was surprising, and the opinion was candidly expressed by the Wiganers that Warrington's was the finest pack seen there for some years. It is remarkable how they have trained on and much is due ti the fortunate circumstance that they have all been lucky to escape injury and thus maintain their excellent combination. The way they opened out and followed the ball was admirable and it must be a long time since the burly Sharrocks was so often captured in possession.

What Warrington lacked was pace behind to finish off the fine work of the forwards. The halves did very well but the threequarter line was at the mercy of the Wigan fliers when it came to a question of getting points. On the defence the Warrington backs were " A 1" and until they had a numerical superiority the Wiganers found the barrier to the goal-line practically impassable, not-withstand- :mg their speed and cleverness. Renwick seemed quite out of place on the wing and did nothing at all, but on the opp ,site flank Williams looked well after Bradley, and the fair-haired successor to Ley- tham had the novel experience of failing to get a single point in the 27. He has been averaging three tries a week lately. But few teams have pushed Wigan harder than/Che Wirepullers this Reason.

To-day, Warrington pay the return visit to Le>eds, who got such an eye-opener as to the virility of the Warrington forwat ds on the last Saturday of the old year. The odds, this time, are cert-tinly on the Yorkshiretnen, who have accounted for the Wirepullers regularly at Headingley for some time. We do better there in cup-ties. But the watch is by no means a certainty for Leeds, and on current form the Warring- ton lads should have a fair chance of earning their 'first points in Yorkshire. They will be at full strength with the exception of Dickinson and Brooks, and I am hopeful that they may create a surprise.

The selected team is : Jolley, full back ; Davies, Trainer, Renwick, and Williams, three-quarters ; Nicholas and Daintith, half backs; Thomas, tlhesters, -Skelhorne, Fearnley, Cox and R Thomas, forwarc's.

Warrington at Wigan. Wirepullers Play Well but Lose.

The wirepullers took their strongest thirteen to Wigan on Saeurday, and for the greater part of the game they put up a fine fight. In the end, injuries to Dickenson weakened the Warrington defence, and Wigan won by a substantial margin. The Collier also had a strong team in the field and as the weathers was fine the popular fixture drew an attendance of 12,000. The team and officials were : vvarsiugton—Jolley, back; Renwick, Tranter, Dicken- son and Williams, tureequarter backs ; Nicholas and Daintetb, half-backs ; G I homes, Cheaters, SkelliJrne, Fearnley, Cox and Thomas, forwards. Wigan—Sharrock, back ; Bradley, Jenkins, Walford, and Todd, tereequarter-backs ; Weave and Thomas, half-backs ; Seeling, liamsdale, Francis, CeAdrick Silcock and Williams, forwards. Referee, Mr W McCutcheon Oldham ; touch-judges, Messrs H Lowe (Brougb ton) and T Kiley (Leigh), Warrington opened the game with their customary energy, and the forwards were quickly in evidence with a concerted dribble which beat Walford and Todd, aad resulted in the capture of Sharrock near the line. Wigan tried hard to get the ball away, but the Warring- ton front rankers were remarkably spry on the heavy ground, and the borne line was hard pressed. Jenkins eventually burst through to centre, only for Jolley to return with effect, and a passing run by the Warrington backs looked promising till Dickenson dropped the ball when well placed. Wigan responded with clever work, but Jenkins was upset by Tranter and Thomas , the latter injuring his wrist in the tackle. The \Varrington third line was slow, ayd again Jenkins and Bradley came away speedily, only for Jolley to " plant" the winger in tine style. Again the Warrington forwards raced away, Fearnley, Cox and Chesters forcing Sharrock to get rid of the ball promptly.Skelhorne had a chance,but co ald not reach the ball. Back came the Wiganers with filie passing, Bradley making another dash which was ended in the firm grip of Williams. The homesters .were now within short range of the Warrington line, and long passes between Seeling, Jenkins, and Thomas enabled the latter to find a way through the defence, and the Warrington line was crassad. The scorer added the goal. Warrington again attacked, the speedy forwards grassing Sharrock in fine style, while a tine &lon, of Jolley'a maintained the attack. G Thomas marked from a weak kick by Gleave and opened Warrington's score with a neat goal. Jenkins eventually burst through again for Walford to score, and soon alter Francis threw across to Seeling, who scored a third try by sheer dash. Warrington were still playing aggre, sively and forced play to the other end. Jenkins was smartly robbed by Tranter, the tall Warrington centre scoring a capital try, Renwick adding a goal. Half- time : Wigan, 1 goal 3 tries, (11 points) ; Warrington, 2 goals 1 try (7 points). On resuming, Warrington were again on the attack for some time, though an injury to Dickenson resulted in Skelhorne haying to play as extra threequarter. He was instrumental in stopping Jenkins when the latter looked like goina through. Teen Trainer created a sensation by breaking clear in his own half and punt- ing over Sharrock's head. An exciting race followed, and Tranter was in the act of grounding whoa he was pushed off the ball by Jenkins, who kicked dead. This was hard luck for the Wirepullers who might have taken the lead. Wigan now forced mattes and Coldrick was the means of Wigan attacking. Bradley was held up by Williams when he appeared almost certain to go through. War- rington relieved and Whams made a good run along the right, but he passed to an opponent and the lost grounu was speedily rtcovered. Wigan continuiug to force matters, Gleave handed on to Sealing, who made no mistake at the eorner. There was a quarter of an hour yet left for play and the visiting forwards con- tianed to show very earnest fight, but they were let down behind. Jenkins made a dashing run, and, bluffing Williams, the Welshman had only Jolley to beat, and this being accomplished the \Warrington defence was pierced, Thomas adding the goal. Warrington appeared to fall away after this, and after Jenkins h.d recorded his second try the day's sport concluded when Seeling in the last minute succeeded in forcing his way over, Trioru,s adding a goal. Result :—W;gan, three goals seven tries (27 peiuts) ; Warrington, two goals one try (seven points).

WARRINGTON "A " BADLY BEATEN.—Warrington "A " were decisively beaten by Wigan "A " at Wllderspool on Saturday, after a game in which con- siderable feeling was introduced. There was a large gate. Harmer opened the game, and for a brief spell play settled in the Wigan half. The visitors were the first to take up a dangerous position. and Curran dashPd over for Hilton to kick a goal. The borne forwards rushed to the Wigan end, where Seddon proved safe, but Critchley opened Warrington's score a moment later with a goal from a mark. Wigan came again, and Curran broke claan through and scored under the posts, Hilton again adding. the extra points. Warrington experienced a piece of hard luck, Clare stepping into touch near the line. Hilton frequently opened out the game and the visiting backs showed some smart COM- bination. A gliring car of obstruction by Mclntyre resulted in the award of a try to Wigan, Hilton adding his third goal. At the interval Wigan led by fifteen points to two. 111-feeling was engendered in the second half, and the football was of a nigh order. CHESHIRE AMATEUR CUP. FIRST ROUND-REPLAY.

LATCHFoRD v. HARRoNVEY. These teams again tried conclusions at New Brighton on Saturday, when Wftrowby had a strong team in the field. The game was only in progress a few minutes before Myers opened the account for Harrowby. Latchford retaliated, and Probyn effected a fine save from a close-quarter shot. A few minutes afterwards a penalty was given against Latchford and was taken by Davies, who scored the second goal for Harrowby. A little latgr the Latchford centre forward had hard lines, putting the ball just over the bar. Davies put in a good shot after a solo effort, which the Litchford goal-keeper just managed to clear. From a melee in the home goal, Smith put in a shot, and Prohyn saved what seemed like a certain goal. Half-time: Harrowby 2 goals, Latchford nil. In the second half the leaders had again the advantage and registering two more goals, won by four goals to nil.

_F WORK, EFTS, ORE RECEIVERS FINED.

An echo of the recent works thefts cases was herd the Borough Police Court on Saturday before Mr J ‘'‘ Hayward (in the chair), Messrs it T Fairclough, T Pemberton and A J JolleY.

- Arthur %\ iiliam Hazeldine, 37, Selby-street, and Ernest Hamblett, Leigh Villas, Grappenhall, were charged with having during th,e past six months received shaving soap and scent, the property of Messrs Crosfield Ltd, from Thomas Lawton, well knowing the goods to have been stolen. Hazeldine with receiving two sticks of shaving soap, value 24, and Ilamblett with receiving six sticks of shaving soap and three bottles of scuit, value 13s 6d.

Mr Henry Greenall, who appeared for "Messrs Crosfield, sAid that on the 18Lh inst the man Lawton was convicted at that C.)urt on a charge of stealing soap and perfumes. In conse uence of a statement he mane then, Inspector Williamson saw tha prisonPr Hazeldine at his house and told him that Lawton Lad stated that he had given him some shaving sticks. Prisoner said he had paid sixpence for the stiAs. The man must have known perfectly well that that was not their value. He also made a statement to the effect that he knew Lawton had stolen them and that he had been stealing for some months. The Inspector then saw the other prisoner woo admitted that he had had the shaving soap and scent. For three sticks of soad he paid threepence and he gave a drink for the bottles of scent. For the remaining sticks of soap he paid threepence each. Although the actual amount involved in the present prosecution was somewhat small, pilfer- ing at the works was assaruing serious proportions. The Company therefore felt it was their duty to deal seriously with cases they discovered. There were a large number of employes engaged in the works and it was as much for their protection as in the interests of the firm, proceedings were being taken. Certain privi- leges were given to employes and if men abused those privileges it was probable that innocent people might be suspected. It was quite probable that some of the privileges might be taken away on account of the way they had been abused. The men could buy the articles in a proper manner.—ln fining each defendant as with thf,alternative of a mouths' imprisonment the Chairman said they had only just missed being sent to prison. They ought to have been sent to prison, but the Bench hoped they would take the case as a warning.

LIQUOR CONTROL. MR. HAROLD SMITH, M.P., AND

CLUBS.

[TO THE. EDITOR. " Ex I)IINEE."j

Sir,—Enclosed please find copy of letter sent to H Smith, Esq, M P, in answer to correspondence pub- lished in the " Examiner" on the I,Sth inst.—Yours truly BENJAMIN T. PARKES (Hon. Sec. Citizens' Temperance League). 8, Willis-street, January 23rd, 191:3. Dear Sir,—ln reply to your letter of the 14th iust, we are surprised at your amazement, and think it is based upon a false idea. You say :—" I am amazed that so many who are devoted to the cause of Temper- ance should remain satisfied with demands for further restricting the sale of liquor in premises licensed and controlled by the magistrates and at the same time ignore the fact that the trade is increasing enormously in clubs under conditions which ve:y often constitute a grave scandal." We are pleased you have had the courage to pen these words, and we ask all who are members of clubs and read this correspoadence to note them, and those who are responsiLle for the carrying on of this " Uncoutr3lled source of supp:y " to ponder well over them. Yet, we do not agree with you that s) many temperance workers do ignore it, and would respect- frilly remind you that in the Temperance Bill of 190 S (which was supported by all Temperance Organisations of our land, and especially by theabove League at a memorable meeting held in -Parr Hall on April 11th of that year) contained clauses for the annual registration and control (including inspection) of clubs. You know, sir, as well as we do, how that Bill was slaughtered at a private meeting held at Lansdowne House.

The Bill, a copy of which you forwarded, is not so satisfactory to us as Pal t 4 of the 190 S Bill was, with this exception, that, in your Bill clause 7 defininitely states the hours for the closing of the bars in clubs. It certainly could be strengthened by including clauses dealing with the following points : 1 Objections by persons other than the police to the registration or renewal of registration. as covered by Clause 37 Of the 190 S Bill. 2 The cancelling of registration on conviction of offence as covered by clause 39 of the 19S Bill. 3 The refusal of registration, if iu respect of the premises for which application is made, during the preceding five years a licence has been f,rfeited or a renewal of licence reluzed as covered by clause 40 of the 190 S Bill.

4 The authority for inspection to be very explicit as covered by clause 41 of the 1908 Bill. if you and your friends will accept the suggestions nhmed we promise you what assistauce we can give by arousing public interest in the measure, and as no Bill will, we hope, in the future, having passed the House of Commons, be killed outright, you may be successful in having placed upon the btatute Book ofthe realm an Act, for which you will deserve the honour which comes to all, irrespective of party, who have honestly tried to do something for the welfare of their fellow citizens.

I hate followed your example and ,sent a copy of this letter to our local Press. Yours'truly. BENJAMIN T. PARKES.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
H. Smith, Esq„ M.P. LETTER FROM REV. H. OGDEN.

Sir,—As a vice-president of the Warringt Citizens' Temperance League, I was much interested in the answer which the Member for this borough gave to the resolutions that were forwarded to him by the above League. I was more interested in what the Member omitted than in what be said. May I add ,sir, that my inability to be present at any of the League's meetings has made it impossible for me to reply through that agency. But sir, I am interested in what the Member said but I am equally interested in what he failed to say. There are no two opinions about clubs in the circle of temperance workers. There is this difference between ourselves and the Member, we should aenihilate the drinking practices of clubs, he would—well, what ? One would venture to suggest that he should commence a temperance campaign in the clubs of this town of which he is either the president or the idol. The constituency which he represents has prubahly fewer drinking clubs in it than any town its size in Lancashire. Those that are in it are the illegitimate children of the eolitical parties and probably one half of them are under the patronage of the member for this borough. As a temperance worker I would suggest that the member carry his missionary outfit witn him to the clubs of which be is the patron. if any opening presents itself at the Carlton a word in season would be welcome. Sir, the member for this borough has written about an agreed subject. We are glad that on the question of temperance be it not an entire heathen Be is better than the company he dwells in, but why labour the question of the clubs when all temperance bodies are clamouring for public or magisterial control? There is one phase of club life, however, that does not pertain in public-house life, the club officials can pur- chase beer from whom they will. I suppose nine tenths of Warrington publicans sigh for that charter. But now, sir, that we are agreed about clubs, what has the member f.r this borough to say t,'.> us about breweries and public-houses ? The member says he is interested in the temperance question; let him give us evidence of it. It has been whispered to me, but then it cannot be true, that the member will preside at the annual Licensed Victuallers' dinner at Birmingham shortly. Rumour has it —but Rumour is ever a lying jade—that the Member was a very valiant, opponent of the last Licensing Bill. But then, all may change their opinions. one the less, 05 per cent. of temper- ance workers were anxious that the Bill should become law. Does the Member for this Borough believe in prohibition, and if he does would he send his tract to Wilderspool Brewery or Walton Hall? because I might incidentally mention to the Member, lest he be not aware, that Wilderspool influences this town very badly on Saturday nights. Does the Member support Local Option or Local Veto? Would he give the right to any locality to say when, where, at what hour and to whom drink should be sold in that locality? Would he give to any locality the right that is theirs, namely of renewing licences when that locality should desire ? Would he refuse the selling of drink to women before noon ? Would he close the public house on Sunday ? Would he support a law that refuses drink to a working man at El in the morning? These and questions of this type the Member for the Borough must answer ere temperance workers can credit that his interest in Temperance is serious. Evidently there is no immediate prospect of a Licensing Bill, but if the Member is agreed with the Temperance worker on the questions I have submitted to him, he will be a valuable asset whenever a Temperance Bill is introduced into the House of Commons.—Thanking you in anticipation, I remain, yours sincerely,

H. OGDEN.

134, St. Mary Street, Warrington.

HOCKEY.

LATCHF ORD. The First Eleven defeated South Manchester by 3.0 at Withington last week, a distinctly good performance. The South backs were rather shaky to start with and Pearson and Minerals scored two smart goals. On changing ends South Manchester severely bombarded the Latchford goal but Bruhl excelled himself, two of his saves from hot shots by Pitt and Roberts being remarkably fine. Near the end Latchford again attacked and after Pike in the home goal had emulated Bruhl's example several times Itlinards put on a third goal with a fast shot close in. Pike, Howard. Pitt, and Roberts were the best of the home side. For Latchford, Fletcher and Knight were in grand form and were the most polished backs on the field. Isherwood and Dale centred well from the wings and along with the halves worked some nice positions for the inside forwards. South Manchester " A " turned up at Latchford short but the sides were equalized, each team playing 10. At one time the home team were leadinz, 4-1 but, the score was 5-4 in their favour at the finish. Eccles, Dixon and Gibts in tbe backs and H Fletcher and Hobin in the forwards played well for Latchford. The scorers were Hobin (2), H Fletcher (2), Banks (1). The First Eleven at St Helens this afternoon is :—J Bruhl, goal ; T Dixon, C 13illington, backs ; J B Wilkin- son, A E Bradshaw, A Knight, halves ; R A Dale, J E Pearson, W H Minards' A Starkey, F W Isherwood forwards. Borough Police Court.

TUR,DAY—llefore Couucillor H Roberts (in the chair),

Aldermen Bennett and Smethurst, Col. Ede!sten, Messrs (.; Jackson Holmes, T Locker, W Rictiardson

and J Hepherd. DID HE INTEND TO STEAL ?—Daniel Taylor, of no fixed address, was charged with stealing on January 11th, a gent's vest, value Is Gd from outside the shop of Messrs Park and Bowden, 59. Buttermarker-street and further with stealing on the 10th January a barrel of bone grease value from tbe works of the Warrington Products Co.—The Chief Constable stated that prisoner pledged the vest for 6d and gave a false name and address. He stated to Detective Sergeant Holland that he had bought two vests from a man in a public- house and also that a woman had given it to him. The barrel was stolen during the night, taken some distance from the works and abandoned in the street. About two hours later prisoner tried to remove the barrel but it was too heavy for him, being about a quarter of a ton in weight.—Prisoner said he had no intention of stealing the barrel.—He was sent to prison for three months with hard labour.

DOMESTIC TROUBLES IN LEOI-1 STREET.

HOW A QUARREL WAS HAPPILY

AVERTED,

" What! Another suit, John. You're downright extravagant. That's the second suit of clothes in two months."

"Quite right, girlie dear, but you see this is an extra suit."

"And a pretty penny it's cost judging by the look of it. How will our Easter holidays fare if you con- tinue to spend twice the amount on your clothes that other men do? "

"It's all right, dear--you will be better off when the holidays come round than other wives are."

" Well ! I can't see it. That suit must have cost more than Dick's—it fits perfectly." "I see, I can't keep it a secret any longer: the truth is I got this suit for 12/11 from that firm in Florsemarket-street, Warrington, 'Suits Limited.'" "To measure-12/11—impossible." "It's right, and what's more they've got the biggest range of patterns to choose from in the shop I've ever seen."

"But 1211! I can't believe it. The 'fit' is perfect." " Oh ! well, perhaps, I should have explained that this is an exceptional sale of theirs; although the value they give is always phenomenal. Everything is being sold below cost to clear the stock befcue their • new spring goods arrive. That's why I got this suit at 12/11. Now! Art satisfied? "

"I wish Suits Ltd. sold costumes, that's all." 'They do, my dear, but, don't you agree I've got a real genuine bargain?" " Yes ! And you had better mention it to Dick in case he hasn't read of it in the ' Examiner.' "

Suits Ltd.'s sale of suits, &c., is the greatest sale ever held in Warrington. All goods have been reduced below cost in order to clear them before 1913 materials arrive. HAVE YOU ORDERED YOUR EXTRA. SUIT YET? If not, call for patterns and judge for yourself. The address is : Jl3 BILEE BUILDINGS, HORSEMARKET ST., WARRINGTON, "SUITS LIMITED."

Lancashire Catholic Players' Society.

On Thursday evening the members of the Lanca- shire Catholic Players' Society gave the first of three performances of "King Arthur" (Comps Carr) in the Holy Name Hall, Manchester. The title role was admirably portrayed by Mr W Kilduff, of Warrington (late manager of the St. Mary's Drama,tic Society) who acted with great power aud was rewarded with round after round of applause. Mrs W Hickey (136 e Miss Nora Fleming), who is also well-known in Warrington amateur dramatic circles, achieved a triumph as Queen Guinevere. It is interesting to note that in the original production of " King Arthur," the two roles above mentioned were played by Sir Henry Irving and Miss Ellen Terry respectively.

SIX MONTHS FOR A COWARDLY KICK. . _ .

At the Liverpool Assizes on Tuesday Peter CT)sirove (24), a collier, was charged with felonious wounding, at Earlestown, on November 25. Mr Singelton pro- secuted.

Evidence was given that prisoner and a labourer named Birtwistle were playing " tip it," in the Rail- way Vaults, where a quarrel ensued between them. As they left the house prisoner tripped Birtwistle and he fell. Later on Birtwistle said prisoner would hear more of the affair, whereupon Cosgrove dealt him a violent blow in the eye. While he was unconscious on the pound prisoner kicked him, freaking both bones of one of his legs. Prisoner said ho had acted only in self-defence. The jury returned a verdict, of "Guilty "and prisoner was sentenced to six months' hard labour; his Lord- ship observing that nothing could justify the cowardly act of kicking a man when he was on the ground.

EVILS OF PhOTECTION.

MR. J. A. SEDDON'S INDICTMENT OF AMERICA Mr J A Seddon, Labour candidate for Newton Division, who spoke on Monday night at the nnruial social gathering of the Haydock Women's Labour League, said he had returned from representing the trades unions of this country in America. He had asked his friends to take him among the workers, and there he saw pictures of poverty that were enough to make one's blood run cold. The leaders ‘.l' the trades union move- ment had to admit that under Prot e,tion it was possible for the rich to grow richer, hut the pier became poorer. hie went into the mining districts, and he found that where trades unionism was weak there the men were living practically in a state of slavery. He said, refci ring to Saturday's meeting at St Helens, Lord New.on ought to be ashamed of himself. Then he noticed that Lord Wolmer had said that he would fight the next election. If that was so, be (Mr Seddon), would challenge him to resign, and in a by-election test the feelings of the Newton Division.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 1, 1913.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY FEBRUARY l, 1913.

The Warrington COUNCIL OR Education Committee CHURCH at its meeting on SCHOOL? Monday evening had to consider a proposal which, innocent enough on the surface, raised important questions of principle. The Cairo- street (Infants) Council school has repeatedly been condemned ,y the Medical Officer of Health as hopelessly insanitary and the Committee decided that, in the interests of the children's health, it was imperative to or find new premises. Owing to the erection of the Evelyn-street Coun- cil School the attendance at Holy Trinity (Church) School has been very seriously depleted, and the recommendation which the School Management Committee brought forward on Monday evening was to close the Cairo-street School and transfer the staff—and presumably the scholars—to the Holy Trinity School where a new infants' division would be formed. Mr. F. R. Roberts very properly took exception to the arrange- ment on the ground that the views of the parents and teachers concerned had neither been invited nor considered. Many of the parents would no doubt exercise their right of choicein favour of Cairo-st. School because it was an unsectarian school and would resent the idea of their children being trans- ferred to a school in which they would receive Church teaching—unless they chose to make thernselves conspicuous by pleading the conscience clause. The teachers, as servants direct of the Education Authority, have not been called upon to give definite religious instruction, and it is not inconceiv- able that they might find it distasteful to begin at this stage, to instil {Church doctrines into the minds of the scholars. There appears to be a tacit understanding with the managers that the wishes of the teachers shall be consulted on this important point, but in the absence of an authoritative statement to that effect the committee would not have been justified in approving the 'proposed transfer. After several members had pressed for safeguards on behalf of teachers and parents, the minute was withdrawn and we have little doubt that the committee will insist that, at all events, the teachers' conditions of service shall not be materially varied. The only satisfaction vouchsafed to the parents, up to the present, is that there is no obligation upon them to send their children to any particular school, but that assurance is worth very little when schools in certain localities are so over- crowded that the so-called selection of a school narrows itself to " Hobson 's choice."

The letter from Dr. A ,ElOO ARGUMENT. Markel to the Score-

tary of the Warrington Infirmary, enclosing a donation of £lOO, is a fine reply to the plea of those wealthy firms which have reduced their contributions to the institution because of the cost of National Insurance. Dr. Markel takes the view that the great scheme of state Insurance "far from drying up the. founts of charity, should hearten all those whose desire it is to make systematic war with disease and distress," and his cheque will, we hope, have a solutary effect on those townsmen who are so ready to ease their backs of charity's burden at the expense of the Infirmary and for the specious reason so callously advanced.,

A poster on the local DERRY'S DECIDER. hoardings appropriately says that " London- derry election spikes Carson's guns." The fact that they were dummy guns, and com- paratively harmless before, does not alter the significance of the Nationalist triumph in Derry. Before the election Ulster returned 17 Unionists and 16 Nationalists,a preponder- ance of political opinion which sufficed for Carson and Co. to claim that Ulster would not have Home Rule. Now that the position is exactly reversed it follows ipso facto that Ulster is a Home Rule province. The Government's mandate is thus unassailable, and no-one need hesitate to laugh unrestrain- edly at the comedy in the House of Lords. Ireland, and England too, are united in their demand for Home Rule. Even Sir Edward Carson must bow down before the fiat o f Londonderry. Ulster has spoken!
