LOCAL WEDDINGS. Barber—Toft.

A wedding in which many local people were keenly interested took place early on Monday morning at St Paul's Church. The contracting parties were Mr T Fairclough Barber, of Greenfield House, Latchford, and Miss Arline Bertha Toft, eldest daughter of Mrs H Toft, Horsematket-street. Mr Barber is an old captain of the Lancashite and Warrington Football Clubs, whose fame as a three-quarter back, was of more than local character. 'An years or so have elapsed since he regularly appeared for War- rington, but he has since then been almost continu- ously identified with the club as a member of the management committee. and a large circle of friends will wish the popular "Fair" Barber a happy and prosperous married life. The wedding was quietly solemnised iii the presence of only immediate relatives, by the Rev E E Peters. The bride wore mole coloured velour cloth trimmed with amethyst and mole velvet, and was attended by her youngest sister Miss Nora Toft, as bridesmaid ; she was gowned in turquoise blue, with black velvet beefeater hat and pale pink ostrich feather mount. Mr Richard Denham, Padgate, uncle of the bride, gave her away, and the best man was Mr William Taylor. Following the ceremony there was a recep- tion at the residence of Mrs Toft, after which Mr and Mrs Barber departed for London for their honeymoon

===== 0002601_19130201_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
Mann—Wrigley.

The wedding took place on Thursday at the Irlam Parish Church, of Mr W P Mann and Miss Mary Jane Wrigley, the ceremony being performed by the Rev R Martin, D.D. (vicar). The bride, who was given away by her father, wore a navy blue costume and tegal hat with grey lancer feathers. The bridesmaids were the Misses Amelia and Edith Wrigley, sisters of the bride, and both wore navy blue costumes and large picture hats trimmed with forget-me-nots and moss roses. Mr W A Mann, brother of the bridegroom and Mr J A Wrigley acted as groomsmen. A recep- tion was afterwards held at Woodville, Cadishead, the home of the bride's parents.

Plans for the new Memorial Hall at the Liberal Club, which is to be the generous gift of Mrs. John Crosfield, have been approved. * *

Who has been knob-sticking? The Society of Painters and Decorators has lodged a complaint with reference to the painting of standards by motor-men and conductors. By the by, the man who paints his own gate-post will be looked upon as a job-robber! *

The Council is to be asked to consider "the desirability of extending the boundaries of the County Borough." Is it true that the proposition comes from Padgate, where there is considerable nervousness anent the cost of the new sewage scheme?

The Fire Engine Committee has resolved to advertise for a 450 gallon motor fire engine. Before spending a "cool thousand" in this direction we hope the Council will fully discuss the project. For our own part we should prefer to see the money spent on Parr Hall organ. At present it sounds more like a fire-engine than a musical instrument. * *

The motor-buses are coming, and tenders have been received, but before deciding, Alderman Smethurst, Alderman John Evans, Councillor Wilkinson, and Councillor Peacock are going on a tour of trial and inspection. Joy-rides of a free and easy character will no doubt be included in the programme.

There has already been a great demand for tickets for the great meeting which the Right Hon. Augustine Birrell, K.C., (Chief Secretary for Ireland) will address in the Parr Hall on Saturday, February 15th. Ward workers are therefore advised to make early application at the Liberal Club, or they will run the risk of disappointment. Women will not be admitted. to the meeting.

There has been an apparent slackening of activities on the Warrington Bridge works and many townspeople have been inquiring the why and the wherefore. We are authoritatively informed that the concreting of thP ribs of the first portion of the bridge was completed on January 23rd. The ribs now have to stand six or seven weeks before the " centreing " can be removed and, in the meantime, no further work can be done on the west side. Work has, however, been commenced this week on the training. walls for the east side.

A country correspondent sends us a hedge cutting which is itself ocular demonstration of the mildness of this exceedingly moist winter. The twig has hawthorn leaves fully half size, and old inhabitants of Moore say they never remember the hedgerows being so green in January. Elsewhere in Cheshire the white rock, primrose, and polyanthus are in bloom. These precocious plants may escape being nipped in the bud, but they are pretty sure to meet with a blighting frost or two before spring time comes round in reality.

* * * At the annual gathering of the National Service League on Thursday evening Captain Crosfield had a good deal to say about our military unpreparedness, and finally prophesied that national service "was coming." We might add: So are the Campbells ! Mrs. B. S. Townroe said, with confidence, that "they had made great gaps in the walls of their opponents. The whole fabric was now tottering under their attack." This cheerful complacency reminds us of Mr. Bonar Law's statement at Edinburgh that the Tory party was never so united as it is to-day. Indications may entirely to the contrary, but indications can be conveniently ignored. We hope to publish an extended report of the proceedings in our next week's issue.

Like threatened me-n, the Parr Hall organ lives long. The Sub-Committee had got so far as to recommend the provision of a new instrument at a cost of from £1,300 to £1,500, but the recommendation has been quashed by the General Purposes Committee on the ground of economy. We can foresee the charge being made that this Committee of the whole Council is deficient in the artistic sense, and the charge will probably not per- turb them. What strikes us, however, is that a Council which joyfully provides bowl- ing greens for working men, is wanting in clarity of vision when it persistently jibs at the provision of reasonable aids to the recreation of the more educated section of the community. Music is surely as commend- able as bowls!

The " World " this week, paying a hand- some tribute to the work of rehousing the poor accomplished in Liverpool, sets forth the following table, whose figures may be described by that gorgeous Oriental phrase— " Apples of Gold in Pictures of Silver." EFFECTS OF REHOUSING IN LIVERPOOL. To DEcE3II3EIt 31, 1912.

Total number of sanitary dwellings erected 2,663 Erected prior to 1897 Erected since 1901 (These are for labouring classes, and are reserved for the dishous-ed).

SOME TIEST:LTS. Death Rate: Declined from 50 to 27 per 1,000. Tuberculosis: Declined from '1 to 119 per 1,000. Typhus Fever: Once never absent; in 1910. not a case. Typhoid: In 1896, 1,300 cases ; in 1911 200 cases. Police Prosecutions: Have fallen 50 ner cant. Cash Saving to City: At least £65,000 per annum or double the cost of the rehousing work * * *

„ New blood in the Town Council obviously implies reform and we note with interest a proposition from Councillor Osborne, the new member for St. Austin's Ward, for the meet- ing on Tuesday. Councillor Osborne's pro- posal is for the creation of a new municipal office: that of permanent shorthand writer to the Corporation, whose duties shall be to take a verbatim report of the pro- ceedings of all committees. Considerable objection is, naturally, raised against the proposal in many quarters, but it has a lot to recommend it. So little is really known of what transpires at committee meetings, disputes as to questions of fact are so frequent in the Council, and there is so little frank intercourse between the committees, that a reliable report of the business done and the comments made would be of value to every member of the Council. There is no suggestion that the reports should be made public and in other respects the advantages seem to outweigh the objections. An inter- esting discussion on the question is assured at the Council meeting.

Presentation to Mr Peter Bate. On Saturday morning at the Castle Rubber Co.'s works, a very pleasing presentation took place, when the staff and workpeopte as-ietnbled to give Mr Peter Bate. manager, a clock and ornaments to commemorate his 2.5 years service with the firm. Mr T O'Brien in making the presentation said that considering the large number of employees who had been with the firm for so many years, it was quite unnecessary for him to dwell on the numerous good qualities Mr *Bate possessed. He was certain, however, that he was expressing the sincere xvisli r f every member of the firm when he liopnd that Mr Bate would be spared to manage the affairs of the company for at least another period as long as the one they were commemorating. Mr Bate in responding said that he felt a difficulty in adequately expressing his thanks, and said the gift would always be a pleasing memento of the fact, of which he was exceptionally proud, that he had during the years of service with the company, earned the goodwill of all those with whom he came into contact. Local Off ical's New Appointment.

At a meeting of the Tynemouth Town Council on Wednesday, Mr Walter Sumner, of the Town Clerk's Department, Warrington, was uranimouslv appointed chief clerk in the town clerk's department,Tynemouth. Mr Sumner commenced his business career in the office of Messrs Robert Davies and Co. in 1.89 i, where he remained until he obtained a position in the Town Clerk's office in July 1903. He has had considerable experience in municipal work and recently has been chiefly responsible for the administration of the Revenue Acts with reference to local taxation licences, this work having been transferred by the Govern- ment to County and County Borough Councils in 1908. In this connection he has been brought into direct daily contact with many townspeople, and whilst carrying out his duties n a highly satisfactory and conscientious manner, he has deservedly won the esteem of all those with whom he has had duties to perform. He is highly respected by all the officials of the Corporation, and is to be c-mgratulated on obtain- ing his new appointment, for which be is eminently fitted. The Council summons for the meeting on Tuesday next contains a resolution that Mr Sumner's resignation be accepted with regret. Mr Stanley Wilson (formerly deputy town clerk of Warrington) is the Town Clerk of Tynemouth. Warrington Swimming Club. - -

DEATH OF MR THOMAS IRELAND.

Through the death of Thomas Ireland, Grappenhall loses one of its most respected residents. Mr Ireland died on Saturday last after an illness from heart affection lasting five weeks. By his quiet, unassuming manner end his broad-mindedness, he had gathered round him a good number of people whom he could truly call his friends, and by these his loss will be greatly felt. He was educated at Grappenhall Schools, and from there he went into the service of the L.N.-W. Railway Company. He was an engine driver for the company for many years; in conversa- tion he could recall some very interesting events as the result of his railway experience, amongst which he related the fact of being the first driver to take a goods train over the Runcorn Bridge. He also formed one of a party to board the first # engine over the iron bridge at Latchford Locks after the completion of the Manchester Ship

Canal. Mr Ireland left the railway 30 years ago. Twenty years were subsequently spent as landlord uf the Cheshire Cheese Inn at Latchford, and from active business life he had been retired for the past ten years. He took a very keen interest in the Warrington Borough Band, ar.d was one of its most ardent and practical supporters. He was at one time a member of the Grappenhall Parish Council. Mr Ireland was a Freemason of the Gilbert Greensll Lodge 1250. and throughout his life was a staurch- Conservative. He leaves a widow and two daughters.

QUERIES AND ANSWERS.

OUR READERS are invited to send It gal queries to be answered week by week. Inquiries must be short, concise, and plainly written, and the name and address of the writer given to show that the inquiry is genuine. The replies are by a member of the profession, and every effort is made to secure accuracy, but we cannot assume any resnonsibility for the opinion or advice given. Mark all envelopes containing queries " Legal," and address them to the EDITOR,' EXAMINER,' WARRINGTON. If 3ou do not wish initials to be used in the reply please t-uggest a nom-de-p/ame.

A 0 J.—lf you were employed from the beginning "for the purposes of your employer's business" you cught to have been insured. If you were emp;oyed for a purpose not connected xvith your employer's business it is nevertheless likely that you ought to have been insured e4ince you were empfoyed regularly cn Thursdays This being so, it is useless for you to stamp the card for the weeks before you entered an Approved Society since you ale not entitled to sick benefits until 26 weeks after ycur entry into the society. The form of application for insurance cannot be antedated.

W.W.—(l) No, there is no other waiting period in the case you mention. There is no second waiting period unless 12 months have elapsed and fifty weekly payments have been made since the last sickness benefit was received. (2) The Approved Society does not alwa)s insist on a low wage declaration. In this case no arreats would be debited against the woman.

SUFFRAGETTE.—If a married woman who was a member of an Approved Society before marriage becomes employed on the death of her husband, the- period of one month after his death will not he taken into account for the purpose of reckoning arrears.

CURIOUS.—If a married woman who was a member of an Approved Society before marriage continues to be employed, she may either remain insured on the same terms or remain suspended and become a voluntary contributor at reduced rates.

DOMESTIC.—An insured domestic servant who marries may either continue paying her 3d a week or pay no more. In the former CRSO she will get medical treatment and medicines; sick pay of ss. a week for 13 weeks and disablement pay of 3s. a week afterwards. Sick pay is not given for the two weeks before and the four weeks after confinement. In the latter case her Society will put aside two- thirds of her transfer value for her benefit and will pay her on confinement 5s a week for not more than four weeks and, if It thinks fit, grant certain pay- ments during any period of sickness or distress. INSURANCE.—The Agent who told you that if you continued to pay your premiums for a little time longer you would get a Free Policy deceived you and ;on can recover the premiums you have paid. "HOLLY COTTAGE."—Your neighbour who has called his house "Holly Cottage" has committed no offence merely because your house is also called "Holly Cottage," and is not actionable.

GUARDIANS' CONTRACTS.

at a meeting of the Guardians. lie further observes that tenders received from tradesmen in respect of quarterly supplies are presented to the Guardians and a resolution is passed authorising the Chairman to open,number and initial the tenders in the Board room; they are then handed by him to the Clerk who has them extended, that is, the prices quoted per unit are multiplied by the estimated requirements for the period of the proposed contract and the results totalled. The tenders are then presented to the Guardians at an adjourned meeting and are considered on their totals only, each accepted tender being accepted as a whole. The goods estimated for are ordered from the con- tractors with the exception of food stuffs and other perishable goods which are ordered as required. The system of accepting tenders en bloc is open to objec- tion, in that it prevents the consideration of tenders from any tradesman who is unable to supply the whole of the articles set out. One tender only was received for each of the following heads : meat, mackintosh sheeting, uniforms, wines and spirits crockeryware, soda-water, coffins, thirds ; two only for each provisions, oatmeal and flour, groceries, paints, sweeping chimneys, tobacco and snuff, drugs and drapery. lam to request that the Board may-be fur- nished with the observations of the Guardians on the above.

Mrs SIMPSON suggested that the letter be referred to the House Committee.

Mr MATHER : If only a certain number of trades- men tender, what are the Guardians to do? Shall we send the auditor round to them and ask them to send tenders in ?

Mr A BROOKS: No, divide the contracts. But I would like to know how you are going to divide chimney sweeping and that sort of thing. If he had tackled groceries, it would have been a different thing, but those are the-very goods for which we have the most competition. Mr JOLLEY said the matter was one for the Guardians and not for the auditor to settle, although the auditor was, of course, within his legal rights in calling attention to it. He (Mr Jolley) believed the system aqopted by the Board had resulted in their securing the best quality of goods at a very low price. The reason why in some instances there was only one or, as occasionally happened, no tender, was that competition was so keen that many tradesmen would not trouble to supply goods, because the margin of profit was so small. He moved that the Clerk be instructed to reply to the Local Government Board to the effect that Guardians, after full, frequent. and careful consideration, were satisfied that their method was the best, in view of all the local circum- stances and conditions, in the interests of the rate- payers as a whole. Mr A BROOKS, in seconding, said it should also be pointed out that the very items in which they got much competition were those which could not be divided.

The CLERK-said that, with regard to drapery, there was something in tbe auditor's complaint. The system of taking tenders en bloc did, in that case, tend to restrict the number of tradesmen competing On one occasion the Board received four tenders for drapery from local firms, but because two of them did not tender for all the items they were thrown out and ndt considered. The com- petition was therefore tied down to one or two who were prepared to tender for every item. With regard to the opening of tenders, the order of the Local Government Board was strictly complied with. Mr A BROOKS said the drapery tenders included nothing but what a draper oueht to stock. If the orders were split up, the Board would have to pay fancy prices for oddments. Mr. Jolley's resolution was agreed to.

Doctors and Insured Patients.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, Mr B L Bent presirlng. There were also present : Mrs Simpson, Mrs Collingwood, Rev A M Mitchell, Messrs A J Jolley (deputy-chairman), G Mather, W Houghton, J Billinge,W Gill, J Backbouse,W Houghton, W Flanigan, T Ritchie, J A Hughes, W Furness. A Brooks, M W Sewn, A Bottomley (clerk), It I Partridge (assistant clerk), J Brown (master), and C E Lowrance (superintendent of Cottage Homes).

REV. A. M. MITCHELL'S QUERIES. On the minutes or the Finance Committee, the Rev A M MITCHELL asked whether the committee went carefully into the question of pay- ments. With regard to the Workhouse master's children, it was understood that there were to be regular paymei ts for their rations. Did the Finance Committee give attention to the point? There was also the question of visitors to the institution.At a recent meeting of the House Committee, it was reported that one of the officers had had some friend or triends staying with him for a few days. The committee ought to be able to say what amount had been paid in respect of those visitors—in fact a scale of charges ought to be drawn up. Then again a coal bill of over £4—working out at 223 a ton—bad recently been passed. He thought that was an exorbitant .ch urge, and the Finance Com- mittee ought to be able to detect it. Mr A J JOLLEY (chairman of the Finance Committee) .said the members of that committee were quite as anxious as Mr Mitchell to look after all the details. They did not show their anxiety in the same form but it was just as real and earnest, and in the interests of the ratepayers. NEW BUILDING SCHEME.

The Building Committee again considered plans for the erection of a building for the accommoda- tion of male and female imbecile and epileptic patients. The architect (Mr Segar Owen) sub- mitted two schemes and, after careful consideration, it was resolved—by four votes to one—that the Board be asked to proceed with the erection of a building in accordance with plans prepared by Messrs W and S Owen, on lines somewhat similar to a building erected at the Reading Parish Workhouse ard in accordance with the suggestion of the officers of the Local Government Boird, and that the Local Govern- ment Board be asked to give their sanction thereto. The Committee, were aware that if the scheme was proceeded with forthwith in its entirety, the question of the erection of new Board room and offices must be dealt with at once, and, so as to defer the office ques- tion for a time, they recommended that temperary pro- vision be made in the main building for the reception of patients, also padded room accommodation ite. Mr Owen was instructed to modify his plans accordingly, and at a further meeting of the committee he submitted a set-,nni- on those lines, involving a total expenditure of £12,580. Cousiaeiable discussion took place on the above minute, the Rev A M MITCHELL moving an amendment that it be referred back. From the first he had opposed that very expensive scheme, because it seemed to him that the Board were taking upon themselves a work which ought to be done by another authority. There was little doubt that when the total outlay came to be reckoned up, it would be practically double the -amount stated in the minutes. Referring to the fact that the Board would shortly go out of office, the rev. gentleman said the Guardians reminded him of people who, the nearer they were ta bankruptcy, the greater dash they made. The Board was approaching the close of its existence and it seemed to have a great desire to spend lavishly and make a big show. Mr W C BROOKS. in seconding the amendment, con- tended that, as part of the scheme was of a temporary -character, the ultimate cost would be £20,000. They would have to undertake the erection of new offices and Board room in the immediate future. He favoured the scheme which was originally p4ssed by the com- mittee but rejected by the Board. Mr BILLINGE characterised Mr Brooks's estimate of £20,000 as very far-fetched. The scheme was the better adspted to the requirements of the Board, whereas the original one, which Mr Brooks favoured, did not commend itself to the officers of the Local Government Board. After further discussion the Building Committee's recommendation was approved by 13 votes to 3. Mr W C BROOKS: k very unlucky number! PRAISE FOR THE INFIRMARY. . .

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

LORD NEWTON. WOMEN'S SUFFRAGE AND THE HOUSE OF CCMMONS.

Lord Newton was in the chair on Saturday at a quarterly meeting in St. Helens of Newton Division Conservative and Unionist Association. Lord Wolmer. M.P., also attended. Lord Newton said that it was more difficult now than formerly to say when a Parliament would come to an end, when its members were each interested to the extent of £4OO a year. At the presant moment the ll,tuse of Commons was nominally discussing a Fran- chise Bill, and discussing it under such conditions as Mr Asquith thought fit to impose upon it. What the House of Commons was trying at the present moment to do was to " dish" the women and to sneak out of difficulties which they had created, either be their own dishonesty or bs their ignorance. "The House of Commons,'" continued Lord Newton," has been alritady sufficiently degraded by Mr Asquith. It has beerr"degraded as much as it is possible to degrade and humiliate any constitutional assembly, but I ques- tion whether it has ever presented so absolutely grotesque, ridiculous, and humiliating a spectacle as it does at the present moment." His impressiom was that nothing would cast such absolute dismay into the Liberel ranks as for the House of Lords to allow the Home Rule measure to pass. He believed any prospect of that kind would fill the Liberal party with the utmost consternation. (Laughter.) If it were passed be believed they would find the difficulties so insurmountable that they would seize the earliest opportunity for leaving office. He did not think the question of allowing this Bill to pass the House of Loras bad ever been really considered, although the suggestion had been made. But the objection to adopting any such course was that it would be a distinctly dishonest proceeding, and he hoped that, whether mistaken or right, the action of the Lords would be dictat,d by honest motives. It would be a dishonest thing to accept the Home Rule Bill for many reasons, but one was that it had never been properly before the country. (Applause). Load Wolmer defended the position he had taken us in regard to the use of the funds of trade unions for the purposes of members of one political party in Parliament It was only fair and just, he said, that trade union, should be allowed to send their representatives to the House of Commons without impediment, but what the Labour party wanted was to use trade union as supporters of one political party. At the next general election he was prepared to maintain the position that every trade unionist, whether Conservative, Socialists Liberal or what not, had a right to choose his own political views for himself. To that mast he nailed his colours, and he was prepared to fight on that question and win against all-comers.—(Applause.)

St. Paul's Church.

At this Church on Sunday the annual sermons on behalf of the Church of England Waifs and Strays Society, were preached morning and evening by the Rev Herbert A Wafter), the organising socretary for the Liverpool district. A "pound service" was held in the aftel noon, when pounds of tea, coffee, anger, rice, flour, Sze., were brought by members of the con- gregation adults and children and placed near the altar. The gifts were afterwards sent to the society's local home in Salisbury-street.

Bold-street Men's Bible Class.

On Sunday afternoon the speaker at the above class was Mr J B Baxter, who gave an interesting lecture on "The Bible." The origin of our Bible was traced from the ancient manuscripts; and the various heroes of Bible production were introduced. The painstaking energy which was ever characteristic of the translaters, their scholastic abilities and the thoroughness of their research, all make our Bible, a book in which we can implicitly believe. The need for a revision of the completeness of the methods adopted to produce the revised version, were pointed out by the lecturer. The president of the cla,s (Mr J C Holland) end several members, expressed their hearty appreciation of Mr Baxter's able address.

English Church Union.

A meeting of the Warrington branch of the English Church Union was held at the Masonic Hall, Bold- street, on Tuesday evening, when the Rev L L Orton (Society of the Faith) gave a lecture on " The Orthodox Greek Church." He contended that the schism between the Eastern and Western branches of the Catholic Church was largely due to racial antagonism and that made re-union all the more difficult.. Referring to the vca.: in the Balkan States, he said all Christians looked forward to the tin e when the Turks would be driven out, and the Greek relfgionists would return to the possession of their Own city. The Rev W J Stanton, who presided, said that one hoped that one of the results of the war And the alliance of the Balkan States would be that the schism, although one entirely of nationality, might be speedily healed, and that there would be peace within the whole of the Orthodox Church. On the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr E H Coates, a vote of thanks was accorded to the lecturer.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was a good meeting on Sunday, presided over by the President (Mr B Farrar), who gave a short report of the Lancashire P.S.A.Federation meetings at Ashton-under-Lyne. Opportunity was taken to con- gratulate Aldermen Parker, president of the Federation, on attaining his 76th birthday. Mr Thomas Dale gave the address on "Choose ye this day whom ye will serve" and "As for me and my house,we will serve the Lord." He emphasised the desirability of a good choice, and said that to serve God meant to make sacrifices for God and man, in trying to bring peace and brightness to the homes of the people. Mr Cotton read the lesson, and Mr Himsworth led the prayer.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
St. John's Presbyterian Guild.

The above society met on Monday evening, when ft paper was given by Mrs Henshall, of Latchford, on "The cry of the oppressed." With many apt illus- trations, the speaker pointed out bow history repeated itself and as God raised up Moses to free the children of Lrael from their cruel oppressions, so He had from time to time, in our own land and in other lands, raised up good men and women to fulfil some work and in doing this, their source of nower had been felt and acknowledged throughout the entire earth, a mighty influence for good. This was the only source ...from which oppression in all its firms could be mitiiited and released; a truer standard of Christian duty faith- fully followed, would strengthen the individual and the nation and overthrow the causes of oppression. There was a good discussion.

St. John's Presbyterian Church.

Preaching at St John's or; Sunday evening last; the Rev W Reid spoke of the various sources of sacrifice in connection with Judaistic worship and its orderly sequence running there through. Taking the sin offer- ing, with its roi.t idea of atonement, be proclaimed the fundamental of Christianity as Christ, in his life, death, resurrection and intercession. The burnt offering,witH its root idea of consecration, marked the next point of development, and spoke of the willing and complete self surrender to the call of God and humanity. The peace offering, having, at its root the idea of thanks- giving, was the point on which the preacher dwelt the most, putting before his hearers suggestions of the out%ard forms in which the thanksgiving spirit might clothe itself, and which were to be found in inereasing interest in and faithfulness. to the various ministries oi the Church, in various rtprns of its worship, and in the upkeep and extension of its ordinances.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
80ld-street Wesleyan Church. "Self-Righteousness."

Choosing as a text Luke xviii, 9, the Rev ET Williamson, 8.A., spoke nu " Self-righteousness." The parable of the Pharisee and the Publican contains, not only a message to some individual Pha-isee of far-if days in Palestine,but a modern lesson to a number of people,who trust in themselves that they are righteous, and despise others. This lesson is perfectly ph,in ; its teaching being that such persons are, judged by ultimate standards, entirely ong It may be allowed that, as a statement of fact, the Pharisee's self-laudation is quite true. The falsehood does not lie in the state- ment. It is to the self-righteousness and pri le that we must look, for the weak spot that vitiates the life. The self-righteous man is always a weak man. The- Pharisee was sufficiently strong-willed to refrain from extortion, adultery, and gluttony ; yet, his strength of will did not imply strength of clrracter. He was crippled by the weakness of vanity, which leads man to commit treacheries of which he would not dream himself capable The strong-willed men, with bitter racial, doctrinal, or political prejudices are puny individuals withal, who worship the smallest of deities, viz. themselves The haughty Englishman is too vain to recognise the fine points in the German ; the orthodox individual is too orthodox to appreciate the vastness of knowledge; the heretic is too busy worshipping heresy to examine the other side of the question. This type of man is too small for us to worship. The great souls are those who have felt the vastness of the world ; feeling with Kelvin and Darwin, that their knowledge is slight, their ignorance profound. The greatest s .ints, like St. Paul and St. John. have confessed themselves the burr blest of sinners. This is not sheer morbidness, but a genuine conviction that, with all their striving, these saintly souls are still an infinite distance from the ideal. The universe is a spacious affair, and it is the sense of its spaciousness we need to preserve. During the Fprwice the anthem," Happy and blest are they," was ably rendered by the choir.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe 13ongregational Church.

The Rev H H Stull. 8.A., of Darwen was the preacher at this church on Sunday. The hard was the subject of his address to the children, based on the text "My soul is continually in Thy Hand." In an interest- ing way he showed the characteristics of the hand and impressed upon his young hearers the importance of keeping their bands free from dishonourable actions. 'their souls, he added, were in an especial degree in their own hands. The sermon dealt with the story of the vineyard (Matthew 20). He said it was the story of human life in its relation to the eternal, and our little figurines and calculations were out of place in regard to such a matter. God wanted our service, loyalty, faih and love early in the morning of life rind all through life but He did not coerce us into these things. '1 here was no bargaining as to the reward, .s God had a way of reckoning of His own. A small quantity of work done in a right spirit was of greater value than a large quantity of work done in a wrong spirit, however, zealously it might have been performed. In the evening, the discourse was specially addressed to young people, the text being "Rejoice, 0 young man, in thy youth ; and let thy heart cheer thee in the days of thy youth, end walk in the ways of thine heart rind in the sight of thine eyes ; but know thou, that for all these things God will bring thee into jude- ment." (Ecclesiastes xi. 9). Youth, he said, was a ' critical time, for momentous issues were being settled in the early years, It was the determinant of what the future life was to be. He proceeded to speak of what the parents can do for their sons and daughters, and secondly of what the sons and daughters can do for themselves, He showed that modern industrialism had brought about the early economic independence of young people and weakened the home ties. It was the character of the parents rather than their money that had the greater effect on their children's lives and happiness. He advised sons and daughters to rejoice in their youth to be pleasant and make themselves agree- able to older people. Owing to the gross miscarriage of education, conversation and reading were becoming lost arts. He exhorted them to cultivate these arts and also their sense of imagination, and above all these things to have a reverent spirit, honouring God, their King and country. Collections were taken on behalf of the Lancashire Congregational Union which works for the extension of Congregationalism in the country. To-morrow (Sunday) the Rev. T T James, M.A., commences his ministry at Wycliffe Church.

Cairo-street Chapel. Dr. Mellor and his Work In Warrington

have never found an atmosphere in which freedom was so intensely real as it is in this church," were the words of the Rev Dr S A Mellor in an interesting address at the annual congregational tea party in con- nection with the Cairo-street Chapel, held in the school- room on Monday evening. About 160 persons partook of tea, the tables being presided over by Mesdames Mellor, Pemberton, Shaw, C Madeley, Hill and the Misses D Monks and Houghton At the subsequent meeting, the choir, under the direction of Mr J M Muir, submitted an excellent anti varied musical programme. Mr W H Woodcock presided and was supported by the pastor, Dr Mellor, the Revs N Cross '.Southport) and E S Russell (Liverpool), and Messrs F W Monks, J W Davies (secretary), H Noble and E Plinston (superin- tendent of the Sunday scho('l). In his opeuiug remarks the Chairman cheracterised such a large meeting as reminiscent uf Cairo-street in days gone by, when the chapel might be said to be at its best. Those days might be repeated to-day and the, chapel might he seen at its best once more. Since Dr Mellor's urrival there had been a considerable increase in the congregations, but still there were a few vacant places which they would like to see occupied. It was really important, if Cairo-street was to attain a high pesition arid bear out its n ible tra tat they should all take a regular part in the services. It was plainly evident that the Sunday School must play a large part in the future developments of the work at Cairo-street, and to see such a large progressive Sunday School as they now had was a good augury. He thought they would agree with him that Dr 'Mellor had made a great impression on Warritigton. (Hear, hear.) Whether they agreed with him or not, in everything he said. they must be conscious that he was teaching them how to think—(bear, hear)—and that was a very important point. He was, as they all knew, deeply interested in all kinds of social work and he realised as he (the speaker ),and all of them realised, how much there ought to be done and how itch there could be done if one really tried to do it. Tee Doctor was by his sermons teaching them to think for t her 'selves, if nothing more. They were extremely Odd to have the visitors with them from other towns to encourage them to work. (Applause), Mr E PLINSTON gave a resume of the Sunday School work and said the Sunday school and all its institutions were in a progressive aria flourishing condition. At the beginning of lase year they had 184 scholars on the books and at the end of the year they had 235. (Applause.) That was a very good advance. The increase had been mostly in the adult classes and they now had almost. 140 scholars over the age of sixteen years. (Applause ) bought they ought to be able , to look to the fir ure of the school and chapel with levery confidence with such a goed number of young peeple under the leadership of eliss Houghton and Dr Mellor. Not only were their numbers increasing hut the older scholars were beceming more animated with that spirit of loyalty tied self-sacrifice which was so much desired. Although they were, doing so well there were two directions in which he would like to appeal to them for support. They needed more teachers for the boys' class and they would also like to increase the number of scholars in the infant and junior classes. They had a sick club, band of hope, teachers' prepara- tion class rind reading circle, librars, girl's club,a penny bank and a youn; pa pl •'s so iety. They also had a cricket club which did good in its ewn way. (Applause). In an interesting ederess Dr Aleesoß said that when four more Sundays had passed by he would have been with ahem exactly a saar, as their minister. (Applause) He thought they could look back on that twelve months with a certain satisfaction, not undue or conceited satisfaction, but humble satisfaction. During that period they had done a little good work—(applause)— the size and character of that meeting bore witness to that fact. Mr Woedcock told them that meeting reminded him of Caito-street in its best days. It always seemed to be rather ma pity that best days should be considered te belong to the past For him the best days of an institution belonged to the future. Best days could uever be past days. They were either present or to come and that was true of institutions, individuals and the wield at large. It was that sort of a spirit that in a way formed the basis of all his philosophy arid activities. It was because be believed profoundly and absolutely in th, future that be did what he did and said what be saia. He thanked them for the extraordinary kindness they had shown to him during his ministry. He had never found an atmosphere in which freed ne was so int.nsely real as it was in that church. (Applause.). The breath of their real life was freedom. The life of the tuture absolutely and utterly depended upon freedom. It could not be lived without freedom of mind, spirit, intellect and will. Without freedom, life was impossible. Sometimes in these days when a ministry was so essentially a personal thing, one was apt to forget the denomination and wider circles of church lite, tut they were there, and in their denomination the tradi i not' religion Was the tradition of freedom. That was what they had stood for through the centuries, and in those old chapels like Cairo-street, with such a magnificent hist re, tat gl 'Hone con-. dition of freedom had been realized. At any rate it had been realized in his case during the last twelve months. Never by word or sign, or in any way to bis knowledge, had any member or friend of the congreg Lion interfered with his freedom. (Applause, That wes not a tribute to his sincerity ; it was a tribute to the'r loyalty to principle. V% hen they became mem be s of that Church and asssoci- ated themselve- with it in any way and gave their hearts and lives to it, they became associated with a certain principle and to that they expressed their loyalty. Mr Woodcock bad said perhi pa it, was not always that members of the congregation end chapel had agreed entirely and altogether with the things he said. That was never likely to he the case and never could be amongst human beings The thing which was so destructive to their real spiritual energy and life, was the attemist to impose an-.'s views upon others to the exclusion of thPir OWD It was the assertion of an external principle of authority that made religion a dead and meaningletss thing. He had, never asked or

expected that every individual should subscribe absolutely and utterly to the views he expressed. All he had expected and asked from them had been that they should endeavour to think for themselves and if the result of their independent and individual thought had taken them in a direction different from that which be bad taken, they had been able to differ, knowing that in spirit they were one. That was the character and life of that chapel and that was why it was possible for him to say things,freely, sincerely and openly. Continuing the Doctcr said he had! found Cairo-street happy in its officials and he took that opportunity of thanking all of them. He was proud of the increase of membership in the Sunday school and lie was also proud of the distinct increase in the size of the congregation. He was happy to think that that increase was likely to be a permanent and not an accidental or temporary one, in conclusion the Doctor said he believed—and he said it entirely without conceit for he was not egostic in the matter—that he had created an atmosphere in which people felt better for the coming into it. They entered the chapel and realised things which they could not realise in their ordinary life. To make the chapel what it c tight to be they wanted freedom, loyalty and, above all, sincerity of purpose. He felt sure that he had found those things during the 12 months he had been with them. How- ever, he could not have made any impression in War- rington if it had not been for the congregation who had given him the freedom he wanted. Ile stood for a strong congregation, spiritually and numerically and he thanked them. (Applause). After interesting addresses had been given by the Revs E S Russell and N Cross a vote of thanks was accorded the Chairman, the visiting ministers and I helpers on the proposition of Mr F 1V Motitis.

The Late Mr. Sam Hassell. Memorial Service at the Workingmen's

Mission

A deeply impressive memorial service to the late Mr Sam Hassid!, conductor of the Warrington Male Chora* Union, was field on Sunday evening in the Working- men's Mission with which he had been identified from early boyhood. There was a crowded congregation. The widow, parents and many relatives and friends of the late Mr Hassan attended. Mr Harrison, of Liverpool, was the preacher. The Warrington Male Choral Union was in attendance and rendered "The long day c,oses " very feelingly. Mrs B A Sanders gave an expressive interpretation of "0 rest in the Lord" and Mr W Shaw, of Runcorn, sang "Thou art passing hence" in a deeply sympathetic manner. Mr F Atkinson was at the organ rind played the i Dead March n " Saul." Mr Geo Dale, in the course of an appreciation of Mr Hassall, said that, while interested in many things, he was most widely known, respected and loved through the musical talent which had been bestowed upon him so lavishly and which he cultivated so thoroughly and assiduously. Towards the end of the year 1900 four or five young men who were closely connected. with the Bank 'Quay Mission decided to spend their winter evenings in rehearsing glees. So satisfactory were those, meetings, that they invited i others to join, and n ebruary 1901 the Warrington Male Voice Choir was inaugurated with a membership of 12, Mr- Hassall being leader. At the pre- sent time the Choral Union, of which Mr Hassall and Mr Nesbitt were latterly joint leaders, had a membership of over GO. He need not remind them of the splendid services of that organisation. They had all been keenly interested in the reports which had come to them from time to time relating to the honours that had been wi ill n important competitions, and they at Bank Quay Mission experienced a glow of justifiable pride as, in their thoughts, they had associ- ated with those triumphs the name of one whom they still claimed as one of them. His connection with the Warrington Musical Society extended over fifteen years and he was also orFanizer and leader of the Highfield Tannery choir. huncor. He was conductor of the mission choir for many years and was willing to help whenever his musical ability could be employed for the furtherance of some high and noble object. Mr Dale referred to several notable characteristics of the late Mr liassall, including his reverence or his parents, his genial disposition and good htunour.Finally he expressed deep sNmpathy with those who, by the ties of nature, were so near and dear to him, and who had so acutery felt the pangs of bereavement.

Workingmen's Mission, Bank Qum Congregational Tea Meeting.

- - The congregation cud workers associated with the Workingmen's Mission, Bank Quay, held their annual tea meeting on Monday evening and, as usual, the function was a very happy and enjoyable one. About 135 sat down to tea, and a nicely varied and very inter- esting entertainment followed. Mrs Crosfleld, of Colwyn Bay, presided and the audience expressed its appreciation of her great interest in and 'practical support of the work of the mission by giving her a very hearty welcome. The major portion of the programme was supplied by the Sylvan , Quartette Party (Mesdames Sara Silcock and 14 Eckersley. Messrs W Dutton and Sephton). The voices of the party blended moat effectively in " When the budding blown," " Good night, beloved," "In this hour of softened splendour " arid " Good evening." Madame Silcock was enthusi- astically encored for en artistic interpretation of " 0 flower of all the world." Madame Eckersley was heard to distinct advantage in " My dear Soul." Mr Dutton acquitted himself well in "*Thy sentinel am I," end Mr Sephton sang "The sailor's grave" with expression. ' Madam is Silcock anu Eckersley were a:sociated in the duet " I heard a voice," and Messrs Dutton and Sephton gave " The battle eve." Masters Dunbavaud and Dowling gave humorous recitatione with great success. Much-appreciated features were the dramatic recitals " he Friend " and "'1 he Lifeboat "by Mr Dean. Mr F Atkinson played the accompaniments in sympathetic style. The annual report on the work of the Mission was presented by the Secretary (Mr A Tilling) who stated that the year had been one of steady progress. He emphasised the fact that there was an indestructible link between the Sunday school and the chapeLso much so that the condition of the one havariabiy reflected the condition of the other. It was quite impossible to treat them as two distinct institutions, it had long been recognised by the past and present workers of the Mission, but in many churches the truth was only just beginning to he realised that the S nday school was an extremely important branch (if not the most important branch) of the church. The membership had been increased by 16 during the year, making the number on the roll 136, the highest it had ever been in the history of the Mission. The number of Communicants during the year was 820, an average of 68 per Communion service. The attend ,nces at all the services of the Mission had been fully maintained, and it was safe to say that the average attendance at the various meetings held en the Mission premises every Sunday (including Sunday school, , cha?el,n.nd lantern services) totalled almost 2,000. The ' new" Excelsior " 11.11 had proved, and was proving, a very great boon. While the thanks of the workers were given utterance to in the Sunday echool secretary's report a few weeks ago, they felt that in the Chapel ' report they must place on record their high appreciation of the splendid gift of Mrs Crosfield, and to again assure her that her kindness and generosity were a great stimulus to the workers, in the responsible and sometimes trying duties they had to perform. They again had no difficulty in filling their pulpit during the year. They had had rnen of many different denomina- ti"ns, and no doubt missions such as theirs did a good deal to break down the barriers which had divided so many Christians in the past. With regard to financial matters, the chapel accounts stood as follows :—Brought forward from the previous year £ll 18s 70, income during the Near £133 2s expenditure during the ! year £136 16s o.d,balance in treasurer's hands £8 4s 7j,d. The bazaar held in November was a great success, the total prefit, after paying all expenses, being £277 5s 2id. That, together with the amount they previously had in the Mission Extension Fund, made a total of £3ll Ss lid in baud. The Officers and Com- mittee for the year 1913 were as follows :—Presiden t Mr Nathan Hough ; vice-president, Mr George Dale ; treasurer, Mr T Taylor ; communion secretary, Mr It Jones ; general ecretary, Mr A Tilling ; Committee Messrs T Dale, D 11111, W Worrell, I? Atkinson A E Edwares, 3 Hassell, T Edmunds, T Woolley, P Cleave Arthur Gleave,S Millington, nos Ashley and Thomson. , The amount dispensed in relief by the League of Help during the pest year was £34' 19s 6d, in addition to numerous articles of clothing, S.c. The League worked very helpfully along with the Civic Guild of Help. The lantern services were crowded with children every Sunday night,and they thanked God for the opportunity of influencing for good so many children, a large number of whom did not attend any Sunday school or place of worship. In responding to a vote of thanks (proposed by Mr T Dale) Mrs Crosfleld expressed her pleasure at being present. She was glad to hear of the continued success of the work of the mission. The amount realised at the bazaar was more than she had anticipated and she thought all must have worked very bard to bring about such a result. Now that the new vestry had relieved the pressure in the Sunday school, she thought that the next matter whirls would require attention was the extension of tine chapel, which might be undertaken probably next year. She greatly appreciated the volun- tary work that was carried on by the misson and hoped it would meet with still greater success in the future. (Applause).

Professor Hobhouse's Visit.

Those who are interested in the principles under- lying social and political progress should embrace the opportunity of hearing Professor L T llobhouse at S o'clock. next Monday night at the .Co-operative Hall, Cairo-street Professor Hobhouse will deal with the relation of biology to politics, and his address should prove interesting and informative. Professor Hobhouse has been described as one of the most brilliant of the Oxford School of Philosophy, and his reputation as an exponent of social philosophy is already world wide. Only recently he was called and gave a series of lectures on Sociology to Columbia University at New York. He is the author of several important works on his subject.

THE TRADE BOOM.

NO CAUSE FOR INTERNATIONAL

JEALOUSY.

ADDRESS TO THE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE.

The great boom in trade was the principal subject of the presidential address of Mr F A FROST at the annual meeting of tue Warrington Chamber of Commerce held at the Town Hall on Monday. He said he was proud to have occupied the presidency during a period of record activity, giving the highest figures of the Empire's commerce that have ever been reached, and this in spite of many disadvantages, such as the coal strike, which was general, most deplorable and disastrous in its effects on all concerned ; the transport strike in London, and the partial railway strike in the North. Had it not been tor those unfortunate setbacks the country would have been better off still. On looking around he noticed we were not the only nation that had prospered during the period under review. Some people are apt to look upon this with an unfavourable eye. Personally, he Was of the opposite opiriion, for JAe considered THE MORE OTHER NATIONS PROSPERED THE I:ETTER.

FOR US; they would have greater needs which we could and did supply, and were in a better position to pay for them either in cash or kind. It was better to have an affluent customer than a poor one. Also the desire and ability for nations to trade together was a very strong deterrent to the abominations of detestable wars, which brought no good even to the victors. Wars, NNhether industrial or international, were to be avoided. The former could only be done on the basis of goodwill between employers and employed, possibly aided by the principle of arbitration, and the recognition that their interests were identical. The latter was some- what difierent for however much a nation objected to fighting it did dot altogether rest with that nation whether it was or not. Every nation was liable to attack from wiLhout, and therefore it was necessary to be so strong that any attack would be doomed to failure. For that strength they had to rely entirely on the manhood of the nation, and he hoped that before long every man would be able to be at his post, in an efficient condition to give a proper account of himself, should he be called upon to do so. As soon as this was acknowledged by all nations there would be little or no risk of outside trouble, and they could continue to attend to their own industrial progress without any fear. Given peace within and peace without, they hoped to see still further record figures, more pros- perity, less and less unemployment with its accompany, - jug misery. (Applause). The PRESIDENT moved the adoption of the report which, in the course of an exhaustive review of the events of the year, stated that the Council had from time to time considered the recent strikes and the industrial situation generally, and it had been decided to ask some prominent leader of industry to address q, pullic meeting at an early date on the possibilities of co-partnership as a solution of the question. The Council, after agitating for the improvement of Bank Quay station for several years, were glad at last to be able to report that the platforms had been raised, and that other improvements were in progress. But in their opinion much still remained to be done before the station was equal to present requirements, and was worthy of the importance of the town. The accounts of the Chamber showed a balance in the bank of Ifis _ _

Mr HOLMAN KINGDON (Messrs J Crosfield and Sons, Ltd..) in seconding, said he was much interested by the President's allusion to national defence, which was really a form of insurance. Nowadays they heard a great deal about insurance from various points oe view, and the question of defence was one that should not be forgotten by any commercial man. The report was adopted. THE INSURANCE COMMITTEE.

The PRESIDEN'T, as the Chambr's representative on the Insurance Committee, reported that the committee was very busy at work, with the object of bringing the banefits home for those whom they were supposed to benefit. At present it was all spade work, and the result would be shown later on.

ILL-HEALTH AND COMMERCIAL INEFFICIENCY Mr KINGDoN, reporting on the year's work of the Education Committee, laid particular stress upon the importance of the physical welfare of the children in its effect on the commercial community. Warrington firms were mainly dependent on the product of the Warrington schools for recruiting the main portion of their staffs. An unhealthy clerk, even if intelligent, was in the long run of little use either to his master, his family or himself, and it was, therefore, only right that the Chamber, with no uncertain voice, should express its approval of the policy of the Education Committee that a healthy body must be a condition precedent to effective school education. It may fairly be said that the medical inspection side of educational work in Warrington was making progress. The medical clinic, which was the machinery tor seeing that the child's ailment,revealed by the school:medical inspection,obtpiined proper treatment was getting better known, and consequently tatter liked. The parents of the poorest children, who, except in serious illness, had hitherto had no doctor at all, were beginning to see the advantage of having their children's defects pro, ptly and properly attended to and on the other hand, where as was unfortunately the case, the ignorance or apathy of the parent prevented the child from receiving the necessary medical treat- ment power was now given under the Children Act to avoid the sins of the fathers being visited on the children. A special branch of medical treatment was the care of the teeth. Roughly speaking, 75 per cent. of the children attending elementary schools in War- rington had unsound teeth, and that fact alone, if further proof were necessary would be sufficient justi- fication for the dental clinic, which was established a little over twelve months ago, and which was getting into good working order. It was hoped that in time the town would see its way to confirm the appointment of at least one whole-time dentist for dealing with this particular work, and there was probably no direction in which public money could be better spent—on the principle of " a stitch in time," and that prevention was always cheaper than cure. England still had a lot of leeway to make up in social matters like those compared with what had been done by our neighbours across the North Son, where in 1912 no less than forty-five of the big towns had well-established dental clinics, which in many instances had been working for some years. Might the fact that Warrington had been the first of the large towns in this country to adopt practical means of raising the standard of national health amongst the children of the elementary schools be not only an incentive to further efforts in the same direc- tion, but also an example to others to follow in her footsteps.

WARRINGTON AND THE TRADE BOOM. The MAYOR moved the re-election of Mr Frost as president. He said that his abilities eminently fitted him for the office, and a more suitable gent lenian could not be found. He was rather disappointed—perhaps it was an oversight in the President's speech—that he did not make it clear whether the becoming prosperity applied to Warrington. He did not know whether the wave of good trade was actually reaching Warrington. They were told it had, but he had not been able to verify it from his own experience, and many other people were in doubt on the point. He was glad the President sounded the note of satisfaction over inter- national prosperity. They ought not to be jealous of the prosperity of other nations, because when other nations; prospered so did we pan i ?Assn Mr R T FAIRCLOUGH (Messrs 3 Fairclough and Sons Ltd.) seconded. Mr Frost had done exceedingly well for the Chamber, and he wished the members would respond in appreciation of his work. The Chamber would then be more useful than it had hitherto bum.

The motion was warmly agreed to. Mr FROST, hi ac!inowledgment, said he did not think it necessary for him to say that the trade boom applied to Warrington. Without Warrington the Empire could not exist—(laughter)—and the Mayor ought to know better than anybody else that Warrington being the hub of the Empire, why of course trade was good in the town. (Laughter and applause.) The NTAYort (smilingly) : There are e,:ceptions to every rule,and I thought Warrington might possibly be excepted. Mr FROST: No, sir • if Warrington were down, the st would be down. _

Captain Crosfield was elected to the vice-presidency. Mr FROST remarking that he had shown hy great ofrsonal sacrifice and his love of Empire. RS well as his devotion for his native town, that he was eniinently fitted for the position. Mr W Skinner was re-elected hoe treasurer, and Mr C D Parkinson was again appointed hon auditor. The retiring members of the Council were re-elected.

THE PARALLEL WITH " ELIA."

To take up another point raised in last week's para- graph, it has been truly remarked that the student cannot read far into cue of his essays without coming to the conclusion that he has much in common with Charles Lamb; above all, what has been well described as " a sense of humorous gravity." A critic, reviewing his "Miscellanies," neatly summed up his individuality by saying : "Mt Birrell's charm lit sin his splendid sanity, his geniality, his sense of humour, his love of common humanity, and his fine scorn of cant" In ' Truth hunting " he writes of his beloved Charles Lamb: "He lived his life cracking his little jokes and reading his great folios, neither wrangling with nor accepting the opinions of the friends he loved ti-) see around him. To a contemporary stranger it might well have appeared as if his I,fe were a frivolous and useless one as com- pared with those of these philosophers and thinkers. They discussed their great schemes and affected to probe deep mystei ies, and were constantly asking: What is Truth ?' Ile sipped his glass, shuffled his cards, and was content with the humbler inquiry: What are trumps ? ' " BIRRELLISMS.

As we were reminded in last week's reference, the author of "Obita Dicta" .whom we shall presently have the pleasure of bearing in Warrington, has added at least one new word to the dictionary. " Historians ! " he ejaculates in a most serious study on an historical subject. "Their name is Perfidy! Unless they have good styles they are so bard to read, and if they have good styles they are so apt to lie! " That comments a fellow-scribe,is a piquant `:Birrellism."No wit could be less artificial than such wit; though in his younger days Mr Birrell has confessed to having been con- sumed with a desire to be witty. This was in order that he might amuse his audiences, not bore them ; and the desire sprang from pity. "I no longer pity my audiences," he declares. "I punish them !" 83 Warrington now knows what to expect. ROUGH ON ACTORS.

It is notorious that Mr Augustine Birrell is peculiar in his utter .lack of sympathy with actors. It has been pointed but that of actors indeed, he has written quite scathingly—for a man of his genial nature. He speaks of "the notorious bad taste of actors" and of " the inherent pettiness of the actor's art," and notes, in his whimsical way, as an objection to the actor's call- ing, that no record of his art survives to tell his tale : " When old gentlemen wax garrulous over actors dead and gone,young geptlemengro w somnolent Chippendale the cabinet-maker is more potent than Garrick the actor. The vivacity of the actor no longer charms (save in Boswell) ; the chairs of the former still render rest imp )ssible in a hundred homes." Warrington has of late years been visited by many distinguished authors, lecturers and politicians, and most of us will count it a privilege to be able to add Mr. Birrell to the list.

SPIDERS' NESTS AS FLYTRAPS.

In Mexico, in certain localities, the people suspend in their apartments nests of sociable spiders taken from oak trees, to which the flies come and get taught, thus ridding the houses of disagreeable guests. These nests, which the natives call mosqueros, vary in size. They sometimes cover a space of six feet. The exterior of the nest is composed of an envelope of two kinds of thread. One somewhat stronger than the other and extending from one branch to another, constitutes the cordages which maintain the edifice. The other threads, softer and glutinous, serve to capture the prey. The interior of this pocket is filled with a network of threads inextricably entangled. Among them numerous cells and galleries are skilfully disturbed, which give the mass a spongy appearance. Each year as the rainy season approaches, the Indians go into the wooded mountain region to gather more mosqueros and bring them back to their homes. They suspend them to the ceilings of their rooms where from that time the spiders continue to live as if they were at home. They place these nests not only in the rooms which they occupy but in their barns and stables as well. A curious thing is that the nests seem to exercise an irresistible attraction to winged insects, which is owing perhaps to their colour or some peculiar odour that our sense of smell is not sufficiently subtle to perceive. The mosquera grows larger concentrically, so to speak, after each capture; when an insect comes and gets caught he is immedi- ately seized and covered in a cell by the spider who makes him his prey and is finally devoured, leaving an empty place which becomes a new cell.

SOME UGLY LITTLE flips.

If you don't believe in fairies, and the elves are not And you your have nfrojefnd aitsh, ii brownies or in gnomes, Let me give you just a glimpse Of the ugly little Imps That invade to-day so many happy homes. Impoliteness is an Imp whom every child should try to shun, And older people, too, without a doubt, Impatience is another Who will cause you lots of bother 'Lest you send him quickly to the right-about. IMPertinence and IMPudence are naughty little twins And, oh, it is astonishing to see he mischief that they do! And, my dear, if I were you, Their comrade I would never, never be. One little IMP will sit astride a pencil or a pen When'er there is a problem hard in view, And draw his mouth 'way down, And whine out with a frown : IMPossible ! IMPossible to do !"

IMPudence and 111 Penitence and IMPulae are three more

(Though the latter is not always under ban) ; And there are more, no doubt, Who are hovering about To get us into mischief if they can. Of little foxes you have heard, who spoil the lovely vines. These ugly IMPS are dangerous, toa, you see. Let us raise a battle shout ! We may put them all to rout Oh, what glorious victory that would be ! THIS WEEK'S STORY. OVERHEARD IN THE FISH SHOP. " 'Ere, mister, this lobster's only got one claw."

"I'm sorry, sir, but you know lobsters fight one another, and I expect this one lost in the fray." "Well, take it away, and bring me the winner."

The, Examiner, Saturday, February 1, 1913.

iANTIQUARIAN CHAPTERS.'

If we quote the Welsh song we shall see that it has the same features as the others. By your leave we will sing Concerning our king Our king is well drest In silk of the best In his coach he does ride With a great deal of pride And with four footmen To wait upon him We have travelled many miles Over hedges and stiles To find you this king Which we now to you bring. Another wren-song which hails from Carmarthenshire is a kind of dialogue song and insists that the hunt shall be carried out in the ancient way. It is too interesting to be omitted:

0, where are you going says Milder to Malder 0, I cannot tell, says Festal to Fose, We're going to the woods, says John the Red Nose 0 what will you do there says Milder to Malder We'll shout the cutty wren, says John the Red Nose 0, how will you shoot her With cannons and guns? 0, that will not do With arrows and bows 0, how will you cut her up With knives and forks ? 0, that will not do With hatchets and cleavers. 0, how will you boil her In kettles and pots ? 0, that will not do In cauldrons and pans

0, who'll have the spare ribs ? 0, Icannot tell,

We'll give them to the poor, says John the Red Nose. AN ENGLISH SURVIVAL. Let us now see what we have in England and Scotland that belongs to wren-hunting. The English nursery rhyme is very old but there is no printed version dating earlier than about 1740.

We will go to the wood says Robbin to Bobbin, We will go to the wood says everyone We will shoot at a wren says Robbin to Bobbin, We will shoot at a wren says everyone, She's down, she's down says Robbin to Bobbin How shall we get her home says Bobbin to Robbin We will hire a cart says Robbm to Bobbin Then hoist, hoist, says Bobbin to Bobbin She's up, she's up, says everyone So they brought her away After each plucked a feather And when they got home Shared the booty together.

The Scotch rhyme is so much like it that it need not be quoted. The wren is "slayed," carried home "in a cart" and goes into the house by " pulling down the door cheeks." It ends.

I'll hae a wing, quo' Fozie Mozie, I'll hae anither, quo' Johnnie liednosie I'll hae a leg, quo' Foslin 'ene, And I'll hae another quo' brither and kin.

Putting these ditties together and taking careful account of the wording and allusions we shall find much to engage our interest. In the first place they prove that the old ceremony of hunting the wren included the cutting up of the body and the eating of the portions. So tiny a bird as the wren would never be hunted for its food value and we cannot come to any other con- clusion than that the bird was cut up in a sacrificial manner every part being valued and even the feathers possessing curious powers. Other allusions show that the wren was hunted by organized bands of hunters. They kill him with bow and arrow or sticks and stones; then he is put upon a cart and carried home ; he is boiled in the brewery pan ; the king and queen are said to have the best parts, poor people have the giblets and the feathers are divided.

The Carmarthenshire version is specially interesting as containing a protest that the programme should be carried out in the old way and that a cart should be used and bow and arrows as of old.

All these arrangements are an absurdity as applied to a tiny bird of only an ounce or two's weight, but they are very interesting as handing down to us the old traditions of wren-hunting and as reflecting in a crude form habits and beliefs that were once realities.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
"WHO KILLED COCK ROBIN ?"

A CHAPTER UPON A STRANGE SURVIVAL.

Visitors to Egypt, who examine the stone carvings at Abydos come upon a familiar scene as they enter one of the side chapels of the temple of King Seti. Chiselled upon the wallis a scene that might easily serve as an illustration of the dirge of Cock Robin. A dead bird lies upon a bier; around it are sorrowing birds which look like hawks, whilst one !which is on the wing appears to be carrying down a seal amulet. These carv- ings at Abydos are about 3,300 years old. So early in the world's history did people tell stories about martyred birds over whom "the birds of the air fell to sighing and to sobbing." Can this, then, be our old lamented friend Cock Robin, and did they thus early sing his dirge?

We hardly think so; but if we can get a clue to the bird at Abydos over whom his friends are weeping we may have a clue to the much-bemoaned Cock Robin of a later day.

Our inquiry will have to proceed upon general lines and we may not get very con- vincing results; but ancient bird-lore is very interesting and won't disappoint us.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
BIRD AND ANIMAL SACRIFICES.

There can be no doubt that many nursery pieces refer to bird sacrifices and cont-iin echoes of the ritual with which they were sacrificed.

In very early times certain animals and birds were held sacred, just as the cow is held sacred in India to-day. These were held in perfect veneration and it was considered an unlucky and even dangerous thing to do them harm.

Yet periodically one of the sacred creatures was killed as a sacrifice with elaborate ritual and a solemn service. The subject has been discussed at great length by our antiquarians, particularly by Professor J. G. Frazer, and it has many attractive features. According to the learned Professor, our early ancestors believed the spirit of the sacrificed bird or animal to become incarnate in other creatures of its kind. They seem to have regarded the animal as a representative ot a certain clan and in killing it together they believed that the sacrifice constituted a bond between a number of kinsmen. _ _

The ancient usage must be mentioned here because it gives us our starting point in a discussion of the curious attitude our ancestors took towards certain birds. We are specially concerned just here with the wren. This little bird was singled out as sacred not only in England but throughout Western Europe. Possibly the little golden crest worn by one kind of wren gave it dis- tinction. That it was held in respect can be discovered from its names. In Latin it was rex avium, (king of birds), in Greek Basilikos, in French the wren is Roitelet. In Spain, Italy and Germany too, the wren has a name which means king or little king.

In— later ages some curiosity was felt that so small a bird as the wren should be called the king and so the story was invented which told that the birds challenged one another as to which could fly nearest the sun, that the wren concealed himself among the eagle's feathers and managed when the eagle was exhausted to fly a few inches higher. But the true explanation is that the wren was held sacred by early man and was called king in a very remote past.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
MODERN WREN SACRIFICE.

Let us now take note of the fact that the custom of sacrificing the wren is still observed. In early days, long before the dawn of history, it was sacrificed for the reasons we have briefly giver. In our own day it is sacrificed in circumstances of peculiar cruelty in Ireland, France and the Isle of Man. Happily, the custom appears to have completely vanished from England. In the Isle of Man it takes place on December 27th. This date marked, according to the old reckoning, the beginning of the New Year. The people used to observe a watch-night in the Church and then pass out to hunt the wren. When a bird was secured it was fastened to a long pole with its wings drawn out wide, and was carried through the lanes to the song,

"We hunted the wren for Robbin the Bobbin."

The chant described that the bird was hunted with sticks and stones, that a cart was hired, that he was boiled in a brewery pan, eaten by the king and queen, and the pluck given to the poor. Such was the song. The usage was to carry the bird round the parish and then lay it upon a bier in the churchyard. Next came a funeral of great solemnity with dirges in the Manx language called "The knell of the wren." The concluding scene was a dance in which the people joined hands and danced in a ring to music.

Fifty years or so ago it ceased to be a ceremony for up-grown people, but fell into the hands of the boys. These killed a wren and carried it from door to door suspended by its legs in the centre of two hoops. The hoops were decorated with ribbons and the boys sang the old dirge. A feather plucked from the wren was left at each house in exchange for a piece of money. The old superstition of the sacredness of the wren still lingers in Manxland in the belief that the feathers of the sacrificed bird possess a charm and will preserve the owner from shipwreck for a whole year. The wren is no longer buried in the graveyard but upon the sea shore or waste land.

The Manx people have lost all knowledge of the meaning of the wren hunt, but have invented a legend to explain it. II is to the effect that a fairy of great beauty acted the part of Syren to the men of the island,leading them by her sweet voice to follow her until she had drawn them into the sea. At last some soldier arose to destroy her but she escaped by taking the form of a wren. They go out on New Year's morning in the hope of settling accounts with the bad fairy.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
WREN SACRIFICE IN IRELAND.

In Ireland wren hunting was common until the last century. It still survives in Leinster and Connaught. Here also the bird is carried round hung in hoops to the song. The wren, the wren the king of all birds, Was caught St. Stephen's day in the furze Altho' he's little his family's great, Then pray, gentlefolk, give him a treat.

This verse is useful to us in our enquiry as retaining the old belief that the wren was a royal bird.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WELSH RITUAL.

In Wales the custom, we think, is now abandoned, but up to quite recently in Pembrokeshire several wrens were carried round in a cage on January 6th (the old Twelfth day) from house to house.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
BIRD SACRIFICE IN FRANCE.

Further light COMI3S to us as we cross the Channel and study the rhyme of France. Here, too, wren-hunting was practised through centuries, but we have not been able to ascertain whether it is still followed. They used to catch a bird on Christmas Eve and take it to the priest who set it free in the Church. The people vied with each other to catch the first wren, and if the fortunate one was a woman this gave the sex the right to jeer at the men and smear their faces with mud.

In other places the lucky man or woman was hailed king and carried round shoulder high. On Twelfth Day he attended Church in mock state and called on the Mayor and Bishop.

They have also in France a song that describes the plucking and dividing of the bird. It goes over the ceremony showing that they began at the left eye and travelled over the body by an orderly plan until the last feather was taken from the tail. Yet all the time the bird is said to keep on singing, a detail that reflects the old belief that the bird was not really dead, but that his spirit passed into the rest. A Breton poem gives another piece of infor- mation. It tells how the wren was kept in a cage and fattened until the day when the butcher came and sacrificed it to an accom- paniment of ritual and then revelry. In this realm of bird sacrifices we also meet our friend Cock Robin. But he figures in so many aspects that we must make him the subject of a special chapter. A. M.
