" BUkNS' NICHT."

THE REV. W. REID'S STRIKING TRIBUTE TO THE POET.

"It's guid to be merry and wise, It's guid to be honest and true, It's guid to support Caledonia's cause, And bide by the buff and the blue." _ _

These lines from Burns' "Here's a health to them that's awa' " could be very appropriately applied to the gathering of local Caledonians and " freens " at the Lion Hotel on Saturday evening to celebrate, in common with canny Scots the world o'er, the anniversary of " Bobbie " Burns' birthday. The festivities, which took the form of a dinner and concert, were conducted in a congenial atmosphere, real Scottish wit and humour prevailing throughout the whole proceedings. A great deal was said about " the land o' cakes" and about its sons being more successful than Englishmen in various walks of life. Those successes, according to Councillor J T Locker, who proposed the toast of "The Warrington Association," were due to the splendid education which Scotsmen received. The numerous other toasts were ably submitted, that of" The Immortal memory of Burns" being entrusted to the Rev W Reid who in the course of an eloquent tribute said that Scotland produced Burns, Scotland claims Burns but ScAland cannot hold him ; the must give him to the world. As the guests assembled, Piper Stoddard, of Seacombe played various Scottish airs on the bag-pipes. The dinner, which was an excellent one, was well served. Ile traditional customs were observed in the serving of the haggis, characterised by Burns as the "great chieftain o' the puddin' race." This was brought in with all the ceremony due to it, Piper Stoddard heading a pro cession of waitresses and waiters, each carrying aloft a dish of the pudding. The president (Mr J Fulton Smith), presided and amongst, those present were : —The Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), Rev W Reid 2 Councillor Locker, the Chief Constable (Mr Martin Nicholls), Chief Inspector Williamson, Messrs J C Copplck, G Bowman, W Skinner and J Tennant (hon. secretary). Dinner over, the Chairman read the following rgreeting from the Manchester and Salford Caledonian Agacciation :

hearts alowe, we're burnin' now, To join the mighty chorus ; To dower Rah's lays is boon a' praise— His spell is hangin' o'er us. Free out the past, borne on the blast, A sang we'll never tine sir, Says you an' me shall brithers be, In mem'ry o' lang syne sir. The loyal toaAts having been submitted by the President and duly honoured, the Rev \V Item pro- posea the principal toast of the evening, " The immortal memory of Burns." In the course of an eloquent tribute he slid there were many thoughts in his mind in regard to Burns, hut the one that arose readiest to the surface was the environ- ment of the poet and the influence it had on his life Carlyle had said that Burns was fortunate in his father, characterising him As one of those strong,indeJendent sturdy type of men, of thoughtful and intense char- acter and a man of real practical integrity ' • a man who valued knowledge and kept an open mind for all that came his way, and who wa3 friendly and tearless." One could not forget Betty Davidson' with her wealth of tale', ballads and songs. Burns had his books about him, not many, just a few choice ones—Shake- speare, Pope, Milton, Ferguson, Ramsay and the theological works of his days, such as might have hen feeund on the shelves of the average cotter, and the Bible ; that, must not be forgotten These boeks were few in number, but B erns's after-writing- showed that they hal been well conned, and the influence of the home of Burns went with him through life. That was seen in his response in later ye r's to the cotter's "Let us worship God," in which he desyribed the " cheerfu' supper," " the patriarchal sire," and "the big ha' Bible." They had the matter summed up in a nutshell in the following lines From scenes like these old Scotia's grandeur springs, That makes her lov'd at home, revered abroad. There was no doubt that Burns carried with him his father's God, and that he never lost his faith in him was seen in the words : An atheist's laugh's a poor exchange For Deity offended. Of course he bad other books—human nature, open fields, &c.—and these he read not letts intelligently than the volumes he had about him, and as he went further into life one was struck with the fact that he realized what every man could if he chose, namely, that he had a mission in life."o that I for puir aid' Scotland's sake some useful plan or brick could make or sing a song at least." One could read the progressive stages of the poet's life in the words he dashed off with such con- summate skill from time to time, and there was no doubt that he was given to his country in a day when Scot- land's harp was neglected and somewhat broken. It was his privilege to take the harp of the country and re-string it, and as his sympathetic fingers played over its strings, and with his soul ablaze, with consummate skill he sang the hopes, the fears, the loves, the joys, the aspirations and the ideals of his people. Ile drew inspiration from his native mountains, valleys, fields, trees and rivers. He sang to his people in their own tongue. In regard to the complaint of many people that they could not understand the language which Burns used, the rev gentleman said that it was suggested by some that the English language should be enriched by some of these Scotticisms and choice inspirations. It was certainly dull work to those who had to get a glossary and keep it by their side and have to turn over the pages through the alphabet in order to find an explanation of words met with in reading Burns. The works would be emasculated if they attempted to do away with the original language. It was a language, not a dialect, and if -they attempted to rob it of that they would take away its vitality, its life and fervour. Burns's genius and his tenderness came into view as he took the little things, the every-day and commonplace things, and transformed them—the sheep, daisy and the mouse, even turning aside his ploughshear to avoid cutting the thistle down. Away at the back of it all, peering through it all, one could see evidence of a great breadth of mind that ran through, actuated, and dominated the soul and being and all the exercises of the man's life. Burns was not an ecclesiastical reformer, minding creeds only when he saw something more than their; uselessness; namely their danger. Scotland was being scorched by ultra-Calvinism. It might be urged that his work was destructive only and, pbrhaps, not without reason, had he written neither more nor less than "The Holy Fair," "The Kirk's Alarm," "The Twa Herds," e&c., each of which made instructive reading though not drawing-room literature, but a decidedly constructive policy was shown, as he said : " God knows I'm no' the thing I would be, Nor am I even the thing I could be, But twenty Hines I rather would be An atheist clean, Than under gospel colours hid, Be jest for a screen. The heart ay's the part, ay, That mak's us right or wrong." All this because Burns saw not in the Christian faith what he saw around him—cant, hypocrisy, tyranny, wrangling and bitterness—but kindliness goodness, justice, righteousness and love. Dr Craik ha"cl said "The generous verse of Burns springs out of the iron-bound Calvinism of the land like liovving water from Horeb's rock." Yet the truth that ultra-Calvinism was mis- interpreted, and whist which misinterpretation Burns directed his energies of sarcasm and wrath, wd very plainly seen in Burns himself. Burns was noble even in death, and the very ruins of his life collosal. With what would be called the war spirit to-day Burns had no sympathy. True, he wrote a martial song, which, if he had penned naught else, would suffice to keep his memory for ever fresh. But his was the charter song of freedom. He sang in defence of the common heritage of humanity, and in giving to

cotland " sc,,ts mina. nee'b. gave to tne peJines of 'Le world their rignt of liberty. With him it was liberty or death. (Applause.) Scotland produced Burns. icotland claimed Burns, But Scotland could not keep .irn. She must give him to the xiorld. In the day hen irreligion and atheism was doing its best to flood he world, and when Scotland, in her recoil against base things, was pinning her faith to a human creed that threatened to separate and alienate her from faith in mankind, Burns in his God-inspired way, converted ier into the vestibule of the realm of universal brother- nood.

Then let us pray and come it may, As come it will for a' that ; That man to man the wand o'er, Shall brothers be for a' that. The "Lament" was played by Piper Stoddard and the toast was drunk in silence. Councillor J. T. LOCKER proposed the toast of "The Warrington Association." He said it was an o:ganisa- tion whose main objects were charitableness and sociability. Those were the characteristics of a race of which they had need to he proud. Referring to the excellent president they had in Mr Fulton Smith, he said that gentleman had rendered valuable service to the town in connection with municipal affairs, and he (the speaker) who had been proud to ha associated with him on the Council, felt that the town of Warrington lost a great help-mate when Mr. Smith decided to withdraw from that side of public life. (Hear, hear.) He, how- ever, still took a generous interest in most of the local associations. Continuing, the speaker said that in most instances one found Scotsmen 2.t the head of the com- mercial concerns with which they were connected. That imprssion had been confirmed by his long general experience in calling upon great commercial concerns. They found that Scotsmen had made greater progress than any other nation with such e small and limited population, as compared with other parts of the empire, and they had great reason to be proud of their advc.nce. They had been most prominent in the race of Anglo- Saxons in their emigration. They had colonised and were holding important positions in the United States, all of which in his opinion was due to the splendid educa- tion which was, and had been given to Scotch people. In the colonies they found men of culture, who had risen from ordinary homes in Scotland, and were now rendering service to the Empire and more particularly to the homeland, which was almost priceless. In his study be had come to the conclusion that it was the educational system of Scotland which had made them pre-eminent in those particular positions. He hoped it would be the fortune of our British legislators to give to this England of ours a system which would be equally useful to the younger generation. If we could only get the sentiments and inspiration in our youngsteis which appeared to exist in young Scottish people, then we should have a race which would emulate the best achievements of the past. (Applause.) The Me-vort (Councillor Dr Joseph), in responding said some of them might be puzzled to know under what pretext he could come forward as a champion of so great a country, which had produced so great a man as Robert Burns. He though that, to a certain extent, he was on his defence in that case. his mother was Scotch,born in'Scotland of Scottish parents.(Applause) Relating a story, his Worship said that in the latter part of the 18th century that state of education which Councillor Locker had spoken of had not arrived He also mentioned the fact that the late Professor Alexander Murray was his maternal great great uncle. The professor had worked himself up from a shepherd boy and was appointed at Edinburgh because of his being practically the only one who could transcribe a letter written in Abysinnian to King George 111., which was of great diplomatic importance. Continuing his Worship said he thought that Councillor Locker had spoken so well and, he believed, so truthfolly of Scotland,that he (the Mayar) was entitled to say some- thing to make Scotsmen worthy of the observatioLs he had maue. It was a well-known fact that the countries which were barren or unfertile and whose natural resources were small, produced the quickest-witted men and women. It seemeo that where the natural resources were very peat', it tended to ignorance as regarded the mind and body. That was the reason he thought, why dm Sc sen were so successful in contesting with their fellow men in other parts of the world. There was no doubt about it that another thing in favour of Scotland was that its people were not only fond of acquiring knowledge but had a great admiration for b-arning. l'hat was a powerful reason for their advance- ment. It was said that the Sc itch were fend of mohey (Laughter ) He did not think that characteristic could be applied especially to Scotsmen. (Laughter). It might be that Scotsmen took more care of their money than others, but he thought that was also a good quality, so long as they were not too niggardly and made ordinarily gcod use of their money. Applause). Mr W SKINNER in proposing the toast ot "Our Guests" said there were not a large number of Scots- men in Warrington but the society had greatly extended its membersh:p until they now had nearly NO. (Applause). Mr J C COPPOCK replied. In one respect the Scots were a much-maligned race, for they were the most humorous people in the whole world. (Hear, hear.) Ladies had come to make themselves a felt power in the world. Men regulated their everyday life accord- ing to the ladies. In his reply Mr SMITH said that they as an Associa- tion had set out to try and help those who were not very well off when they came to England, but he was glad to say the calls on the secretary were very small indeed. "Oh ! you mean that rubbish about- our going with uncovered heads to church," she said, lightly; "as if the parson oughtn't to be do Lighted to see us there at all! Besides, that text in the Corinthians applied to Orientals—not us. Those poor women wore a heavy veil, con cealing even their features ! If when one meets a group of you on the sands the men's arms aren't round your waists, one feels that they uught to. .13c!',

Ile girl laughed again. She was beautiful in a warm, slumbrous way, with a skin like ivory, and rich dark hair and eyes; but now she shot a quick glance at the man's face as he looked thou_g._htfully out to sea. _ . .

"Whose 'fault is it," he said—"the women who give too much,_ or the men who ask so little? We only demand modesty, decency • we don't want to share the woman we love with the whole world. There should be charms, there should be graces, jealously hidden from the world, and kept only for home consumption. Iz. the free-and-easy, go-as-you-please manners of women towards men, of men towards women, is to be found the real reason of the failure of chivalry on the one hand, of true womanliness on the other."

"Oh ! " cried Lenore, with lazy scorn: "wo don't faint at a mouse, and die of a mig)--;n1 as our grandmothers did, if you mean that ! " "But did they?" said the man. "There must have been good stuff in those girls, physically and n;entally, to make the splendid mothers they did. With our own parents went the last of the old cchool. Motherhood nowadays is practically a lost art. The man who wrote the line 'To suckle fools and chronicle small beer' was a fool himself, and a vicious fool at that! " "Now you are indecent ! " cried the girl; yet she was not angry. He kri,lw that she would not resent 'anything that he might say. His nostrils contracted disgustfully, as he moved a little further from her. • It had all gone so pleasantly from first to last —this untrammelled companionship of man and girl—winked at by a blind and complaisant world that had itself set a lax standard of morals and manners. Yet the man knew well enough that it was all wrong--that there had been no true courtship the courtship that should be the dim, cool, fragrant alley leading to the sacrament of marriage in the temple (for marriage is a sacrament to all true women), as it would have been to Dell, from whom Lenore had stolen him; and now he was to slip by a lade wicket, as it were, into marriage, as men tisid girls nowadays drifted, without respect, without illusion.

Lenore drew nearer, and rubbed her e,.)ft cheek Against his coat-sleeve. Yes, that was how she :lad detached him from Dell, by giving so much 4.5 keep him away (and Dell had given so little), apd now he had come to see that modesty was the first and chiefest good in a woman, modesty t.nd a love of home and little children, and 'aeauty was of very little account when it made :tself cheap. Dell's winsome, homely face seemed 10 him now the sweetest thing in life, as repre- renting the sweetest things life had to give, in rye, good repute, and honour, at that moment.

The girl thrust out a little naked foot, beauti ful in form and colour, spanned by a. tan-coloured strap, .and laughed. "The doctors are for ever preaching the open air cure," she said, " urging us all about marc. air and light, and less clothing, yet the momer: we women leave off our hats and stockings, you men grumble ! He sat quite rigid—his nerves crisping in re- 'pit under Lenore's unwelcome caress—knowing tat he had come to the turning of the ways, hat to marry this girl meant the wrecking ti his future life, and that if he had not the Jourage to break with her now, he would in- vitably break with her after marriage. And as he gat watching his stern, lean face, his silence, is stillness, 1 frightened her. This was more than mere huffiness. Suddenly he felt danger in the air, and sat up straight. " I know who it is," she said, angrily, "it's Dell." He shook his head. "I have not seen her for Dyer a year," he said. It was for exactly a year that his intimacy with Lengre Lepell had lasted. " Dove-eyed, demure glcved," (she would die a shame to be seen even putting on her gloves in the street!) "and a little mother to a host of squalling brats—apparently she is your ideal of what a woman should be!" "Listen, Lenore," he Said. "We have had a good time, you and I. and now we are going to part. No one will say anything. In the present lax state of society, our affairs are no one's busi- ness but our own, and plenty of men are ready to take my place—for beauty is so very, very rare."

Suddenly it struck him how horrible it was that he should be sitting here, saying these things—incredible things; but the girl herself had put the cart before the horse. And yet he was to blame. . . . a man is always to blame if he /cm a woman make herself common and cheap to him. "It's Dell," she said, under her breath. "Dell, Dell, Dell!"

"Lenore," he said, and almost groaned, "why don't you women make us men respect you— Heaven knows we are eager enough to do it! " Lenore laughed contemptuously. Her sleepy eyes were now widely open, for she knew that she had played her cards badly—carelessly—and lost the man. _

Nor could she punish him. When a woman claims complete independence, and emancipates herself wholly from home authority, she forfeits the proLction that her mankind could and would extend to her did she conform to the canon's of conduct that rule girls who respect themselves. Of course, she knew that these accidents some- times happened to girls of her views and habits— that marriages, drifted towards without visible intention on the part of the man, sometimes did not come off; but it was very rarely that a man had the moral courage to "got out" at the last moment, and she ha.d not for a moment expected such courage in -Wilco Gibson. And the man was rich, desirable in every way, and—here was the sting--he would go back to Dell and marry her, and she, beautiful Lenore, would have to put up with one of the inferior men eager to take truce's place. "I feel a cad and a beast," he said; "yet know that what I am doing is for the happiness of both of us—if love were not forthcoming from me you would seek it elsewhere." "Oh! don't mind me," said Lenore. in a hard, dry voice. "It has taken you a considerable time to find out that we were thoroughly unsuited to each other—but better late than never." He whitened, understanding now why so many men married unhappily—because it. was easier to them to be unhappy than cads; yet he knew that his whole future life hung on the issues of the next few moments—that he must he a brute, and a. resolute one, if he were to cut himself clear of her—and suddenly he sprang. to his feet. "Yes, go," she said, furiously; "go back to Dell," and she turned on him a face so deformed by passion that he hardly knew it. Truly "hell bath no fury like a woman scorned," and he felt like a beaten hound as he left her.

The children were all at school, and Dell was done in the little Highgate house. fragrant with flowers, wnen miss Liepeu was announced. There was only a. very slight acquaintance between the two, and Dell rose, surprised, as the tall, beauti- ful girl swept in, made a pretence of touching her hand, and plunged at once intc the subject that had brought her there. , "Miss Brunton," she said, "for a year Bruce --Gibson and I have gone about everywhere to- gether. ,He behaved to me its the girl he was going to marry, and now he has left me." Dell had been working; now she folded her hands on the work, and said, gently: "He asked you to marry him?" "Men do not ask girls nowadays," said Lenore hotly. "They just drift into an engagement; but the one who backs out is—dishonourable." For a moment Dell was silent, a lovely colour coming into her cheeks, that transformed her al- most to beauty. "It don't know." she said, quietly. "You see, that's all just playing round, and marriage is a working partnership, is it not? A man should be so very, very sure before he asks, and a girl should hesitate so very, very long before she consents." • "Oh call it sordid prudence, not love," cried Lenore—" the sort of 'Love me little, love me long' business." "Hasty love is quickly gone," concluded Dell. "Miss raepell"—she lifted her brown eyes— " Bruce loved me, and, when he asked me, I was prepared to confess that I loved him,- but you took him away from me—you made him appa- rently one of that fast brigade to which you be- long. But he is too strong. too fastidious a man to permanently adopt its manners and mode of life. Have you come here to ask me to persuade him to return to it?" "I have come to. you," said Lenore, "to put it to you, as 8. potnt of honour between one

Then she turned resolutely to other matters. inquired for his people, spoke of her father and the children, gave him tea, and made him feel that for a whole year he had shut himself out from the only real home that since his mother's death he had ever known.

"FARES PLEASE !" WARRINGTON TRAM CONDUCTOR CHARGED WITH EMBEZZLEMENT.

Wesley Horton,a tram conductor in the employ of the Warrington Corporation and residing at 182, Padgate- lane was, at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, committed to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions on a charge of embezzling the sum of 2d.. the monies of the Warrington Corporation. A large amount of interest was taken in the case and there was a crowded court. The magistrates were Coun- cillor H Roberts (in the chair), Aldermen Smethurst and Bennett, Messrs. T Locker, J Hepherd, W Riobard- sou and Col. Edelston—Mr A T Hallaway (deputy town clerk) conducted the prosecution and Mr T S Steel (instructed by the Tramway employe's association) defended the prisoner, and intimated that if, after hear- ing the evidence, the magistrates were of the opinion that a prima facie case hoci been made, his client elected to go tor trial. Mr Halloway said the sum appeared trivial in itself but the charge was a serious one. The prisoner was a person who was in a position of trust, and the offence was one which was most difficult to detect unless certain steps were taken as in the present case. The evidence would show -that on Friday evening, January 17th, two clerks in the Borough Treasurer's department met in the evening and proceeded to Thewlis-street, where they boarded a tramway car, which left Ryden ds-street at 8.20. They at on the top of the car and purchased two tickets. On 384 and On 385. They marked those tickets by a pin prick through the "d " beside the figure one. They left the car on arriving at the terminus, leaving the tickets behind them on the seat. On the return journey from Smik.ey Bridges to Warring- ton at 8.30 the car was boarded by a checker near the Ship Inn. The checker did not know the numbers of the tickets that bad been takon by the two clerks, but te did net know that they proposed to mark them in the way he had indicated to the Court. Consequently when he was examining the tickets he would be able to tell by the feel of them whether the identification mark was on or not. He examined the tickets and found that they all corresponded with the waybill, The way- bill showed that tickets On 384 to Cu 306 had been issued on the journey from Sankey Bridges to Rylands- street, though the prosecution alleged they were issued on the journey before. The checker found the ticket On 385 in the possession of a passenger on the car. On 384 was not to be found on the car, but there was evidence to show that a pasuenger had left the car before the checker went on to it,. It was also interest- ing to note that that ticket, On 385, which they alleged was sold on the journey to Sankey Bridges, was punched for that journey, which appeared to his mind to be corroborative evidence of their statement to a great extent. The next ticket Cu 386 which was sold was punched in the right,secton. The duties of the bench that morning had been considerabl / simplified by the action taken by Horton. William Thomas Eccles and Percy Clarke, two clerks in the Borough Treasurer's office, gave evidence in support of Mr Hallaways statement. Mary Ellis, who travelled on the,8.30 car from Sankey Bridges to Rylands-street, staid she paid her fare to the prisoner. The checker came on the car, and examined the ticket, and she gave her ticket to him. A gentle- man had pre,viously left the car. Cross-examined.—She did not remember the prisoner punching her ticket. She was asked to make a state- ment to the checker immediately on leaving the car. When George Washington Pendleton, a checker, was called Mr Idallaway humorously remarked that there was hardly any necessity to have him sworn. (Laughter). Witness stated on oath that he boarded the 8.30 car from Sankey Bridges to Warrington near the Ship Inn. He inspected the tickets and the waybill finding that all the tickets corresponded erectly with the waybill with the exception that ticket Ou 384 was missing. He found Ou 385 with a pin prick in the "d" in the possession of the last witness, but the ticket was punched on the outward section instead of the inward section. Cross-examined by Mr Steel . He bad known Horton for about eleven years and had always found him to be truthful. It was customary to make up the waybill within 200 yards of the terminus. By Mr Steel: It was not a common practice with the conductors when they had made up their waybill and passengersibad got on that they credited the tickets on the return journey. Detective-Sergeant Holland said when charged Horton replied, " It is not true." Mr Steel said he failed to see any evidence in regard to the sale of the ticket On 384. If those two tickets had been sold on the outward journey and booked on the waybill for the inward journey it would simply be an iriegularity. With regard to the ticket On 385 there was the evidence of Miss Ellis which, he sub- mitted, was not evidence on which any court would act and find a prima facie case had been made ont. Miss Ellis did not remember how many people were in the car, and how many tickets were punched before she received her ticket and she had no recollection as to whether or not her ticket was punched. The two young men got on the car after Horton had made up his way- bill, mad in order to avoid altering it he booked them as on the inward journey, and the ticket which was produced and given to the checker by Miss Ellis was not the one his client issued to her. It appeared to him to be unfortunate that no question was put to Horton by the checker with regard to the ticket. Lying about in the car were innumerable tickets and Miss Ellis was a delicate and nervous woman and there was a possibility that she might have picked up a ticket thinking it was the one which was issued to her. The Chairman : You are going to ask us to assume a very extraordinary state of things. Mr Steel, continuing, said be was sure the Bench would appreciate that though he might have acted irregularly that would not justify him being charged with embezzlement. The Chairman said the tickets in the first instance were issued on the top of the car, while the ticket VVISR afterwards found inside the car at the bottom. Could he suggest any means by which the ticket could have been conveyed to the bottom of the oar? Mr Steel : It is not for me to suggest. The Chairman: I hope you are not going to ask us

SUCCESSFUL MUSIC PUPILS.

The annual distribution of honours certificates and prizes in connection with the Trinity College of Music, London, took place in the Concert Room of St George's Hall, Liverpool, last Saturday afternoon. Mr Behrend presided and the Lady MayoreEs presented the prizes. Amongst the recipients was Miss Marie Hawley, 77, Knutsford-road, Warrington, who was awarded the silver medal of the College, on obtaining highest marks at the three examinations in the last year in pianoforte playing, junior division. The medal, which is of beautiful workmanship, bears the inscriptions: "Trinity 3ollege, London, Liverpool Centre, Gloria in Excelsis Deo " and" Marie Hawley, Pianoforte, Junior Honours, 94 marks, 1912." Miss Hawley's teacher is Mr William Cooke, FRC 0, organist of Lymm Parish Church, this being the fifth time Mr Cooke's pupils have received medals at these functions, a pupil of his last year receiving the Lord Mayor's Gold Medal for highest marks in the senior division.

77, Knutsford-road, Warrington,

Plabs Parkinson,.

Miss Gladys R Hale, daughter of Mr Alfred Hale, Leigh Villas, Grappenhall, has had conferred upon her after examination, the Professional Diploma of Licentiate of Trinity College of Music, London (L.T.C.L.). In the theoretical part of the examina- tion (which took place in Lond( n last month) Miss Hale satisfied the examiners in a severe paper on the Art of Teaching, in addition to general rudiments and harmony. Miss Hale obtained exceptionally high marks—DO out of 100 the practical work in piano- forte playing and was highly complimented on her performance by the examiners. She reilly is to be congratulated on her success in this very difficult examination, as is her teacher Mr. William Cooke F.R.C.O.

A RIDDLE FOR WOMEN.

Why do thousands of women suffer when there is nothing to be gained by doing so? Every woman knows that over and over again she will experience really great pain in the back and hips, and a throbbing headache which makes her feel a hopeless victim to the penalties of her sex. All these sufferings may be avoided. They weaken and unnerve the victim if neglected, but women can be spared them if they will use, according to a woman's own directions, a woman's medicine for women. Any chemist will supply Mrs. Lydia E Pinkham's Vegetable Compound, which acts in harmony with Nature, restoring and preserving health. It is wonderfully soothing. It brings speedy relief. Read what it did for a Manchester lady "I have for two years been troubled with pains at the bottom of my back and hips. I had tried several remedies, arid none did me any good. Lydia E Pinkham's Vegetable Compound has made me feel quite a different young woman. It is a most sure and reliable cure." Lydia E Pinkham's Vegetable Com- pound only costs 2/9 for a bottle, or 4/6 a very large bottle. LANCASHIRE P.S.A.'S.

Delegates from many parts of Lancashire and the adjacent counties attended the annual meeting at Ashton-under-Lyne on Saturday afternoon, of the Lancashire and Neighbouring Counties Federation of P.S.A. WOMEN'S GHAT.

Votes for Women.—Advocates of women's suffrage are naturally disappointed at the turn of events, which have once more interposed to prevent the House of Commons from dealing in a free and unfettered way with the question of votes for women. They were promised that they should have this opportunity when the Franchise Bill came up, and they have some right o complain of the unreality of the situation arising t:rom the doubt as to whether this course conformed to .he rules of Parliamentary procedure. Clearly this 'angled situation should have been forseen but Suffragists will have no cause for regret, if as a result of Luis confusion facilities are afforded for a Woman's Suffrage Bill standing on its own merits, which would command the support of the members of all parties, in tavour of votes for women. The question divides all parties, all possible Governments, and also women as a whole, but the country would welcome as a guide, and index to popular opinion a plain, straightforward pro- nouncement on the issue from Parliament. It is a question upon which Members of Parliament are prone to act on their own personal judgment, but they should pay some regard to the trend of opinioa in their con- stituencies of which presumably they have some know- ledge. In this connection it may be mentioned that last year out of forty-nine candidates at by-elections in Great Britain, only eight declare" themselves anti- , Suffragists. _ _ _

Women and the Legal Profession.—The part taken by women in professional, industrial and political life, is everywhere expanding, and in the Colonies, France and other countries there are women barristers practis- ing on equal terms with men, and with equal success. In this country the Bar refuses to admit women to the honour of dignity and membership, and the Law Society still withholds facilities to enable them to enter the profession of solicitors. A test action against the society has teen commenced by four University women, to determine whether on the ground of sex alone they could be debarred from earning their living as solicitors. Ihere is no question that this opposition on the part of use Bir and the Law :,ociety, is directly traceable to the fear of increased competition. Women have been debarred from becoming lawyers, divines, and until recent years from becoming doctors, but there is 00 doubt that they will sooner or later be admitted to the legal profession. This reform evoked at first some &tie jealousy in France, but, according to a lady who , speaks from oxperience, the men arid women advocates at the Palms tie Justice, are always on the hest of r.re,s fraternising freely, and sometimes sharing chambers.

Lao,.k,uas Weather Lore.—The 2nd of February, Cal.dletuas Day, is an imp.ir tant festival in the Churches, in both Protestant and Roman Catholic countries, and the state of the weather on that day is proverbially said to determine that of the Spring. Dozens ot ancient lands attest its importance, and insist that the vorse the weather on Candlemas Day the better it will oe afterwards. If the sun happens to shine upon this occasion it is an ominous sign indeed, for it means that nly part of the winter is over and that there is as much or worse to corne.,_

===== 0002601_19130201_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
The Mormot and Seal.—The barbarous cruelty permitted in pelogic sealing on the coasts of Newfound- land and Canada, would deter any woman from wearing the beautiful pelt derived from the seal, if only the ruthless methods employed were more widely known. One reason why the marmot has become so scarce, is the cruelty of the trappers who in ono season destro fed many hundreds by drowning them in their holes. Years ago, one set of handsome furs sufficed for even a "woman of quality," to-day, the fashionable woman has a costly set to match each costume Then again, fashions in peltry Lhange nowadays, as frequently as do those of dresses and millinery, all of wnica tends to increase the demand, and to raise the price.. Indeed furs have quite lost their former characteristics, and are used in ways that would astonish early Victorians. They can now he draped as easily as velvet and are thus employed on the huge wraps that are se much worn this winter. In fact they not only trim each other, but take the place of ordinary materials as trimmings and in combination. The Winters's Nluffs and Ilats.—Muffs this season may be divided into two cusses, the fiat, of which there are several varieties, and the round, which resemble a drum, more than anything else. Some of the newest muffs are composed (II the strangest combinations, such as rJueh dog skin and shaggy bear. Panther, badger, and monkey skies are among the novelties tee furrier has lately introduced. The hat of fur, with satin and velvet, or of fur oak, is evee more popular this winter than last, and the small hat which has become almost an obssssion, adapts itself admirably to the use of peltry of all kinds. The picture La, has however, by no means disappeared, but its place is the smart Junction, race meetings, and weddings. For every other occasion the smart hat which grows smaller every dty, is universally worn and milliners are devoting much time and trouble to new shapes and designs for the spring. The Coreelet Skirts.—Four or five years ago the corselet skirt was in high favour, then it disappeared for a season or two, but at present it is to the fore again, and has much to recommend it. It needs how- ever some skill in cutting, and is rarely successful in the hands of the amateur dressmaker. It is irreproach- able in its neatness, requires no belt, and any blouse can be worn with it. But although admirable for morn- ing and outdoor wear, it is not so suitable for evening, The newest models fit, closely to the hips, are tight at the back and slightly loose in front. Fashionable Laces.—The lightest laces are the chosen of the moment. Shadow varieties have a new finish, somewhat resembling Shetland veiling. Black lace is used in every department of dress and millinery, and the new jewelled kinds are gorgeous in the extreme. Ficelle is seen again, and a novelty in Chantilly has a coloured pattern stamped on it, but the effect is more bizarre than attractive. Small lengths of real or good imitations of the best laces should never be despised ; they can be used in a multitude of different ways. Mounted on black ribbon velvet, slightly wider than the lace, a charming neckband results, that would bean expensive trifle to buy ready made. Then dark strips of fur, with pieces of some light lace on eithsr side, make a desirable muff. Coatees for wearing over even- ing blouses, can also be made from odd lengths of lace bought for a mere " song " at a. sale. Front Fastening.—Dame Fashion is not always as kind to us as she might be. For example she long ago deprived us of the really useful dresspocket, and then wont on to make it as difficult as she could, for us to get in to, and out of, out shirts, bodices and blouses. To the woman who possesses a maid, it matters little how fastenings are placed, but when no servant, or friend, is at band to "do one up," the struggle in to, and out of bodices, is not a procedure calculated to improve either the condition of the garment, or the temper of the wearer. In this particular direction however, a happier condition of things is at hand. Leading modistea are causing their newest creations to fasten crosswise, sideways, down the front, or zig-zag fashion, all these devices being easily manipulated by the wearer.

LANCASHIRE INSURANCE COMMITTEE. -- DOCTORS TO DISPENSE WHERE NO CHEMIST.

At Monday's meeting of the Lancashirelnsurance Committee, the Medical Benefits Sub-committee recom- mended that no chemist should supply appliances other than those in the tariff except at the cost of the insured person. It was stated that some doctors were pre- scribing extraordinarily expensive appliances, which chemists did not ordinarily supply, and which were not on the list. Hence the necessity of the instruction to chemists not to carry out such prescriptions except at the cost of the insured persons. The Sub-Committee also recommended that when in urban districts there was no qualified chemist within one mile from the insured person's house, the doctor attending should be allowed to dispense and receive payment in accordance with the chemist's scale. A scheme for the constitution and appointment of district insurance committees was approved.

"TRADE AND COMMERCE IN THE MIDDLE AGES."

LECTURE BY MR. J. DALBY.

Continuing his lectures on " Early English Trade Unions " at the Socialist Hall, Wilderspool Causeway, on Monday evening, Mr J Dalby took for his subject "Trade and Commerce in the Middle Ages." He pointed out that in the Middle Ages, the towns were little islands of craftsmanship in a great sea of agricultural life ; therefore trade and commerce, in anything like the modern sense, did not exist in England. The medireval economic conditions represented a condition of trade which demanded little or no capital beyond the tools of the craft, generally worked to supply a demand which was local and fixed, undisturbed more- over by any such bewildering changes of demand as modern " fashion " bad brought about. In the Middle Ages they had no sort of conception of unrestricted competition. Full as they were of communal ideas and communal feelings, they regarded such a scramble as barbaric. Neither had they, generally speaking, a clear idea of the principle, accepted by many Socialists, of an equal reward for all and still less of the higher principle: from each accord- ing to his ability, to each according to his need. They had a very strong belief in a just price which existed for every article and ought to be given to the work- man who made thit article. Whether for good or bad, they had not enthroned what was called " political economy " as an absolute monarch whose word to its subject "economic man " should be law. Theology was then the queen of sciences, and it behoved men to pay the fair reward to the labourer on the principle of doing to others as they would that they should di unta them. Of course, behind their notion of a fair reward of labour was public opinion as to the stand ird of comfort recognized as appropriate to that class of labourer. They saw there how unprogressive, how stationary the Middle Ages were. People then did not raise the question as to the existing standard of comfort for teat particular class, why there should be classes, on what principle one set of craftsmen were recognized as having a right to a higher standard of comfort than anotner. Accepting the status quo, the existing condition of affairs, as right in the main ' they wished to preserve it, on the one hand by diving it the sanction of religion and morality and on the other ' by removing obvious abuses. It was not a critical age. But it showed up fairly well against our Own, which was content that wages should be determined by the standard of comfort which a class could manage to maintain, left to itself, or rather exposed to the com- petition of machinery and immigrant foreign labour. Mr Dalby then sketched the conditions of trade as determined by the guilds, pointing out that stringent regulations were made against any kind of deceit. Short weight, short measure, dishonest (15 eing of wool, the placing of stones in hay and wool sacks, were all offences punishable by flne. The measures of bread and ale, the two most important food commodities in the Middle Ages, were also fixed. It was not attempted to fix the weight of the loaf absolutely, but the weight was to vary according to the price of wheat, in a fixed scale. Similarly a scale was fixed,according to which the number of gallons sold for a penny (times were better then) variea with the price of barley. The average price seemed to have worked out at two gallons of ale for a penny. In terindon a butcher accused of selling bad meat was brought before the Mayor and aldermen and, if be were condemned, was put into the pillory and had the meat burnt before his face. Forestalling, or the buying up of kupplies with the object of selling them retail at a higher price, was strictly forbidden. Sometimes the goods had to stand in the market place for a certain period until each citizen had had an opportunity of buying what he wanted before any great amount could be purchased by a merchant. Mr Dalby touched upon the restrictions imposed against foreigners (included in which term were merchants from outside cities), and also dealt in an interesting way with the origin of markets and fairs. Reference was also made to the Church'S prohibition of usury or money lending and to the devices which were resorted to for the purpose of circumventing it. All through the Middle Ages (concluded Mr Dalby) the conception of trade morality was much higher than ours. We asked ourselves why it was that, this being so, the system of trade then estate lished broke down and gave us our hideous capitalistic system. The answer was, ha thought, that their attitude to the economic organization of society was largely 9 negative one. The early Christian teachers looked upon the world as, at best, a bad place, and many of them had very definite views as to its speedy dissolution. A man like Aquinas believed in theology as a divine system which was to bring all other sciences under its sway. They did not under- stand that the organization of society and the method of that organization were as important, to say the least of it, as the systematization of the truths of theology. Not understanding the sacredness of the human oody, they could nit understand the sacredness of social economic organization—which was the body of the social life. They had no enthusiasm in the Middle Ages for the right economic organization of society in and for itself. They did not understand that we must set up heaven on earth. And we to-day were faced with a slmilar problem. In the Middle Ages they did believe in ideals ; that belief was very limited to-day, and the number of people who wanted to see those ideals manifested in a corporate way, in 9 new re-organisation of society, were a small multitude indeed. Ours was the task of shaping society, or getting society to shape itself as the corporate expres- sion of highest known ideals. We had to make a neW heaven in a new earth.

The settlement of Central and Northern Saskatchewan is proceeding at a rapid rate. The homestead Puttied filed at the Land Titles Office at Prince Albert aggre- gated 2,263 for the past year, as campared with 2,099 for the previous year. These entries represent, of course, only a tithe of the actual settlers who have made their way into that portion of the province, and who have purchased land in districts already open and withio easy reach of railway communication.

THE LYNEVI BILL.

BIG MAJORITYF FOR 66uRaiL;s PROPOSALS:

The opponents of the Lymm Waterworks Bill should have taken Saturday's weather as a good omen, as water was, for once, conspicuous by its absence, the beautiful atmospheric con eitions tempting ratepayers to come out to register their votes. As ore toured the district during the afternoon there was very little evidence that any momentos decision hung in the balance, there being a total absence of vehicles bearing the usual election advice "Vote for Mr . . .?". The non-existence of rival candidates with their election agents and supporters also tend to make the poll rather quiet with the result that the officers in the various polling stations were able to carry out their duties in quite an unusually easy manner. rile Presiding officers were: —Booth's Hill Ward, Nit- T Newton ; Heatley Ward, Mr Denham ; Lymru Ward, Nir H Oddie ; and Statham Ward, Mr W Mallard. The polling was concluded at 8 p.m. and within half an hour the ballot boxes had arrived at the Council Chambers and counting was in operation. Mr W G Groves, as Returning Officer, presided over the proceedings and was assisted by Mr W Other Councillors present were :—Rev G B Thurston, M.A., Messrs R H Gotobed, J Holt, F Lockyer, 0 G Smith, T Warburton and G L Welford. Amongst other gentlemen in attendance were Mr T Ridgway (Solicitor to the Council), and leaders of the opposition of the Bill. As the ballot papers were sorted and counted any student of physiogomy would have had a happy half hour, for some of the facial expressions that one saw were iudeed wonderful to behold. After the papers had been counted and checked Mr W G. Groves announced the result as Total p01l For the resolution 566 Against the resolution Majority for • 362 He then declared the resolution put before the rate- payers' meeting of January Sth as passed. He said that they had had a greiat fight and though bard words had been spoken at valious meetings he hoped all would be friends again. The Rev G B Thurston, MA, proposed a vote of thanks to the Returning Officer and expressed his desire that a friendly spirit would exist between the members of both parties. Mr 0 G Smith, in secondieg the proposition, caused some amusement by his witty rerurk "First the Church and then the State." He said that he hoped they would all accep.t the result in perfectly good temper. The proposition was carried, after which Mr W G Groves briefly replied. The doors were then opened and Mr Groves, as Returning Oi:icer, announced the result from the front of the Council Chambers to the large crowd that was eagerly waiting. The figures were received with hearty cheers and it was some time before the enthusiasm had sufficiently quietened down to allow Mr G L Welford to address the gathering. Especially prominent were the shouts, They wanted a poll and they've got it ! " Mr We:ford said that as the senior memoer of the Council he wished to say a few words. They had had aligreat fight, not that, they regretted it, but rather they welcomed it. He expressed his thanks to the electors of LyEara for the way they hsd supported them and said the members of the Council have said all along that it was to the best interests of Lymm that they should possess the waterworks. "We beiieve that this scheme is for the good of the district and that our opponents will at a future date feel gled that they lost the poll to-day." He said that the result of the poll would cause the electors to have more faith in the Council in the future. Most of the opposition were personal friends and he hoped that no ill-feeling woulo remain in their breasts. He appealed especially to certain people of the Heatley Ward that they would cleanse their minds of the pre- judice they had against the Council. Mr R H Gotobed who came next, said that as one who bad taken a prominent part in the fight he thanked those who bad voted in favour of the resolu- tion. "We desired your confidence and thank you for having given it us to-day." a hough some of them had had heated arguments he hoped that all would be friends again, and work together to further the interests of the people of the village. Mr W H Finch followed, and said that as chairman of the opposition meeting, he accepted the verdict as given by the majority of the ratepayers. He said that they too thought they were working in the interests of the ratepayers. As generous as the Council have been to them, so would we have been victorious. "Speak- ing on behalf of myself and the gentlemen who supported me, we shall throw in our united support with the verdict that has been given."

Mr 0 G Smith, said that ho considered that the electors had expressed their sanity and good citizen- ship. "The Council will feel strengthened by know- ing that they have the confidence of the electors of Lymna." He hoped that the result of the vote would prove to he fruitful in blessing and advantage to the whole of the district. He thankeu the electors for their support. air T Ridgway said that he was pleased that Mr Finch had spoken in such a stiaightforward manner. He was glad that the poll had taken place, and was thoroughly satisfied that the proposals of the Council were for the good of the people of Lymm. Mr. .1 H Holt spoke hopefully of the future, and predicted a reduction of the water rate and cost of water for baths. " The ratepayers have done something to-day which they will never regret." Mr C E Cowper said that he liked a fight, and that they had had one. " I was only against the price that is to be paid, not against the purchase of the water- works." Be thought it best that the people should have their say on the matter. We have had it and we are satisfied."_ _ _ -

.IVIr Hiram Driukwater proposed a hearty vote of thanks to "The ten gentlemen (the Councillors) who have worked so hard on our behalf."

In seconding thehoposition Mr D Daniels said that the Council membeill did without doubt spend a lot of time on their behalf and thought that they all ought to appreciate it. The proposition was carried_ with cheers. After several calls for Mr Ratcliffe, that gentleman came forwatd and said that he hoped the contest would stimulate a greater interest in the work of the Council. Those people who had opposed the purchase of the waterworks and Lske believed that the ratepayers should have a chance of expressing their feelings. This they had done in an unmistakable manner by endorsing the action of the Council, and time alone would show whether their decision had been a wise one. He thanked both those who had supported the Council and the opposition, and said that he hoped no one would take offence at their action. THE WORK OF THF. S.P.C.E.-00 Sunday services in aid of the Society for the Propagation of Christian knowledge and the National Society were held in the Parish Church. At the morning service the special preacher the Rev R A :Cochrane, M.A., organizing secretary for the S.P.C.K., spoke about his Society being the mother of elementary education in England. He said that this educational work was now being carried on in this country by the National Society, which was one of the offsprings of the S.P.C.K. The Revs G B Thurston, ALA., and H Gray, M.A„ also took part in the service.—At the evening service Mr Cochrane dealt with the emigration work of the S.P.C.K. Offertories were made on behalf of the above two Societies. On Monday night the Rector, the Rev G B Thurston, M.A.' presided over a well- attended lecture held in the Parochial Schools. The Rev R A Cochrane said the society hoped to celebrate its 216th anniversary in March by obtaining 216 new members on or before that occasion. Dealing with the work of the society, the Lecturer spoke of its religious and secular educational work ; colleges in Loudon, Toronto, Lennoxville, Tinevelly and Madagascar.; and its efforts to introduce industrial education in the foreign fields. Medical Missionary work was also touched upon, as was the society's publishing and print- ing department. The Lecturer also spoke of the build- ing grants the Society gave, and said that they helped to build 36 churches every week. After speaking about emigration work, Mr Cochrane concluded an interesting lecture, with an appeal for the prayers and assistance of the audience.—The Rector touched upon his having been an emigration chaplain, and spoke of his trips to Canada. He announced that Mr Bailey had been com- pelled to give up the local secretaryship of the S.P.C.K. owing to advanced age, and that Mr Beckett had kindly consented to hold the office.—The lecture was enhanced by the exhibition of a number of lantern lisdes manipulated by Mr Peet.

PADGATE.

PRINIROSE LEAGUE.—A successful concert, whist drive and dance was held in connection with the above on ThurFday evening in thc Church of England school- room. Mr G Mather presided, and a speech was delivered by Lady Gerard in which, of course, the iniquities of the present Government were fully exposed. Songs were rendered by Miss Roderick and Messrs F Turner and E Bate. In each case an encore was demanded and granted. The Misses Gorton were well received in a pianoforte duet. Mr F Blackwell acted as accompanist. A hearty vote of thanks was carried to chairman, speaker and artistes,the spokesmen being Messrs P H Bennett, F Blackwell and E Lunt. The winners of prizes in the whist drive were:—Ladies, 1, Miss Povey ; 2, Mrs Cook. Gents, 1, Mr E Stringer; 2, Mr James Warburton. The M.C.'s were Messrs Pomfrey and MT Houghton. Dancing was indulged in until about 2 a.m, to the playing of Mr W Wainwright. Messrs J Critclaley and T Antrobus, jun., being the M.C.'s. WESLEY GUILD.—A debate has become quite a regular feature in the annual syllabus and the subject for this year—" Is the present system of party govern- ment desirable ? " was discussed on Monday evening. The Rev E Morgan presided and the debate was opened for the affirmative by Mr H B Mason, while the chief spokesman for the negative side was Mr Burgess. Messrs Birchall, Lunt and Duckworth spoke in favour of the present system. Upon a division an equal number voted for the ayes and the noes and, as the Chairman did not give his casting vote,the result was a draw. A vote of thanks was accorded to Messrs Mason and Burgess on the proposition of Mr Rosoollor, seconded by Mr Proffitt,

The Examiner, Saturday, Februaryl, 1 913, i STOCKTON HEATH.

OLD PEOPLb'S TREAT. The old people's treat was held at the Stcckton Heath Institute last N 1 onday under very happy circum- stances. 'The treat had been organised by a committee foamed from members of the four local churches— St Thomas', the Wesleyan Methodist, Independent Methodist end Hill Cliffe Baptist. Contributions were asked for at each house in the district and a-substantial sum was collect,d, which enabled the committee to provide an admiraole tea and to make gills. All who bad reached the age of 60 were invited. About 140 assembled for tea and thoroughly enjoyed the repast. After tea a lantern entertainment was conducted by Mr John Hallows, pictures of local celebrities and scenes of the old village being approp7iately chosen. At seven o'clock the Vicar of the Parish, Rev. J Collins, took the chair, supported by Mr. and Mrs. Vero Davies, Rev. G Curtis Mr. Arthur 141m:infield and Mr. J Miller. The artistes at the concert were Miss May Isherwood, Madam Lily Fairhurst, Mr and Mrs W H Wounds, Mr E Whitehouse, and Mr E A Sanders, Mr F Banner being accompanist. The entertainment concluded with a humorous sketch " Mixem's Matri- monial Mart" in whicy,h the following took part : Miss S Ealey, Miss Ada Grounds and Messrs.J Roughs- edge, G Moss, P Moss and G Ault.

MR. JADIES LAWTON, aged 5.9, who has lived in the village all his life

At the commencement of the entertainment, a little ceremonial took place which giently pleased the guests. It was discovereu that the oldest guest was Mr James Lawton, who has almost completed his 89th year. He was invited to the platform and duly crowned " king" of the assembly. Mrs Vera Davies carried out the crowning ceremony by placing on his head an elaborate smoking cap, and Mr Vera Davies presented a silver- mounted pipe. Mr Lawton was duly enthroned and made a picturesque figure-head to the platform. He was further honoured by the singing of" God save the king." At the close of the entertainment Mr Curtis thanked the subscribers, Mr A Mounfield eXpressed indebtedness to the artists, and Mr J Hallows thanked the Chairman. As the old people left tobacco was presented to the men and a packet of tea to each woman.A mince pie was also given to each. Many of the old people were conveyed to their homes in cabs and motors and these who were unable to leave home (of whom there were a large number) were presented with packages of groceries. Toe treat was moat successful in elery way and gave great pleasure to the workers as well as to the numerous guests.

- It ought to he mentioned that the use of the Institute with crockery, &c., Ras generoukly granted lree of charge.

but we want to be put on equal terms with the foreigner." Every now and again, he concluded, they read that there was a crisis in the Unionist party and that the Government were coming out" Don't believe it "be added. "This Government will not come out till they are forced out. They will stand anything. They quarrel among themselves ; they are log rollers, but they are not coming out. They are fastened down securely by screws' and there is no chance of moving them."

Mr W Peter Rylands went out of his way to call attention to the fact—a hich Liberals have repeatedly emphasised—that the representatives of the Govern- ment are in a hopeless minority in the House of Lords. Therefore, he added, they could not force their opinions down the throats of the Opposition by the remorseless operations of the division bell. On the question of Home Rule he expressed concern that Ireland, left to manage her home affairs, would not enjoy the social ieforms which fell to the lot of Englishmen, and in this connection he instanced the benefits of Old Age Pensions and National Insurance of which Ireland would, under Home Rule, have been deprived. Songs were given by Mr Harry Gibbon and Mr T S Bryden, the accompaniments being played by Miss Esme Callender.

Mr Torn Boothroyd's baud played for dancing, Mr Fred Evans acting as M.O.

GRAPPENH ALL.

INDEPENDENT METHODIST CHURCH. HONOURING A VETERAN.

Thursday last was the time of great rejoicing at the above place of worship. It was the 85th anniversary of the birthday of Mr Joseph Sanderson, and a social evening and concert was given to celebrate tbei event. The whole proceedings had been arranged and g ven by Mr and Mrs R Nelson. Mr Nelson, who is president of the Warrington P.S.A., brought with him the P.S.A. orchestra, which, under the able conductorship of Mr J Hannan, added immensely to the evening's entertainment. Refreshments were pro- vided at the interval, during which the orchestra rendered very pleasing items. councillor Henshall presided and after stating that the proceeds would go to the new chapel fund, wished Mr Sanderson on behalf of the church and members many happy returns ' and hoped that God would spare him to be with them for many years to come. (Applause.) The following items were then rendered, and judging by the ready applause, gave every satisfaction :—Tbe orchestra, " Tanhauser " ; Mr Warburton, a song, "The Admiral's Broom " • Miss Blanche Lbwis, a recitation entitled, "Papa's letter" ; Miss Whitmore a song, " When the heart is young" ; the Misses Blanche Lewis and Maggie Fairhurst, a dial ouge entitled, " Hunting for a servant " ; selection by the orchestra, " Batiste's Andante in G " ; a Lancashire sketch entitled "Courtin neet," by Mr R Nelson; Mrs Warburton, a song entitled " Swallow," (encore) "Who's that tapping at the garden gate" ; Mr and Mrs Hannan were encored for the duet "The keys of heaven." The veteran guest of the even- ing then entertained the audience with a recitation "The land of my birth," and a reading entitled "The old gardener" ; Mr and Mrs Wurhurt on a duet "When the wind cometh in from the sea "; Mr Hannan a song, "Good company," (encore) " Absent "; Miss Whitmore, song " Golden days "; Mr R Nelson a humorous sketch " Tha'rt welcome, bonnie brid " ; Mrs Hannan a song, " Down the vale." Mr H Harding's rendering of the

Lost chord "as a cornet solo- was excellent. The accompanists were Miss Sbakeshaft, Miss Edith Watts end Master E Hannan.Mr Sanderson thanked all present for the very hearty manner in which they had received him ;such a happy evening would live in his memory to the end of his days. Below we give a photograph and a brier sketch of Mr Sanderson's carreer.

Ehoto, MR. JOSEPH SANDERSON.

Although a native of Yorkshire, the subject of our sketch has been a familiar figure in and about Warrington for the past 12 years. To sit and chat with one who has weathered 85 winters is an oppor- tunity whereby one can gather items of interest recalling the methods and customs of past generations first band. Mr. Sanderson began work in the woollen mills of Benjamin Shaw and Co. in Honley 77 years ago. He was then eight years of age. None know the details of a woollen worker's trade much better than our old friend. He started as a "billy piecer, " and from that to the "devil hole" and then he rose from " slubber " to manager. Mr Sanderson tells how he used to stand at the warehouse door and watch the old stage coach pass ithrough the then little village en route for London. It is decidedly interest- ing to hear him talk of his father making a light with the flint-box and steel when calling his lads to work at 5 o'clock in the morning. Those were the times when 12 hours' work per day had to be put in. is recollections of the Coins Valley flood are very vivid This disaster was caused by the bursting of the Holmflrth Reservoir; the water dashed into Colne Valley and did incalculable damage, and 70 people were drowned. Mr. Sanderson assisted in the recovery of the bodies.—Mr Sanderson goes on to tell many interesting stories, but space will not allow of their repetition. The old mania bale and hearty, enjoying the use of nearly all his faculties. He reads well without glasses ; he can do a good day's gardening ; be can entertain at a concert, and his only drawback is deafness, although this is but slight. He is now resident with his daughter and son-in-law, Mr. and Mrs R.Nelson, of Castlemere, Grappenhall. Ile is lock- ing forward, in his own words, to still many years of useful life. Our readers, we feel sure, wi,h this cheer- ful veteran of industry, many years of comfort and well-earned happiness.

MR. KEBTY-FLETCHER, M.P., AT LYME

ABUSE OF THE GOVERNMENT.

The Primrose Leaguers of Lymrn were much elated on Wednesday evening, for their annual social gather- ing in the Drill Hall was made Lotable by the presence of the Member for the Division, Mr Kebty-Fletcher. Otherwise the proceedings followed the usual lines—a judicious blending of tea, politics, and patriotic songs, concluding with that most popular feature of all—a dance. There would be an attendance of about 500, Mrs Cornwall Legh (Ruling Councillor) presiding over the meeting and concert. Among others on the plat- form were : Mrs Callender (bon secretary), the Hon Mrs Tollemache, Mrs 'glands, Mr and Mrs W Peter Rylands, Mr Callender, Mi s Burrowes, Mrs Moles- worth, Miss Boumphrey, Miss Esme Callender, Mrs K M Tweedale, Messrs J R Newton, W E Hiller, F Evans, Newton Worthington, H S Saville, and R Oliver. As to the vocal items, there were the approved ditties about our fighting navy, but quite the tit-bit of the evening, was a sort of vccal war-cry from the pen of Mrs Callender, the burden of the refrain being Keep Kebty-Fletcher at the top of the poll, and you're all right, you know." Of course, the writer could not resist the _opportunity of a dig at the much-maligned Insurance Act, and the insured worker was asked to conjure up a touching vision of the fate of the patient, in the lines:

"If you are very nearly dead and to the doctor go You'll find 300 other chaps all waiting in a row." The Ruling Councillor predicted that, owing to a cold, the gentleman who undertook the singing of this choice number would probably sound "a bit like a crow" but, as a matter of fact he did very well, and sent the faithful into ecstacies of delight. Mr Kebty-Fletcher's most devoted admirers would hardly class him as a serious politician. His chief stock-in-trade appears to consist of a somewhat feeble flow of invective, and practically the whole of his speech at Lynim was given over to extravagant state- ments and steady abuse of the Government, without a semblance of real argument. He trotted out tee familiar bogey about Ulster losalism and Home Rule, which led him to lament that " at present here is no House of Lords." Technically, he added, there was such a place, but it had no more power than a Lymm Debating Society. The House of Lords could not alter a single sentence, line or word in the present Bill, and it must go through. Alter this unequivocal statement, however, Mr Fletcher curiously added the qualification : If it becomes law, the present Government will have to enforce it on the people of Ulster. They would not have it, he declared, except by force of arms and it became a very serious thing in these days when our own people had to be coerced and dragooned into leaving the Union Jack and living under the Irish Nationalist flag. Mr Fletcher next turned attention to the land question and predicted that house rent was bound to rise, because the "people's budget" and form 4 had killed the building trade and practically no cottages were being built to meet the increased ' demand for housing. Quite oblivious of the fact that agricultural land is untaxed, Mr Fletcher coolly accused the Government of having helped to make food dearer by taxing the land which was " the raw material of food." Next he advanced the Protectionist pro- posals by which the foreigner is to relieve us of part of the unpleasant duty of paying taxes, but, if tariffs do not increase the prices of commodities, why should Mr Fletcher have gone out of his way to state that "the last thing you should tax is raw material " ? The Welsh Church Bill, of coui se, came in for castigation, Mr Fletcher indalging in delicate sarcasm at the expens3 of Sir Alfred Mond, whom he described as "that fine spsoimen of a Welshman " and stating that the Welsh Church, with the wboie of its present endowments, was poorer than "the Brunner, Mend's. " Disenoowrnent, he added, was only a long word for theft and be charged the Government with "robbing the roar curate who is not able to to defend hirte,elf." The Diseadowment Bill would recoil on the Government and with double force on the Nationalists, who had aided and abetted the robbery, when the Radicals again threw the schools into the melting pot and the only defenders of those schools would be the Unionist Party. The only utility that Mr Fletcher can discover in the land taxes is that they "provide Radicals with highly paid posts." Can it be that Mr Fletcher studies the speeches of Mr Harold Smith' to provide himself with ammunition ? He made a great point of the methods which Mr Smith has recently been telling his supporters that certain Radical clubs in London resort to, in order to raise the wind. These entertainments were said to include music hall turns and boxing matches on Sunday, and Mr Fletcher added that most of the Radical clubs were kept open by their sale of "dirty whisky." Mr Fletcher nest made statements which could only be 'regarded as accusations of jobbery on the part of members of the Governnment, but he took care to add that "I am not suggesting anything, but there you are !" With regard to tariff "reform" he said "We don't want Protection at all,

PENKETH.

LABOUR CLUB.-At the fortnightly meeting of the Peoketh Labour Party held on Wednesday in the above club room, the members and friends were favoured with a visit by Mi Lowe of Warrington, who gave an interesting address on " What is Socialism." The speaker dealt with it in a fair and broad-minded way and he was listened to with close attention by a very good company. Several questions were asked at the close of the address by the members. Mr Lowe has promised to pay another visit to the club at some future date. Mr Clay of Warrington presided.

DENTH OP MR. RALPH LAWTON.—The death of Mr Ralph Lttwton, of Brookside Farm, Orford, and formerly of Wellfield-street, Warrington, took place on Monday morning after comparatively short ilhss. MOORE.

WESLEY GUILD.—On Wednesday evening last Mr SA Elliot gave a paper entitled "An evening with Keats." Mr Elliot's visits to the Guild at Moore are always keenly looked forward to and consequently there was a very good attendance. %Ir. K. H. Meldrum made an excellent chairman. Contrary to common practice, in dealing with the biography of this youngest of the English Poets who have become famous, Mr Elliot commenced by giving a graphic description of the death of Keats, at the early age of twenty-five in Rome whither he had gone on a fruitless quest for health. Then reverting to the time of his birth he outlined in a very interesting manner the life (alas all too short) of John Ke,its. During the evening many beautiful selections from the poet's works were quoted and Mr Elliot possesses such elocutionary powers that many of the selections brought forth well merited applause. At the conclusion of the meeting Miss Dutton voiced the very sincere thanks of the members to Mr Ell jot for his most interesting paper.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

BLAMED THE CAT.

At Runcorn, on Monday, Philip Paylor, of Norley, was summoned for trespassrng in search of game. Albert Edward West, as3istant gamekeeper to Sir Richard Broolse,said he saw Taylor setting rabbit traps in a field in th,?. occupation of Mr Charles Percival. Defendant said the cat had brought home the rabbit trap found on him. He did not know how to set a trap. It was just a chance that be bad the trap in his pocket. He was fined 10s, and advised to turn the cat out when next it brought a trap home.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr J Spruce presiding.

ASSISTANT NURSE.

Miss C Roughsedge wrote resigning her position as assistant nurse at the Union Infirmary. The resignation was accepted and it was decided that a successor be advertised for.

THE MENTAL DEFICIENCY BILL.

The Clerk to the Wharfedale Union forwarded the following resolution passed by them and asked the Guardians to give it their support. "That His Majesty's Government be urged to re-introduce at the earliest possible moment the Mental Defficiency Bill and use every endeavour to have the measure passed into law."

The Chairman (to the clerk) : What do you recommend? The Clerk: You cannot tell in what form the Bill would be introduced. It was compulsory- Mr Linak er : I think if we had a rest from legisla- tion for a time it would be better.

Mr Linaker, jun., moved that the letter lie on the table.

Mr G Pilling in seconding said: " It would be as well ; it might affect many of us." (Laughter.) NATIONAL CHILDREN'S HOME.

The Secretary of the National Children's Home and Orphanage wrote stating that the cost of maintenance would in future be 78 per week. He stated that the boy Wallace in their custody attended the Elementary Day School. He had progressed very well and was nearing the end of a year in which he has been in Standard V. The medical report stated that the boy was developing well. The boy, when 14 years of age would be able to take up work on the farm and garden. The boy was well fitted to do the work. Mr Linaker suggested that the Board should consider the advisability et removing the boy to the Dutton Homes. (Hear, hear). _ _

Mr WoOdvvara asked on what ground the boy would be taken away ?_

The Clerk : On the ground of expense. You can just as well.look after him at your own homes.

After having spoken at some length in favour of the work which the Children's Home did, Mr Wood said he thought the publio ought to give the Board credit for saving the rates of the Union to such a great extent as they did, by taking children in the homes, other than those who bad passed through the Workhouse. Mr Roylance said he was afraid it would cost them more than is per week.

It was decided that the question of the advisability of transferring the boy to the Dutton Homes be con- sidered.

THANKS FOR TREAT.

The minutes of the Visiting Committee, which were adopted, showed that Miss Mary Hannah Potter was recommended for appointment as laundress at the Workhouse at a salary of £2O per annum. Mr Spruce proposed that the Board tender thanks to the Ladies Visiting Committee for the preparations they made on the occasion of the treat to the inmates. He was only sorry that tbe Board was not more fully represented at that gathering. Alderman Norman seconded, and siid the treat was equal to any they had had befoie. Messrs Davies and Baxter supported. RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Dutton, on Tuesday. Mr C E Linaker presiding.

WHOSE ROAD.

The County Architect wrote raking the Rural Council to permit the construction of a cartway crossing at the Stockton Heath Police Station, across the footpath to the ambulance entrance gates. It was pointed out that the road in question was Grappenhall-road which was controlled by the County Council and it was decided that the Clerk write to the Clerk of tbe County Council stating that the Runcorn Council did not control the road and that it was the County road.

THE WATER SUPPLY.

The SECRETARY of the Local Government Board wrote respecting the quality of the water supply in the Parish of Antrobus. it was stated that it was not good and he was to enquire what action the Council have taken or propose to take to provide satisfactory supplies within the parish ? It was observed that supplies derived from wells in the Parishes of Alvanley, Antrobus, Bartington and Kingswood are stated to be adequate but inconvenient" in this connection, and whether it applied to all supplies in the parishes. The CLERK was instructed to formulate a suitable reply stating the position of the parishes referred to, also that the" inconvenient" applied to the distance.

ThA MEDICAL OFFICER (Dr. Adams) submitted his report which was adopted. It showed one case of scarlet fever and one of diptheria from Stockton Heath.

ST. HELENS.

ST HELENS CYCLE CLUB.

The annual dinner of the St Helens Recreation Cycle Club was held on Saturday night at the Royal Raven Hotel, about sixty members and friends being present. Mr Geoffrey Pilkington presided. The toasts of the N.C.U. and the St Helens Cyclists' Club were proposed by Mr W Taylor. Mr W Inman, chairman of the centre responded. Mr I Little responded for the St Helens Club, and thanked the Recreation Club for their kind- ness in allowing the club the free use of their cycle track for supplementary sports. In response to the toast of Messrs Pilkington Bros." Mr Pilkington said he was sure that working men living in towns like that wou:d find great benefit from the pastime of cycling. for it would improve their health and make them happier in every way. ASSIZES.

John Lloyd, coal deal;:,-1-I,—fork-stre3t, St Helens, was charged on Monday with wilfully setting fire to his stables. Mr Quinlan). who prosecuted for the Hearts of Oak Fire and General Insurance Company, pointed out that, after completing an insurance on his shop and stables and two horses on January 8, accused paid 5s 9cl premium, and received his policy. Within a few days the police found the man's stables, in which there were two horses, on fire. The fire sergeant noticed a strong smell of paraffin and the circumstances were so suspicious that Lloyd was arrested, and was now charged with setting the place on fire by means of paraffin. Mr Donnellan, for the defence, denied the imputations of the prosecution, and said that the magistrates were invited to decide against the prisoner simply on a number of assumptions. The bench com- mitted Lloyd to the Assizes.
