Burning Fatality.

An inquest was held at the Wa;rington Coroner's Court, on Wednesday morning by Mr F A Jones (deputy coroner), touching the death of Elizabeth Leigh, wife of William Leigh, a wire rope maker, of Bostock-street.—The husband stated that his wife was. .54 years of age. On the 10th of January he was at work, when he was informed that she had been burned Be returned home and found she had been badly scorched on the body, chest and arms. She told him that her Deice asked her to get a pencil which was on the mantel shelf over the kitchen fire. She stood on the fender to get it, and in reaching over, her apron caught fire. The flames travelled up her dress and her blouse became ignited. She tried to put out the flames by wrapping herself in an old macintosh and by pouring cold water over herself. She was successful in her endeavour and then rushed out into the yard screaming. A neighbour came to her assistance and she was taken to the Infirmary. She appeared to be recovering, until Saturday, when she became worse and died early on Sunday morning.—The jury returned a verdict of 'Accidental death."—Mr R Roberts watcbed the proceedings in the interests of the relatives.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
El

Sir,—l am afraid that "Phil Squod" has failed to grasp the nature of my last letter. Its purport was to demonstrate what should be the marks or characteristics of the True Church established by Christ. The question of doctrines taught by that or any other Church is quite anoher matter and beyond the scope of the argument. I have .not yet attempted to expound any Catholic doctrine, although quite willing to do so if requested. As to "Phil Squod's" alleged perplexity as to what Church or branch of a Church I refer to., I think there can be no doubt in the minds of your readers generally, as the heading of the letters is sufficiently significant. I don't refer 'to any branch, but to the Roman Catholic Church, which is either the one True Church or nailing. Our friend urges, in his last letter, that this Church is corrupt, and if such is the case it has not a claim to be evert a branch of the true Church. The wickedness of individuals within her pale is one thing however, and the alleged 'corruption of the Church and her doctrines quite another. But to refer to his idea of u Church. He states that "a true Church should expound the Scrip- fures, teaching nothing but what is contained therein." Such a statement clearly implies an authority to teach, and for a man to be teaching he must necessarily be explicitly and definitely ex- pounding truths which there is no gainsaying, for if he. is merely expressing his own opinion he is not teaching. Now, the Catholic Church teaches among other things the Unity and Trinity of God; the Incar- nation, Death .and Resurrection of Our Saviour; Purgatory, Immaculate Conception, etc. Whether these doctrines are true or not is not the question. The point is that she teaches this perfect and definite code of doctrines explicitly,- emphatically, unitedly, and universally. Thus I maintain that even if her doctrines are not true (which I don't admit), she possesses the elements necessary for a true teacher, viz., certainty, confidence, and unity. Moreover, as God's Church must be universal, she also possesses that distinction. This I asserted in my last letter, and as it has not been questioned, it is therefore admitted that such is a fact. Now, if the Roman Church is wrong and bad, there must exist somewhere the true one, and which must necessarily have better credentials than that which is untrue. Then what. I ask "Phil Squod" to tell us is : What teaching body outside the Roman Catholic Church propounds for man's belief a definite code of doctrines? Which is that "Church of which any preacher of such Church can say to his congrega- tion : "You must believe this or that dictrine which I deliver to you, because I am one of the preachers of that authoritative body which Christ established for the expounding of the Gospel? And what I deliver to you is the same as is taught by my fellow preachers throughout the world."

If "Phil Squod" can bear testimony to a Church having such or bettek credentials, he will give us cause for serious reflection.—Yours truly,

JOHN RICHARDSON.

86 Buttermarket-street, January 29th, 1913

IMPORTS OF MANUFACTURES 1903-1912.

Sir,—ln Warrington as elsewhere. we are eon:

vinced that after the ten years' ding-dong of Tariff Reform we are all heartily sick of the sub- ject. I hesitate therefore in writing anything that bears any relation to it. But leaving out of con- sideration the inconsistencies, sectional appeals, economic confusion which have all along charac- terized this movement, it is well that we should carefully watch the tendency of our trade with the outside world which this ill-started movement was designed to alter. And here we pass from the region of theory to that of fact. For the pur- pose of ascertaining the growth of our imports of fully-manufactured goods ready for use I have as below extracted from the Board of Trade re- turns the figures for 1903 and 1912. The period is of special interest, because it covers the time in which the fiscal controversy has been prominently before the country. To ascertain the balance for home consumption, the re-exports are deducted: It must be acknowledged that the term fully- manufactured goods ready for use is difficult to apply. It is impossible to tell in many of the items what proportion is subject to further manu- facture, and hence difficult to determine to what class these properly belong. For example, in the case of. silk manufactures, which form nearly a third of our fully-manufactured imports, a pro- portion of these imports are in the form of broad- stuffs, which although as such are fully manufac- tured must necessarily go to our workshops to be made up into articles of clothing. Again, ma- chinery although not subject to further manufac- ture is almost entirely us-e'd by and benefits our manufacturers. For this reason I have thought it proper to omit it from the manufactured goods ready for home use. The accompanying list (although the selection is necessarily of an arbit- rary character) contains the items in our imports which can be most appropriately designed as above. And inasmuch as comparisons are made of the same goods for both years, the totals show fairly accurately the progress made in supplying our increasing requirements for manufactured articles ready for use. goods The amount of semi-manufactured goods is ar- rived at by deducting the fully-manufactured goods as per list from the total imports of goods classed in the Board of Trade returns as "Goods wholly or mainly manufactured." These semi-manufac- tured goods are mainly composed of 'blooms, bil- lets, and slabs, sheet bars, tinplate bars, and the like; copper, lead, tin, zinc in a semi-manufac- tured state; chemicals including materials for making dyes, drugs, colours, and tanning extract; leather; paper; motors and motor-cycle parts; paraffin wax, oil cake,-starch, etc.; stones and slates.

===== 0002601_19130201_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
EXPORTS.

We now trim to the eporta of our manufactures of a similar character to the goods enumerated above. We find that while the imports of these have slightly diminshed, our exports in the similar classes have increased by £04,000,000. The total exports of manufactures have increased .U. 51,000,000.

It is only right to say that the general increase in values has effected semi-manufactured more than fully-manufactured goods, beCause these are nearer to the raw material. In manufactured goods, while also dependent on raw material costs, the ' proportionate dependence is less, hence to

ascertain the exact increase or decrease it would be necessary .to enter into elaborate calculations beyond our present capacity. But a comparison of the quantities imported in the contrasted years shows that while the correction would be required, it would not in any way invalidate the deduction which can be fairly drawn from the figures. This deduction is as follows During the last ten years, under our system of free imports, our nianufac- tures are tending more and more to supply the natural increase in the demand .for the finished articles for home consumption, and our increase of imports of an-called manufactured 'goods is only in those things Which supply our manufacturers with what to them is raw material. CRARLES E. PARKER.

Penketh, January 30th, 1913.

The Examiner, Saturday, February 1, 1913.

DVANCED EDUCATION.

should like to express iilyself upon a sUbject which some superior person signing him- self "Astonished" seems to' speak with authority, viz., the instruction given at the local Secondary School. _ _ _

I was a scholar at • this institution for four years, and can therefore claim to know a little of the education which is received there. I have been an "Old Boy" for nearly six years, and have had time to test, in some degree, the value of the instruction received. Others there are who were students at the school with myself and they, too, will testify that the instruction given in those days was thorough, and the methods employed by the staff were.quite as modern, and as successful, as those of any other school. The list of suc- cesses gained by the students is one of which the town should be proud, and these distinctions have been gained by the efforts of the staff, coupled with-the -industry of the pupils. Evidently "As- tonished" is so utterly overwholmed by his child's inability to keep up with the rest of the form that-in his paternal blindness he fails to reoognise his child's duty towards the schooT. • Moreover, if he had a genuine desire to see his child succeed, he would have interviewed the Principal, or the "offending" teacher, and, not have ignorantly rushed into print. May I suggest to "Astonished" that he kindly leaves the education of the children to those who have been trained expressly for this purpose? The duty of a right-minded parent is to stimulate his child to vigorous co-operation with his tutor, and not by sneers to make it impossible for the youngster to respect; and so derive benefit from the efforts of the teacher. He May as well take his child away from the school now that the teacher's good name has been held up to ridicule. The education at the Secondary School is "very advanced" for tho small-minded and unsympathe- tic prig; but to any one who will realise his duty to his school, it is not above his capabilitieq, and will stand him in 'good stead in later life.—Yours, etc., "W.:.

MISS GORNALL'S CONCERT.

The charity concert, given in the Parr Hall on Thursday evening by Miss Mary Gornall for the benefit (f the Royal Albert Institution Lancaster,was distinctive in being conceived on a novel plan involving the free admission of the audience and the taking up of a silver collection during the iteerval. There was quite a large gatherit g of baluonably dressed people in the hall, and a very attractive, but all too long, programme of vocal and instrumental music and recitations was given by a number of capable amateur and pro- fessional artistes most of whom have appeared on past occasions at Miss Gornall's matinee concerts. We understand that the Royal Albert Institution has claims upon the public of Warrington inasmuch as some half-dozen at least of the inmates of that admirable harbour Lf refuge are from this neighbc.ur- hood. It is gratifying therefore to be able to announce that what is regarded as a very satisfactory money contribution will be made to the funds of the institu- tion as the outcome of the self-sacrificing efforts of Miss Gorman and her friends. As the circumstances under which the concert was given preclude anything in the way of detailed criticism, it must suffice to say that a very enjoyable evening was spent in listening to an entertainment the only fault to be found with which was its inordinate length. Otherwise the programme was as nicely diversified as song, recitation, and instrumental music both of the solo and concerted kind, could make it. The songs by Miss Hilda Cragg-James and Mr Mann were mostly both intrinsically good and well sung, and, beirg in their nature and character as widely apart as the poles, the effect of each was thereby enhanced. One could not but feel grateful that the first named singer included amongst her contributions the finely emotional but too much neglected "Spirit's song" f Haydn. To such an item and to Teresa del Riego's "Sink, red sun," some rollicking and good- humoured songs by Mr. Mann of which "The little grey home in the West" and "The Ringers of St. Peter's" are specimens, served admirably as a foil, and served also in lifting into more cheerful surroundings much else in the programme that was perhaps of too gloomy a character. Mrs. Ridgway's two recitations "The happy Prince" and" Dora" were given most feelingly and with great restraint, but, though they were listened t., with deep attention, one could not help feeling that the inclusion of both these big things resulted in unduly prolonging an already overloaded programme. Amongst the instrumental music were a Fantasia (Chopin) by Miss Mabel Fairclough, and an adaptation for two pianos of a piano concerto 17 Mendelssehn in which Miss Goruall took the more °nen us and responsible part. There were also a number of more or less interesting violin and 'cello solos by Miss Amethe Leadbetter and Miss Gladys S. Stewart and a trio (the that movement of the Mendelssohn D minor) in which these two ladies were associated with Miss Gornall.

Military Dinner The Warrant Officers and Sergeants (past and present) of the Prince of Wales's Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) held their annual dinner in the Lecture Room at the Drill Hall on Saturday evening. Sergeant-Major W Powcer presided over a gathering of over SO. Lieutenant-Colonel B Fair- clough, commanding the 4th Territorial Battalion, as present. An excellent dinner was served by Mrs Atkinson, from th, Cafe, Bridue-street--Referring to the presence of Lieutenant-Colonel Fairclough, the Chairman said he was an old friend of the Ist Battalion, having served with them during the South African war with ,the Volunteer Service Company.—Colcnel Prendergast, commanding the 2nd Battalion, who said he was pleased to be present, read a letter from Colonel Richardson (who takes a keen interest in the club) wishing them every success. (Applause.) The com- bined bands of the depot, The King's and South Lancashire Regiments, played an excellent selection of music.

PALACE AND HIPPODRO-NIE.

"Queen of Lilliput." - Such is the title which has been bestowed upon Lady Little, the smallest woman in the world, at the sight of whom, the large audiences at the Hippodrome this week have been perplexed and astonished by her exceeding diminutiveness. Although over 20 years of age she is only 23 inches high, and does not turn the scale at eleven pounds. She is accompanied by her director, Mr Harold L. Hilliard, who, although only ordinarily tall, appears to be some giant besides the dainty little doll-lady. In child-like tones she tells the audience interesting facts about her life, and not a few of the audience are given the oppor- tunity to shake the hand of this little artiste. Lady Little has an enormous amcunt of valuable jewellery which, as is explained, she has received from Royal personages the world over. We are also humorously told that in view of the single blessedness which Lady Little enjoys, many offers of mart iage have been received from War- ringtonians. Someone evidently wants a pocket edition entertainer. The premier all-round instru- mentalists, Cavalotti Bros are highly successful in a refined and clever musical turn, playing most skilfully on numerous wind instruments. It is indeed a beautiful ten minutes with the great com- posers. Conway George is a vocal and character impressionist of no mean older, his child mimicry being very well clone.fhe music hall favourites of our fathers and of the present day are presented by Graham, the human marionette. We see the music hall of 1870 and the" stars" of that period as well as the elaborate vaudeille stage of 1912. His representa- tions cf Barry Lauder and George Lashwood evoked much laughter. Hal Bryan is a popular vocal comedian with a bright style and greatly pleases in his popular number" You can't judge a woman by her clothes." Ragolio is a rag artist and lightning sketch artist, for whom these is a fair amount of applause. Frank and Beryl Harmeiltre clever drawing room entertainers in burlesque comedy and harmony, Little Connie Edmonde, a juvenile serio comedienne also appears.

CRINIE IN WARRINGTON.

The Chief Constable of Warrington (Mr. Martin Nicholls) has issued his sixth annual report dealing with the state of crime in the borough and the work of the police force for the year just ended. The number cf crimes reported during the year was 304, being an increase of 98 over the previous year. From these offences 195 persons were pro- ceeded against, an increase of 41 on the figures for 1911. The value of property stolen amounted to £646 10s lid and the amount recovered was £325 19a 11d. There was also an increase of 9 in the number of juvenile offenders charged with theft and apprehended dut log the year. The number of persons proceeded against for non-indictable offences was 1,553, of which number 922 were apprehended and 631 summened. As compared with the figures of the previous year there was a decrease of 15 in the number of persons arrested, and an increase of 57 in the number of persons summoned. There was a decrease of 9 in the number of persons proceeded against for drunken- ness. The police had restored to their parents 355 children who were found wandering in the streets.

INSURANCE ACT AND THE "FOUNTS OF CHARITY."

THE REV. A. J. BRATT. M.A.

Tell.

The impending departure from Warrington of the Rev A J Bratt, senior curate at the Parish Church, will be genarally regretted, not only by Churchpeop.le, but by members of many other denominations with whom Le Las came into contact during his ministations in the town. Mr Bratt has accepted the position of clerical organizing secretary of the Church of England Waifs and Strays Society for the dioceses of New- castle, Durham and Carlisle, and will enter upon his new duties shortly after EA ster. An M.A. of Durham University, Mr Bratt received his theological education at St Aidan's Theological College, Birkenhead. Having held curacies at Huddersfield, Portsmouth (where he worked in the parish of St. Agatha's Landport, which will ever be associated with the name of Father Dolling) and Tamworth, Mr Bratt came to Watrington four-and a- half years ago. Bis gifts as a preacher ate exceptional, for be combines a happy facility of expression, with a directoess, originality and wealth of homely illustra- tion, which appeal to all. Moreover, he makes a point of keeping in touch with the latest developments in the social and scientific worlds and he has always given his hearers the benefit of his studies. Mr Bratt is also an energetic wotker and the remarkable success of the Men's Bib!e Class is due to his splendid leadership and initiative. Useful work has also been done in the parish by Mr Bratt's sister, Mrs Hopkins.

THE RECTOR MD NONCONFORMITY.

ALDERMAN BENNETT TAKEN TO

TASK.

In his breezy and interesting little publication "The Sunrise Annual," Alderman Bennett, in the course of a review of the events of the year 1912, has the following reference to the Welsh Church Bill:

We —are not very much concerned about Disestabliehment, and, as Mr. Arthur hlounfield has shown in his interesting history of "Warring- ton Nonconformity," now appearing in the "War- rinktna Examiner," a good share of the original endowments of the Church belong, by right, to the churches which now represent the brave ejected ministers who, two hundred and fifty years ago, to the tune of some two thousand, gave up home and all they held most dear, and went into the wilderness sooner than betray their consciences. One of them, we recollect with pride, was Robert Yates, the Rector of Warrington. . • • If the Government had proposed to allocate the huge endowments which had been accumulated up to 1662 amongst the various sects that sprang from the great schism, we should recognize a sort of rudimentary justice in the Bill ; but to. take them from the Church which is, at present, making Filch good use f them, and not to hand the spoil to those who might legitimately claim a share, but to the County Councils, seems to us to be both mean and foolish. We, at any rate, as Nonconformists, will not countenance the plan. Speaking at the annual Couversazione in connection with the Warrington Church Society held in the Patten Hall on Thursday evening, the Rector (Rev Canon Willis) referred to the above expression of opinion. He said Alderman Bennett told them that the Non- coi4,rmist ministers who were the descendants of the ministers during the Rebellion had a right to the endowments. Let them think calmly and quietly over what really took place. Those Presbyterian ministers i had only been n office seventeen years—from 1015 to 1662—and then, to their honour, they resigned because they could not agree to the terms. But they were treated with gi eat leniency compared with the way in which thttr predecessors of seventeen years before had been timed out with t'ie greAtest cruelty. If it were true that there were 2,000 Nonconformist ministers who resigned, it was also true that there were three or four times as many rightful occupants who were turned out by the Nonconformists. It was simply a matter of history. Tne Rector also said he was supposed to have a hankering after the Roman • Church. (Laughter.) Nothing could be further from the truth than that. What he longed for was that the Roman Church should give up the Papal supremacy which was comparatively modern. The Eastern Church would never acknowledge the Papal supremacy and, as Anglicans, they wanted to be one with the Eastern Church, as well as with the Church of Rome, on right terms—and by right terms he did not mean for one moment, the recognition of Anglican Orders by ROMP, but he did not mean the giving up of Papal supremacy. The Rector further defined the Holy Catholic Church as meaning the whole Eastern, Roman and Anglican Churches, and all Nonconformists who had been baptised. Baptism was the one and only entrance into the Catholic Church. On the question of religious education, the Rector referred to the discussion in the Education Committee on the proposed transfer of teachers and scholars from Cairo-street to Holy Trinity school. He had every respect for those who cared for sanitary Fonditions, for the health of the body and for good instruction for the minds of little children, but what distressed him was that, apparently, because men differed so ranch in their religious views, they would prefer to banish definite religious instruction altog.ethT% Nothing could be better than the secular education in the Council schools, find, so far as it went, the religions instruction of an undenominational character was good, but why the Education Committee should refuse to have undenotninational religious instruction in the Council Secondary School when they had it in the elementary schools;he could not conceive. While pleading with the audience to do their utmost to secure religious instruction in the Secondary School, the Rector said the enly other course to take was to make their Sunday schools as efficient as possible, not for one moment being content with reading stories and amusing the chiloren, but seeing that they bad definite religious instruction spiritually and lovingly imparted. (Applause.) A most enio3 able min ice] programme had been arranged by AlrWB Randall (organist of Holy Trinity Church). It included songs by Madame Sara Sileoek, Miss L Davey, Mr C H Ford and Mr .1 B Poole, humorous interludes by Mr II M Ashton, a violin solo by Mr F H Randall and a pianoforte solo by Mr W B Randall (who also acted as accompanist).

===== 0002601_19130201_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
"IN THE HAND OF THE POTTER." Rev. J. E. Ratten bury on Mission Work in London.

"The Hand of the Potter " is the title of a striking book in which Harold Begbie shows what remarkable moral reformations have been effected through the agritcy of Christianity. It is well known that Mr Begbie found mcst of the material for ,his book in the great Christian and social work of thPsWest London Mission, of which the Rev J E Rittenbury is the superintendent. On Thursday Mr Rattenbury paid another of his welcome visits to Warrington and lectured in Bold-street Wesleyan Church on the ever- fasoinating theme of the West London Mission and its rescue operations. There was a large congregation, Mr C E Parker presiding. Mr Rattenbury is the inheritor of the traditions and spirit of the mission's great founder, the Rev Hugh Price Hughes. The Mission began as the West London Mission in 18S7 and it had its home for many years in St. James' Hall. On the demolition of this hall the Mission found a temporary headquarters in the Lyceum Theatre, while the new Kingsway Hall was being built. This building lately opened occupies a commanding position in the broad new thoroughfare. It has accom- modation for over 2,000 people and is crowded every Sunday. Surrounding the Central Hall are various rooms for meetings. There are all the accessories associated with the idea of an institutional church and these are already in full use. The dominant note of the Mission has ever been evangelical Christianity, coupled with what might be called the Social Gospel. But Mr Rattenbnry would claim that this was not a tacking on of the Gospel but an integral part; he would probably say that the message of the Mission was " Conversion for the individual, righteousness for the State, and a call to all for service and for sacrifice." The CHAIRMAN, in the course of a brief speech, said this was a great age, and everything that affected human beings collectively seemed to he assuming larger pro- portions Having referred to the tendency to amal,,,,a- [nation in industrial affairs, Mr Parker touched upon the enormous waste of physical, mental and spiritual activity involved by the many divisions in our denom- inational system. He pictured numerous little congregations engaged in singing about the Church marching on to war and " hell's foundations trembling" at the attack, but added that one was almost inclined to think that if hell's foundations did tremble as the result of our split-up denominational methods, thrse foundations must have been put together with uncommonly poor cement. (Laughter ) The large missions connected with the Wesleyan society were in contrast to the stagnant conditions which held good in connection with the ordinary efforts of almost every denomination, proving a great success, and it was to that branch of the Church's work that they looked with most hope for the future. (Applause). In his powerful address Mr Rattenbury emphasised the fact that the conversion of the individual was the dominant note of the mission from the beginning. He combatted the idea that conversion belonged to a differ- ent age, and was only applicable to particular individuals and races.He contended that the experience gained in the Mission established beyond question the applicability of the Gospel to every human being, and this for all time and all races, and gave many striking illustrations to prove this point. In dealing with the world-wide influence of the Mission, Mr Rattenbury spoke of several instances which came to his notice in his recent visit to America. He told how on board ship, after public meetings, in private interviews, in the States and in Canada of the many who had come to him with particulars of individual personal experience of the good they had received at the West London Mission. The rev gentleman also emphasized the sadness and dangers of the loneliness of London, especially to the many young people who come up from all parts of the country as shop assistants and the like. He described how at the Mission they had made a special effort to provide a home for these lonely ones, and told that in his membership there were over 300 young shop assistants, mostly from the country. One of the most impressive parts of the teverend gentleman's address efts his reference to the work of the Sisterhood, first organized as a Methodist Institution by the founder of the Mission and subjected at its formation to a consider- able amount of suspicion and distrust. Mr Rattenbury, in a very earnest and graphic passage, described tho heroic work of Sister Mildred in the rescue of fallen women, a heroism as great and greater than had been manifested by the heroes of early Methodism. Mr Rattenbttry claimed the support of the provinces for the Mission on the ground of the national and cosmo- politan character of its ministry and service. The audience responded to the appeal in a very practical way, the collections at the afternoon service and evening meeting amounting to £37.

TURTLE AND INFLUENZA.

At this season of the year, when influenza is rife, the danger of infection with this debilitating and dangerous disease is ever present. When we are tired or cold—particularly when both tired and cold—the natural disease-resisting forces of the body at e at low ebb, and we are at the mercy of the influenza germ. What the system needs at such moments is nourish- ment that can be assimilated rapidly enough to restore vitality at once: ordinary tonics and stimulants are useless, as they reduce the vital forces further in pro- ducing a fictitious and temporary strength. The finest and rn,}st rapid restorative food is turtle : it abounds in the phosphorous the system needs, in such easily digestible form that it is completely assimilated and its benefit felt within half-an-hour of taking it. Turtle has long been known to doctors for its rapid restorative effects, and they have always prescribed it in cases of nervous debility, exhaustion, and in con- valescence, for those who could afford it. In the past turtle has been extremely dear. To-day the enterprise of Bellis's, the largest firm in the turtle importing trad,% has brought turtle within reach of all. In Bellis's Turtle Cup you have finest turtle in con- centrated form, fully prepared so that all you need to do is to add boiling water to it in order to prepare the most nourishing and delicious food drink imaginable. Bellis's Turtle Cup exercises to the full the invigorat- ing and revitalising effects of turtle. Ask your Doctor, You can obtain Bellis's Turtle Cup from any grocer, in shilling bottles ; or direct from Turtle Cup (1911), Ltd.. Dept. 53, 15 Bury-street, St. Mary Axe London. Be sure you get the genuine Bellis's Turtle Cup.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE. "Mother Goose" Pantomime.

In its gorgeous and beautiful setting, the comic pantomime "Mother Goose," presented by Mr Gar Kiddie's Company at the Royal Court Theatre this week, has attracted large " houses " at both perform- ances: The story of the goose which laid the golden eggs is too well known to relate here, and all that need be said of the pantomime version is that nothing of any material value is " potted "so as in any way to lessen the humorous element which prevails,iu a large measure, throughout. The various characters are well sustained, Mr Arthur Carlton being excruciatingly funny in the title role. Kar and Kooney era delight- fully humorous as Parchment and squire Swank. They indulge in the most eccentric typo of mirth-making, keeping the audience in one continual roar. It is due in a large degree to their energies that the pantomime goes with the swing which has made it so popular in Warrington. The Three Merry Wags introduce a speci- ality for which they are loudly applauded. Miss Dorothy Dare is a dashing comedienne and male impersonator, portraying the character of Archie, the Squire's son, in quite a manly manner. Her songs are breezy and catchy and her bouyancy thoroughly grips the audience who join in the choruses with great gusto. The character of the two policemen Robert and Timothy are in the able hands of Frank Langford and Harry Hughes who aid in keeping the ball of humour rolling, as well as proving themselves capable harmonising vocalists. Miss Brenda White is a captivating Marjory and along with her lover Jack, a character undertaken by Blanche 1114 r-ion, she scores a decided success. Bert Rex as Billy is very popular, his drollery being crowned with wit and irresistible humour. Miss Anna Rutland is a captivating Benevo- lence and Mr Bert DlArcy a cunning and unscrupu- lous Avarice. There is an extensive ballet, the groupings being carried out with much taste for the artistic. The music is enchanting, Mr G Gerrard Sinclair being an able musical director. There will be a matinee at 2.30 to-day and two performance* to-night.

MYSTERY PLAY NOT TO BE ACTED IN CHURCH. Almost at the last moment the Bishop of London has intervened and prohibited the performance of a Mystery Play in the Church of St. Silas-the-Martyr, Kentish Town. The Bishop considers it to be undesirable that plays should be acted in church.

BURGLARS UP A TREE. Awakened at 4 o'clock on Thursday morning by a barking dog, Mr James Gibb, of 290, Finchley-road London, came down to find his dining-room ransacked and a window open. Silver articles were scattered about the floor and others, to the value of £2OO, bad been stolen. He sent for the police and got his son to drive his motor-car quickly round and round the block in which the house was situated. The block was sur- rounded in the fog by police, and eventually two young men named Jones and Harrison were found up a tree in one of the gardens. In their possession, it was stated, was a large part of the stolen articles and an unloaded revolver.
